Tangentads: a formal approach to tangent categories
MARCELLO LANFRANCHI

Abstract
Tangent category theory is a well-established categorical context for differential geometry. In a previous paper, a formal approach was adopted to provide a genuine Grothendieck construction in the context of tangent categories by introducing tangentads. A tangentad is to a tangent category as a formal monad is to a monad of a category. In this paper, we discuss the formal notion of tangentads, construct a 2 2 -comonad structure on the 2 2 -functor of tangentads, and introduce Cartesian, adjunctable, and representable tangentads. We also reinterpret the subtangent structure with negatives of a tangent structure as a right Kan extension. Furthermore, we present numerous examples of tangentads, such as tangent (split) restriction categories, tangent fibrations, tangent monads, display tangent categories, and infinitesimal objects. Finally, we employ the formal approach to prove that every tangent monad admits the construction of algebras, provided the underlying monad does, and show that tangent split restriction categories are 2 2 -equivalent to tangent ℳ \mathscr{M} -categories.



Acknowledgements
.
The first notion of tangentads was introduced in
[
15
]
under the name of
tangent objects
. We thank Ross Street, Alexander Campbell, Steve Lack, Jean-Simon Lemay, Robin Cockett, Dorette Pronk, Geoff Vooys, Rory Lucyshyn-Wright, and Geoffrey Cruttwell for suggesting alternative names and participating in the discussion on naming. We opted for
tangentads
, in agreement with Street’s suggestion of adopting the ending
-ad
. We would also like to thank Rory Lucyshyn-Wright and Michael Ching for useful discussions, which helped clarify this paper. This material is based upon work supported by the AFOSR under award number FA9550-24-1-0008.

1
Introduction

Fibred, indexed, internal, and enriched categories represent different flavours of category theory. The multitude of these flavours is reflected in the theory of tangent categories, introduced by Rosický “
to axiomatize properties of the tangent functor on the category of smooth manifolds
”
[
18
]
and later generalized and extended by Cockett and Cruttwell in
[
2
]
.

Cartesian tangent categories
[
2
, Definition 2.8]
, tangent restriction categories
[
2
, Definition 6.14]
, tangent fibrations
[
3
, Definition 5.2]
, and tangent monads
[
8
, Definition 19]
represent different evolutions of tangent category theory.

Formal category theory offers a common theoretical framework for different constructions of category theory, which captures the similarities of the various theories. A formal approach to tangent category theory will be beneficial in keeping track of the several directions in which the theory is developing.

In a recent paper
[
15
]
, a formal approach to tangent category theory was adopted to characterize tangent fibrations as fibrations equipped with a
tangent structure
. This approach was employed to construct a Grothendieck construction for tangent fibrations (
[
15
, Theorem 5.5]
).

The goal of this paper is to take further steps into this intuition and explore a formal theory of tangentads, previously introduced with the name of tangent objects.

A tangentad is to a tangent category as a formal monad is to a monad of a category
.

Using a formal approach to axiomatize categorical constructions is not a new idea. In particular, our work is inspired by Street’s formal theory of monads
[
19
]
in which he introduced the notion of a monad in a
2
-category and formalized the construction of algebras.

For starters, we recall and revisit the definition of a tangentad in an arbitrary
2
-category; we study the global structures and properties of tangentads; we equip the
2
-functor
\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}
with a
2
-comonad structure; we characterize Cartesian tangentads and compare with tangentads of Cartesian objects, and we introduce adjunctable and representable tangentads.

We also present numerous examples of tangentads, such as tangent fibrations, reverse tangent categories, tangent restriction categories, and display tangent categories. We employ these characterizations to prove that tangent monads admit the construction of algebras provided the underlying monads do. Finally, we adopt the formal approach to proving that tangent split restriction categories are
2
-equivalent to
\mathscr{M}
-tangent categories. In particular, this
2
-equivalence shows that the restriction domain of a morphism
f\colon M\to N
in the context of a tangent restriction category is an open subobject of
M
(Remark
4.45
).

The formal theory of tangentads proves to be useful in the following circumstances:

•

When two
2
-categories are known to be
2
-equivalent and the tangentads of at least one of the two are known, one can lift the
2
-equivalence to the tangentads of the two
2
-categories. In
[
15
]
, the formal approach was used to construct a genuine Grothendieck equivalence between tangent fibrations and tangent indexed categories (Theorem
4.13
). In this paper, this technique is employed to prove the
2
-equivalence between tangent split restriction categories and
\mathscr{M}
-tangent categories (Theorem
4.44
);

•

To lift a construction of a given
2
-category to the corresponding tangentads. We adopt this technique to prove that tangent monads admit the construction of algebras (in the sense of Street
[
19
]
) in the
2
-category of tangentads, provided the underlying monads do;

•

To simplify definitions which involve several structures. This, for example, applies to the notion of a tangent indexed category
4.12
, in which several structures and axioms are required to define this concept.

In upcoming work, we aim to investigate this formal approach further to address the following goals:

•

Extending vector fields, differential objects, differential bundles, and connections, to new tangent-like notions. In particular, when the tangentads of a given
2
-category can be characterized as tangent categories with extra structure, one needs to “carry around” such a structure;

•

Formalizing some key constructions of tangent category theory, such as the Lie bracket of vector fields, the covariant derivative, Riemann and the torsion tensors of connections.

2
A formal theory of tangentads

The objects and morphisms of a tangent category can be regarded as locally linear geometric spaces and locally linear functions, respectively, where the notion of local linearity is not intrinsic but contextual and dictated by the tangent structure. Inspired by algebraic and differential geometry, one of the goals of tangent category theory is to categorify geometric constructions in the context of arbitrary tangent categories. As part of this research program, some categorical concepts, such as fibration and monad, have been translated into tangent-theoretic versions. One is left to wonder if the interpretation of local linear behaviour for these concepts still applies to these notions.

In this section, we first recall the notion of tangent category and associated morphisms and recall an equivalent presentation of tangent categories due to Leung
[
16
]
which comes in handy in our story. We employ such a construction to introduce tangentad, unwrap this definition, and introduce the related morphisms.

2.1
A brief overview on tangent categories

Tangent categories were first introduced by Rosický in
[
18
]
, and later revisited and generalized by Cockett and Cruttwell in
[
2
]
.

For starters, we recall the definition of additive bundle and related morphisms.

Definition 2.1
.

In a category
\mathbb{X}
, an

consists of a pair of objects

and

together with three morphisms

,

, and

, respectively called the

, the

, and the

, where

denotes the

along itself. Moreover,

,

, and

need to satisfy the following conditions:

(a)

For every positive integer

, the
n

In the following, an additive bundle
(q\colon E\to B,z_{q},s_{q})
will be denoted by
\mathbf{q}\colon E\to B
or by
\mathbf{q}^{E}_{B}
. When the symbol that denotes the additive bundle is decorated with a superscript or a subscript, the same decoration is applied to the projection, the zero morphism, and the sum morphism. For instance, the additive bundle
\mathbf{q}^{\prime}\colon E^{\prime}\to B^{\prime}
consists of the projection
q^{\prime}
, the zero morphism
z_{q}^{\prime}
, and the sum morphism
s_{q}^{\prime}
.

.

An

from an additive bundle
\mathbf{q}\colon E\to B

\mathbf{q}^{\prime}\colon E^{\prime}\to B^{\prime}
consists of a pair of morphisms
f\colon B\to B^{\prime}
and

satisfying the following conditions:

An additive bundle morphism is denoted by
(f,g)\colon\mathbf{q}\to\mathbf{q}^{\prime}
, where, in the first position
f
indicates the base morphism and in the second one
g
indicates the top morphism.

The “correct” notion of limits in a tangent category is the notion of
\mathrm{T}
-limits.

Definition 2.3
.

Let
\mathbb{X}
be a category and
\mathrm{T}\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}
be an endofunctor of
\mathbb{X}
. A
\mathrm{T}
-limit diagram
consists of a limit diagram in
\mathbb{X}
whose universal property is preserved by all iterates
\mathrm{T}^{n}
of
\mathrm{T}
. In the following, we refer to pullbacks, equalizers, etc., whose underlying limit diagram is a
\mathrm{T}
-limit, as
\mathrm{T}
-pullbacks
,
\mathrm{T}
-equalizers
, etc. When the endofunctor
\mathrm{T}
is the tangent bundle functor of a tangent structure,
\mathrm{T}
-limits are also called
tangent limits
and the convention extends to
\mathrm{T}
-pullbacks,
\mathrm{T}
-equalizers, etc.

Definition 2.4
.

In a category
\mathbb{X}
equipped with an endofunctor
\mathrm{T}
, a
\mathrm{T}
-additive bundle
consists of an additive bundle
\mathbf{q}\colon E\to B
such that the
n
-fold pullback
E_{n}
of the projection
q
along itself is an
n
-fold
\mathrm{T}
-pullback diagram.

Definition 2.5
.

A
tangent structure
on a category
\mathbb{X}
consists of the following data:

]

\displaystyle(\mathsf{id}_{M},c_{M})\colon\mathrm{T}\mathbf{p}_{M}\to\mathbf{p}_{\mathrm{T}M}

(a)

A
tangent category
consists of a category
\mathbb{X}
equipped with a tangent structure
\mathbb{T}\>\colon\!=(\mathrm{T},p,z,s,l,c)
. A tangent structure
has negatives
when it is equipped with:

​
[
Negation
]
:

A natural transformation
n_{M}\colon\mathrm{T}M\to\mathrm{T}M
, natural in
M
, satisfying the following condition:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}_{2}M}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}M}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{M}}

In the following, we denote the tangent bundle functor of a tangent structure with the same symbol adopted to denote a tangent structure, e.g.,
\mathbb{T}
, in the font
\mathrm{T}
. The projection, the zero morphism, the sum morphism, the vertical lift, and the canonical flip are respectively denoted by the letters
p
,
z
,
s
,
l
, and
c
, and when the tangent structure has negatives, the negation is denoted by
n
. When the symbol denoting the tangent structure is decorated with a subscript or with a superscript, the tangent bundle functor and each of the structural natural transformations are decorated in the same way, e.g., for a tangent structure
\mathbb{T}^{\prime}_{\circ}
, the tangent bundle functor is denoted by
\mathrm{T}^{\prime}_{\circ}
, the projection by
p^{\prime}_{\circ}
and so on.

Definition 2.6
.

Given a tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, a functor
G\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}^{\prime}

preserves the fundamental tangent limits
when it preserves the universality of the
n
-fold tangent pullback diagram of the projection along itself and the local linearity of the tangent bundle. Concretely, these two conditions amount to say that the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G\mathrm{T}_{n}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G\mathrm{T}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G\mathrm{T}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GM}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\pi_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\dots}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

is an
n
-fold tangent pullback diagram in
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
and that:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G\mathrm{T}_{2}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}_{2}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G\mathrm{T}^{2}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G\mathrm{T}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(z_{\mathrm{T}}\times_{z}l)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(\pi_{1}p)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\mathrm{T}s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\mathrm{T}p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Gz}

is a pullback diagram in
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
.

A tangent
1
-morphism from a tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
to a tangent category
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
consists of a functor
F\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
together with a distributive lax
\alpha
between the functor
F
and the tangent bundle functors, which is compatible with the tangent structures. Tangent
1
-morphisms come in different flavours. In the next definition, we clarify this distinction.

Definition 2.7
.

Let
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
and
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
be two tangent categories. A
lax tangent morphism
from
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
to
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
consists of a functor
F\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
together with a natural transformation
\alpha_{M}\colon F\mathrm{T}M\to\mathrm{T}^{\prime}FM
, natural in
M
, called a
lax distributive law
satisfying the following conditions:

(a)

Additivity:

\displaystyle(\mathsf{id}_{F},\alpha)\colon F\mathbf{p}\to\mathbf{p}^{\prime}_{F}

is an additive bundle morphism, where
F\mathbf{p}
denotes
(Fp,Fz,Fs)
and
\mathbf{p}^{\prime}_{F}
denotes
(p^{\prime}_{F},z^{\prime}_{F},s^{\prime}_{F})
;

(b)

Compatibility with the vertical lift:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F\mathrm{T}^{2}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}F\mathrm{T}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime 2}FM}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F\mathrm{T}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}FM}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\mathrm{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fl}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l^{\prime}F}

(c)

Compatibility with the canonical flip:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F\mathrm{T}^{2}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}F\mathrm{T}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime 2}FM}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F\mathrm{T}^{2}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}F\mathrm{T}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime 2}FM}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\mathrm{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fc}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\mathrm{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{\prime}F}

A
colax tangent morphism
from
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
to
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
consists of a functor
G\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
together with a natural transformation
\beta_{M}\colon\mathrm{T}^{\prime}GM\to G\mathrm{T}M
, natural in
M
, called a
colax distributive law
, which satisfies the dual of the conditions of a lax distributive law, namely, additivity and the compatibility with the vertical lift and the canonical flip, in which the direction of the distributive law is now reversed.

A
strong tangent morphism
is a lax tangent morphism whose distributive law is invertible. Finally, a
strict tangent morphism
is a strong tangent morphism whose distributive law is the identity.

A tangent morphism
preserves the fundamental tangent limits
when its underlying functor does it.

A lax tangent morphism is denoted by
(F,\alpha)\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
while for colax tangent morphisms we adopt the notation
(F,\alpha)\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\nrightarrow(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
. Finally, a strict tangent morphism is simply denoted by its underlying functor.

Remark 2.8
.

Different conventions have been adopted in the literature around the definitions of colax and strong tangent morphisms. In particular, our convention aligns with
[
14
]
, but differs from
[
2
]
, in which strong tangent morphisms were required to preserve the fundamental tangent limits.

Remark 2.9
.

As pointed out in
[
3
, Section 4]
, a strong tangent morphism is also a colax tangent morphism (called a
comorphism
in that paper).

Let us recall the definition of a tangent natural transformation between tangent morphisms.

Definition 2.10
.

Given two lax tangent morphisms
(F,\alpha),(G,\beta)\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
, a
tangent natural transformation
from
(F,\alpha)
to
(G,\beta)
consists of a natural transformation
\varphi_{M}\colon FM\to GM
, natural in
M
, satisfying the following compatibility with the distributive laws:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F\mathrm{T}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}FM}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G\mathrm{T}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}GM}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi\mathrm{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

A tangent natural transformation between two colax tangent morphisms
(F,\alpha),(G,\beta)\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\nrightarrow(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
consists of a natural transformation
\varphi_{M}\colon FM\to GM
, natural in
M
, which satisfies the same compatibilities as a tangent natural transformation between lax tangent morphisms, where the distributive laws are reversed.

Finally, given two lax tangent morphisms
(F_{\circ},\alpha_{\circ})\colon(\mathbb{X}_{\circ},\mathbb{T}_{\circ})\to(\mathbb{X}_{\circ}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}_{\circ}^{\prime})
and two colax tangent morphisms
(G,\beta)\colon(\mathbb{X}_{\circ},\mathbb{T}_{\circ})\nrightarrow(\mathbb{X}_{\bullet},\mathbb{T}_{\bullet})
and
(G^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})\colon(\mathbb{X}_{\circ}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}_{\circ}^{\prime})\nrightarrow(\mathbb{X}_{\bullet}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}_{\bullet}^{\prime})
, a
double tangent morphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathbb{X}_{\circ},\mathbb{T}_{\circ})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathbb{X}_{\bullet},\mathbb{T}_{\bullet})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathbb{X}^{\prime}_{\circ},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}_{\circ})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathbb{X}_{\bullet}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}_{\bullet}^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(G,\beta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F_{\circ},\alpha_{\circ})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F_{\bullet},\alpha_{\bullet})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(G,^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

consists of a natural transformation
\varphi_{M}\colon F_{\bullet}GM\to G^{\prime}F_{\circ}M
, natural in
M
, satisfying the following compatibility with the distributive laws:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{\bullet}\mathrm{T}_{\bullet}GM}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{\bullet}G\mathrm{T}_{\circ}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G^{\prime}F_{\circ}\mathrm{T}_{\circ}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}_{\bullet}F_{\bullet}GM}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}_{\bullet}^{\prime}G^{\prime}F_{\circ}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G^{\prime}\mathrm{T}^{\prime}_{\circ}F_{\circ}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{\bullet}\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{\bullet}G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi\mathrm{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\prime}\alpha_{\circ}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\prime}_{\bullet}\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{\prime}F_{\circ}}

Tangent categories with their tangent
1
and
2
-morphisms can be organized into four distinct
2
-categories:

•

Tangent categories, lax tangent morphisms, and corresponding tangent natural transformations form the
2
-category
\mathsf{TNGCAT}
;

•

Tangent categories, colax tangent morphisms, and corresponding tangent natural transformations form the
2
-category
\mathsf{TNGCAT}_{\mathsf{co}}
;

•

Tangent categories, strong tangent morphisms, and corresponding tangent natural transformations form the
2
-category
\mathsf{TNGCAT}_{\cong}
;

•

Tangent categories, strict tangent morphisms, and corresponding tangent natural transformations form the
2
-category
\mathsf{TNGCAT}_{=}
.

Furthermore,

•

Tangent categories, lax tangent morphisms for vertical morphisms, colax tangent morphisms for horizontal morphisms, and double tangent morphisms for double morphisms form a double category denoted by
\mathbb{TNG}\mathsf{CAT}
.

In
[
16
]
, Leung presented a different approach to defining tangent categories, which comes in handy for our story. Let us briefly recall it here.

First, let
W_{n}
denote the rig (a.k.a., semiring) freely generated by
n
generators
\varepsilon_{1},\dots,\varepsilon_{n}
, subject to the following relations:

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j}=0

for every
i,j=1,\dots,n
. For
n=0
,
W_{0}
denotes the rig
\mathbb{N}
of natural numbers and for
n=1
,
W_{1}
is simply denoted by
W
.
\mathsf{WEIL}_{1}
denotes the strict symmetric monoidal category freely generated by all
W_{n}
for every positive integer
n
and by the following morphisms:

\displaystyle p\colon W\to\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle z\colon\mathbb{N}\to W

\displaystyle s\colon W_{2}\to W

\displaystyle p(\varepsilon)=0

\displaystyle z(1)=1

\displaystyle s(\varepsilon_{1})=\varepsilon=s(\varepsilon_{2})

\displaystyle l\colon W\to W\otimes W=W^{2}

\displaystyle c\colon W^{2}\to W^{2}

\displaystyle l(\varepsilon)=\varepsilon^{\prime}\varepsilon

\displaystyle c(\varepsilon)=\varepsilon^{\prime}\qquad c(\varepsilon^{\prime})=\varepsilon

where
W^{2}
denotes the rig freely generated by two generators
\varepsilon
and
\varepsilon^{\prime}
subject to the following relations

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{2}=0=\varepsilon^{\prime 2}

and where the monoidal product coincides with the usual tensor product of rigs, namely, the coproduct in the category of rigs.

The monoidal category
\mathsf{WEIL}_{1}
comes equipped with a tangent structure whose tangent bundle functor is the functor
W\otimes(-)
and whose structural natural transformations are induced by the structural morphisms listed above. Leung’s classification theorem states that a tangent structure
\mathbb{T}
on a category
\mathbb{X}
is equivalent to a strong monoidal functor

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}[\mathbb{T}]\colon\mathsf{WEIL}_{1}\to\mathsf{END}(\mathbb{X})

from the monoidal category
\mathsf{WEIL}_{1}
to the monoidal category
\mathsf{END}(\mathbb{X})
of endofunctors of
\mathbb{X}
, which preserves the fundamental tangent limits. Moreover, these limits are preserved in a pointwise way, meaning that the limit operator commutes with the evaluation functor.

Furthermore, a tangent structure with negatives
\mathbb{T}
is equivalent to a strong monoidal functor

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}^{-}[\mathbb{T}]\colon\mathsf{WEIL}_{1}^{-}\to\mathsf{END}(\mathbb{X})

preserving the fundamental pointwise tangent limits, where
\mathsf{WEIL}_{1}^{-}
is obtained by replacing the generators
W_{n}
of
\mathsf{WEIL}_{1}
with the rings
W_{n}^{-}
(not just rigs, so in particular, now
W_{0}^{-}
denotes the ring
\mathbb{Z}
of integers). In particular,
\mathsf{WEIL}_{1}^{-}
comes equipped with an extra structural morphism:

\displaystyle n\colon W^{-}\to W^{-}

\displaystyle n(\varepsilon)=-\varepsilon

Let us introduce the following naming convention.

Definition 2.11
.

A
Leung functor
consists of a strong monoidal functor

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}[\mathbb{T}]\colon\mathsf{WEIL}_{1}\to\mathsf{END}(\mathbb{X})

which preserves the fundamental pointwise tangent limits, namely, which commutes with the evaluation functor. Moreover, a
Leung functor with negatives
is a strong monoidal functor

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}^{-}[\mathbb{T}]\colon\mathsf{WEIL}_{1}^{-}\to\mathsf{END}(\mathbb{X})

which preserves the fundamental pointwise tangent limits.

2.2
Tangentads formalize tangent categories

In this section, we introduce the protagonist of this paper: the formal notion of a tangentad. The first definition of this concept appears in
[
15
]
in the context of tangent fibrations, with the name of
tangent object
.

In this section, we start by recalling the definition of a tangentad, by extending Leung’s classification of tangent categories, and by spelling out the details in a more concrete form. Secondly, Lax, colax, strong, and strict tangent
1
-morphisms are introduced, together with the corresponding notions of
2
and double morphisms.

For this section, let
{\mathbf{K}}
denote a strict
2
-category. In order to define the notion of a tangentad, first we need to clarify the notion of
pointwise limits
in a given
2
-category.

Definition 2.12
.

Given a strict
2
-category
{\mathbf{K}}
and two objects
\mathbb{X}
and
\mathbb{Y}
of
{\mathbf{K}}
, a limit in the category
{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{Y})
is
pointwise
when it is preserved by each functor
{\mathbf{K}}(f,\mathbb{Y})\colon{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{Y})\to{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{Y})
induced by
1
-morphisms
f\colon\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{X}
of
{\mathbf{K}}
.

Remark 2.13
.

The author is grateful to Rory Lucyshyn-Wright for pointing out the importance of this assumption for tangentads during an informal discussion. This aspect was missing in the original definition provided by the author.

When the
2
-category
{\mathbf{K}}
is the
2
-category
\mathsf{CAT}
of categories, pointwise limits of
{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{Y})
are those limit diagrams in the category of functors of type
F\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{Y}
that are preserved by the evaluation functor. Concretely, this means that, for an object
X
of
\mathbb{X}
, and a diagram
D\colon I\to{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{Y})
, the functor
\lim D\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{Y}
evaluated at
X
is isomorphic to the object
\lim D(X)
of
\mathbb{Y}
, where
D(X)
represents the diagram
I\to\mathbb{Y}
obtained by evaluating each functor
D_{A}\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{Y}
, for each
A
of
I
, at
X
.

When the target category
\mathbb{Y}
has all finite limits, so does the category of functors
{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{Y})
and each limit is pointwise. However, when the target category is not known to be finitely complete, there is no guarantee for the limits of
{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{Y})
to be pointwise. A counterexample can be found in
[
11
, Section 3.3]
.

Unfortunately, in tangent category theory, the requirement of the existence of limits is a subtle matter since in differential geometry, not every pair of morphisms admits a pullback. In particular, a tangent category cannot be required to be finitely complete since this would rule out one of the main examples of a tangent category. Therefore, to make our definition of tangentads compatible with the usual notion of a tangent category, the limits involved in the definition of a tangentad must be pointwise.

Definition 2.14
.

A
tangentad
in a
2
-category
{\mathbf{K}}
consists of an object
\mathbb{X}
of
{\mathbf{K}}
together with a strong monoidal functor:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}[\mathbb{T}]\colon\mathsf{WEIL}_{1}\to\mathsf{END}_{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbb{X})

which preserves the fundamental pointwise tangent limits. Moreover, a tangentad
has negatives
when the strong monoidal functor
\mathfrak{L}[\mathbb{T}]
factors through the inclusion
\mathsf{WEIL}_{1}\to\mathsf{WEIL}_{1}^{-}
.

Remark 2.15
.

In
[
16
, Definition 14.2]
, Leung already suggested a generalization of a tangent category, by introducing the notion of a
tangent structure internal
to a monoidal category
\mathcal{E}
as a strong monoidal functor
\mathfrak{L}[\mathbb{T}]\colon\mathsf{WEIL}_{1}\to\mathcal{E}
which preserves the fundamental pointwise tangent limits. In this sense, a tangentad consists of an object
\mathbb{X}
in a
2
-category
{\mathbf{K}}
together with a tangent structure internal to the monoidal category
\mathsf{END}(\mathbb{X})
.

Another approach to generalize tangent structures was employed in
[
1
]
in which
\infty
-tangent category is defined as a strong monoidal functor from the
\infty
-category of Weil algebras
\mathsf{WEIL}_{1}^{\infty}
to the monoidal category
\mathsf{END}(\mathbb{X})
of endomorphisms of an
\infty
-category
\mathbb{X}
.

As pointed out by Michael Ching in an informal discussion with the author,
\infty
-tangent categories generalize to strong monoidal functors from
\mathsf{WEIL}_{1}^{\infty}
to the monoidal category
\mathsf{END}_{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbb{X})
, where
{\mathbf{K}}
is a
(\infty,2)
-category, which preserve the fundamental tangent limits. Such functors split along the homotopy category of
\mathsf{WEIL}_{1}^{\infty}
, which coincides with
\mathsf{WEIL}_{1}
. Thus, every such functor gives rise to a tangentad.

Definition
2.14
is rather abstract; to have a better grasp of this concept, let us unwrap it into a more concrete form. A tangentad of
{\mathbf{K}}
comprises:

​
[
Base object
]
:

An object
\mathbb{X}
;

​
[
Tangent bundle
1
-morphism
]
:

A
1
-endomorphism
\mathrm{T}\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}
;

​
[
Projection
]
:

A
2
-morphism
p\colon\mathrm{T}\Rightarrow\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}
, which admits all pointwise
n
-fold pullbacks

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

which are preserved by all iterates
\mathrm{T}^{n}
of
\mathrm{T}
;

​
[
Zero morphism
]
:

A
2
-morphism
z\colon\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}
;

​
[
Sum morphism
]
:

A
2
-morphism
s\colon\mathrm{T}_{2}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}
;

such that,
\mathbf{p}\>\colon\!=(p,z,s)
is a
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
-additive bundle of
\mathsf{END}(\mathbb{X})
, where
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
sends an endomorphism
F\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}
to
\mathrm{T}\circ F
;

​
[
Vertical lift
]
:

A
2
-morphism
l\colon\mathrm{T}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{2}
such that

\displaystyle(z,l)\colon\mathbf{p}\to\mathrm{T}\mathbf{p}

is an additive bundle morphism;

​
[
Canonical flip
]
:

A
2
-morphism
c\colon\mathrm{T}^{2}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{2}
such that

\displaystyle(\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}},c)\colon\mathrm{T}\mathbf{p}\to\mathbf{p}_{\mathrm{T}}

is an additive bundle morphism;

Moreover, the following coherence conditions must hold:

(a)

The vertical lift is coassocative, and compatible with the canonical flip:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{3}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{\mathrm{T}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{3}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{\mathrm{T}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{\mathrm{T}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}l}

(b)

The canonical flip is a symmetric braiding:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{3}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{\mathrm{T}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{\mathrm{T}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{\mathrm{T}}}

(c)

The tangent bundle is locally linear, namely, the following is a pointwise pullback diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}\circ\mathrm{T}_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{\mathrm{T}}\times l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}

Furthermore, a tangentad admits negatives when it is equipped with:

​
[
Negation
]
:

A
2
-morphism
n\colon\mathrm{T}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}
such that:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle n,\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}

We can now define morphisms of tangentads.

Definition 2.16
.

Let
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
and
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
be two tangentads of
{\mathbf{K}}
. A
lax tangent
1
-morphism
from
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
to
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
consists of a
1
-morphism
F\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
together with a
2
-morphism
\alpha\colon F\circ\mathrm{T}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\circ F
, called a
lax distributive law
satisfying the following conditions:

(a)

Additivity:

\displaystyle(\mathsf{id}_{F},\alpha)\colon F\mathbf{p}\to\mathbf{p}^{\prime}_{F}

is an additive bundle morphism, where
F\mathbf{p}
denotes
(Fp,Fz,Fs)
and
\mathbf{p}^{\prime}_{F}
denotes
(p^{\prime}_{F},z^{\prime}_{F},s^{\prime}_{F})
;

(b)

Compatibility with the vertical lift:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F\circ\mathrm{T}^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\circ F\circ\mathrm{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime 2}\circ F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F\circ\mathrm{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\circ F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\mathrm{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fl}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l^{\prime}F}

(c)

Compatibility with the canonical flip:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F\circ\mathrm{T}^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\circ F\circ\mathrm{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime 2}\circ F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F\circ\mathrm{T}^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\circ F\circ\mathrm{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime 2}\circ F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\mathrm{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fc}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\mathrm{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{\prime}F}

A
colax tangent
1
-morphism
from
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
to
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
consists of a
1
-morphism
G\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
together with a
2
-morphism
\beta\colon\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\circ G\Rightarrow G\circ\mathrm{T}
, called a
colax distributive law
, which satisfies the dual conditions of a lax distributive law, namely, additivity and the compatibility with the vertical lift and the canonical flip, in which the direction of the distributive law is now reversed.

A
strong tangent
1
-morphism
is a lax tangent
1
-morphism whose distributive law is invertible. A
strict tangent
1
-morphism
is a strong tangent
1
-morphism whose distributive law is the identity.

Finally, a tangent morphism
(F,\alpha)\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})

preserves the fundamental tangent limits
when the functor
{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbb{X},F)\colon\mathsf{END}(\mathbb{X})\to{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{X}^{\prime})
preserves the fundamental pointwise tangent limits of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.
A lax tangent
1
-morphism is denoted by
(F,\alpha)\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
while for colax tangent
1
-morphisms we adopt the notation
(F,\alpha)\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\nrightarrow(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
. Finally, a strict tangent
1
-morphism is denoted by its underlying
1
-morphism.

Definition 2.17
.

Given two lax tangent morphisms
(F,\alpha),(G,\beta)\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
of tangentads in
{\mathbf{K}}
, a
tangent
2
-morphism
from
(F,\alpha)
to
(G,\beta)
consists of a
2
-morphism
\varphi\colon F\Rightarrow G
satisfying the following compatibility between the distributive laws:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F\circ\mathrm{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\circ F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G\circ\mathrm{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\circ G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi\mathrm{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

A tangent
2
-morphism between two colax tangent morphisms
(F,\alpha),(G,\beta)\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\nrightarrow(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
consists of a
2
-morphism
\varphi\colon F\Rightarrow G
which satisfies the same compatibility as a tangent
2
-morphism between lax tangent morphisms, where the distributive laws are reversed.

Finally, given two lax tangent morphisms
(F_{\circ},\alpha_{\circ})\colon(\mathbb{X}_{\circ},\mathbb{T}_{\circ})\to(\mathbb{X}_{\circ}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}_{\circ}^{\prime})
and two colax tangent morphisms
(G,\beta)\colon(\mathbb{X}_{\circ},\mathbb{T}_{\circ})\nrightarrow(\mathbb{X}_{\bullet},\mathbb{T}_{\bullet})
and
(G^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})\colon(\mathbb{X}_{\circ}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}_{\circ}^{\prime})\nrightarrow(\mathbb{X}_{\bullet}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}_{\bullet}^{\prime})
, a
double tangent morphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathbb{X}_{\circ},\mathbb{T}_{\circ})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathbb{X}_{\bullet},\mathbb{T}_{\bullet})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathbb{X}^{\prime}_{\circ},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}_{\circ})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathbb{X}_{\bullet}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}_{\bullet}^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(G,\beta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F_{\circ},\alpha_{\circ})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F_{\bullet},\alpha_{\bullet})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(G,^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

consists of a
2
-morphism
\varphi\colon F_{\bullet}\circ G\Rightarrow G^{\prime}\circ F_{\circ}
satisfying the following compatibility with the distributive laws:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{\bullet}\circ\mathrm{T}_{\bullet}\circ G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{\bullet}\circ G\circ\mathrm{T}_{\circ}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G^{\prime}\circ F_{\circ}\circ\mathrm{T}_{\circ}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}_{\bullet}\circ F_{\bullet}\circ G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}_{\bullet}^{\prime}\circ G^{\prime}\circ F_{\circ}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G^{\prime}\circ\mathrm{T}^{\prime}_{\circ}\circ F_{\circ}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{\bullet}\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{\bullet}G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi\mathrm{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\prime}\alpha_{\circ}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\prime}_{\bullet}\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{\prime}F_{\circ}}

Tangentads with their tangent
1
and
2
-morphisms can be organized into four distinct
2
-categories:

•

Tangentads of
{\mathbf{K}}
, lax tangent morphisms, and corresponding
2
-morphisms form the
2
-category
\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})
;

•

Tangentads of
{\mathbf{K}}
, colax tangent morphisms, and corresponding
2
-morphisms form the
2
-category
\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}({\mathbf{K}})
;

•

Tangentads of
{\mathbf{K}}
, strong tangent morphisms, and corresponding
2
-morphisms form the
2
-category
\mathsf{TNG}_{\cong}({\mathbf{K}})
;

•

Tangent categories, strict tangent morphisms, and corresponding
2
-morphisms form the
2
-category
\mathsf{TNG}_{=}({\mathbf{K}})
.

Furthermore:

•

Tangentads of
{\mathbf{K}}
, lax tangent
1
-morphisms for vertical morphisms, colax tangent
1
-morphisms for horizontal morphisms, and double tangent morphisms for double morphisms form a double category denoted by
\mathbb{T}\mathsf{NG}({\mathbf{K}})
.

Example 2.18
.

The archetypal example of a tangentad is a tangent category. Indeed, tangent categories are precisely tangentads in the
2
-category
\mathsf{CAT}
of categories.

Example 2.19
.

In a
2
-category
{\mathbf{K}}
, every object
\mathbb{X}
is trivially a tangentad once equipped with the trivial tangent structure
\mathds{1}
, whose tangent
1
-morphism is the identity over
\mathbb{X}
and all the structural
2
-morphisms are the identity over
\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}
.

Example
2.19
induces a
2
-functor:

\displaystyle\mathsf{Inc}\colon{\mathbf{K}}\to\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})

\displaystyle\mathsf{Inc}(\mathbb{X})\>\colon\!=(\mathbb{X},\mathds{1})

\displaystyle\mathsf{Inc}(F\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}^{\prime})\>\colon\!=F\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathds{1})\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathds{1})

\displaystyle\mathsf{Inc}(\varphi\colon F\Rightarrow G)\>\colon\!=\varphi\colon F\Rightarrow G

which sends each object to the trivial tangentad.

Proposition 2.20
.

The forgetful
2
-functor
\mathsf{U}\colon\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})\to{\mathbf{K}}
, which forgets the tangent structure, is both a left and a right
2
-adjoint to the inclusion
2
-functor
\mathsf{Inc}\colon{\mathbf{K}}\to\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})
.

Proof.

First, let us prove that
\mathsf{Inc}\dashv\mathsf{U}
forms an adjunction. The unit

\displaystyle\eta\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathds{1})=\mathsf{Inc}(\mathsf{U}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}))

is the lax tangent morphism
(\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}},p)\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathds{1})
, induced by the projection
p
of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. While the counit

\displaystyle\varepsilon\colon\mathsf{U}(\mathsf{Inc}(\mathbb{X}))=\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}

is the identity. Let us now show that
\mathsf{U}\dashv\mathsf{Inc}
forms a second adjunction. The unit

\displaystyle\theta\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}=\mathsf{U}(\mathsf{Inc}(\mathbb{X}))

is the identity, while the counit

\displaystyle\tau\colon\mathsf{Inc}(\mathsf{U}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}))=(\mathbb{X},\mathds{1})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})

is the lax tangent morphism
(\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}},z)\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathds{1})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, induced by the zero morphism
z
of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.
∎

Dually, one can also observe that the
2
-functor
\mathsf{U}_{\mathsf{co}}\colon\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}({\mathbf{K}})\to{\mathbf{K}}
which forgets the tangent structure also admits a left and a right adjoint
\mathsf{Inc}_{\mathsf{co}}\colon{\mathbf{K}}\to\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}({\mathbf{K}})
. Since
\mathsf{U}_{\mathsf{co}}
plays an important role in the next section, let us state this in a proposition.

Proposition 2.21
.

The forgetful
2
-functor
\mathsf{U}_{\mathsf{co}}\colon\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}({\mathbf{K}})\to{\mathbf{K}}
which forgets the tangent structure is both a left and a right
2
-adjoint to the inclusion
2
-functor
\mathsf{Inc}_{\mathsf{co}}\colon{\mathbf{K}}\to\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}({\mathbf{K}})
.

2.3
The monad structure of a tangentad

In
[
2
]
, Cockett and Cruttwell showed that the tangent bundle functor
\mathrm{T}\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}
of a tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
comes equipped with a monad structure for which
c\colon\mathrm{T}^{2}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{2}
defines a distributive law and such that
l\colon\mathrm{T}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{2}
becomes a monad morphism between
\mathrm{T}
and the monad
\mathrm{T}^{2}
induced by the distributive law
c
. In particular, these are the data of a tangent monad, as introduced in
[
8
, Definition 19]
, which is a monad internal to the
2
-category
\mathsf{TNGCAT}
.

This construction can be easily formalized within the context of tangentad theory. In this section, we briefly recall this construction.

Lemma 2.22
.

The tangent bundle
1
-morphism
\mathrm{T}\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}
of a tangentad
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
in a
2
-category
{\mathbf{K}}
comes equipped with a tangent monad structure on
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. Concretely, this structure comprises a monad structure on
\mathrm{T}
, whose unit is defined by the zero morphism
z\colon\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}
and whose multiplication is defined as follows:

\displaystyle\mu\colon\mathrm{T}^{2}\xrightarrow{\langle\mathrm{T}p,p_{\mathrm{T}}\rangle}\mathrm{T}_{2}\xrightarrow{s}\mathrm{T}

Moreover, such a structure also includes a distributive law, represented by the canonical flip
c
, between the monad
(\mathrm{T},z,\mu)
and the tangent bundle
1
-morphism
\mathrm{T}
, which makes
\mathrm{T}
into a strong tangent morphism
(\mathrm{T},c)\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.

Proof.

The proof is precisely the same as the one of
[
2
, Propositions 3.4 and 3.5]
.
∎

Lemma 2.23
.

The operation defined by Lemma
2.22
which sends a tangentad
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
to a tangent monad
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};\mathrm{T},z,\mu,c)
extends to a
2
-functor:

\displaystyle\mathrm{M}\colon\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}({\mathbf{K}})\to\mathsf{TNGMND}({\mathbf{K}})

Proof.

The compatibility of the colax distributive law of a lax tangent morphism
(F,\alpha)\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\nrightarrow(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
with the tangent structures makes
\alpha\colon\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\circ F\Rightarrow F\circ\mathrm{T}
into a
2
-morphism of tangentads:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F,\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{T},c)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{T}^{\prime},c^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F,\alpha)}

Moreover,
\alpha
is compatible with the multiplications and the units of the monads
(\mathrm{T},\mu,z)
and
(\mathrm{T}^{\prime},\mu^{\prime},z^{\prime})
. Thus,
(F,\alpha)
becomes a morphism of tangent monads. Finally, a
2
-morphism
\varphi\colon(F,\alpha)\Rightarrow(G,\beta)\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\nrightarrow(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
of tangentads defines a
2
-morphism of tangent monads.
∎

3
Global properties and structures of tangentads

In previous sections, we introduced tangentads and discussed some examples. In this section, we investigate some of the global structures and the global properties of the operation which sends a
2
-category to the
2
-category of tangentads.

First, in Section
3.1
, we show that this assignment is
2
-functorial. In particular, we establish that pointwise pullback-preserving
2
-functors
\Gamma
are sent to
2
-functors
\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}(\Gamma)
.

In Section
3.2
, we prove that each
\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}(\Gamma)
is also pointwise pullback-preserving and that
\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}
becomes an endofunctor on the
2
-category
\mathsf{TWOCAT}_{\mathrm{pp}}
. We use this argument to construct a
2
-comonad structure on
\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}
.

In Section
3.3
, we introduce Cartesian tangentads and discuss the relationship between Cartesian objects in
\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})
and tangentads in
\mathsf{CART}({\mathbf{K}})
, proving they are
2
-equivalent.

In Section
3.4
, we extend the notion of adjunctability and representability to the formal context of tangentads and in
3.5
, we formalize Cockett and Cruttwell’s construction of the subtangent category with negatives
[
2
, Proposition 3.12]
as a right Kan extension.

3.1
The functoriality of tangentads and some technical lemmas

For starters, let u prove a useful lemma.

Lemma 3.1
.

The
2
-functor
\mathsf{U}_{\mathsf{co}}\colon\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}({\mathbf{K}})\to{\mathbf{K}}
reflects pointwise limits. Concretely, given a diagram

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle\colon I\to\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}({\mathbf{K}})((\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}),(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}))

and a cone
\lambda^{c}\colon L\to D(c)
of
D
, if the cone

\displaystyle\mathsf{U}[\lambda^{c}]\colon\mathsf{U}[L]\to\mathsf{U}[D(c)]

is a pointwise limit cone of the diagram

\displaystyle\mathsf{U}[D]\colon I\xrightarrow{D}\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}({\mathbf{K}})((\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}),(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}))\xrightarrow{\mathsf{U}}{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{X}^{\prime})

then
\lambda^{c}
is a pointwise limit cone of
D
.

Proof.

Consider a diagram
D\colon I\to\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}({\mathbf{K}})((\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}),(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}))
and let
\lambda^{c}\colon L\to D(c)
be a cone of
D
. Since
D
is a diagram in
\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}({\mathbf{K}})((\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}),(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}))
each
D(c)
consists of a colax tangent morphism
(F^{c},\alpha^{c})\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
, and each morphism
D(f\colon c\to d)
consists of a
2
-morphism of tangentads
\varphi^{f}\colon(F^{c},\alpha^{c})\to(F^{d},\alpha^{d})
. Similarly,
L
consists of a colax tangent morphism
(L,\kappa)\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
and
\lambda^{c}\colon(L,\kappa)\to(F^{c},\alpha^{c})
is a
2
-morphism of tangentads.

Suppose that
\mathsf{U}[\lambda^{c}]\colon\mathsf{U}[L]\to\mathsf{U}[D(c)]
is a pointwise limit cone of
{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{X}^{\prime})
. Being pointwise means that for each morphism
G\colon\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime}\to\mathbb{X}
, the cone

\displaystyle\mathsf{U}[\lambda^{c}]_{G}\colon\mathsf{U}[L]\circ G\to\mathsf{U}[D(c)]\circ G

is a limit cone of
\mathsf{U}[D]\circ{\mathbf{K}}(G,\mathbb{X}^{\prime})
in
{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime},\mathbb{X}^{\prime})
.

Consider a colax tangent morphism
(G,\beta)\colon(\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. We want to show that

\displaystyle\lambda^{c}_{(G,\beta)}\colon(L,\kappa)\circ(G,\beta)\to(F^{c},\alpha^{c})\circ(G,\beta)

is a limit cone in
\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}({\mathbf{K}})((\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime}),(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}))
. Consider a cone
\gamma^{c}\colon(H,\theta)\to(F^{c},\alpha^{c})\circ(G,\beta)
. Since
\mathsf{U}[\lambda^{c}]
is pointwise,
\mathsf{U}[\lambda^{c}_{(G,\beta)}]=\mathsf{U}[\lambda^{c}]_{G}
is a limit cone in
{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime},\mathbb{X}^{\prime})
, thus, by the universal property of
\mathsf{U}[\lambda^{c}]_{G}
, we obtain a unique morphism of cones

\displaystyle\varphi

\displaystyle\colon\mathsf{U}[\gamma^{c}]\to\mathsf{U}[\lambda^{c}_{(G,\beta)}]

which corresponds to a
2
-morphism
\varphi\colon H\Rightarrow L\circ G
of
{\mathbf{K}}
, satisfying

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{L\circ G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{c}\circ G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda^{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma^{c}}

for every
c\in I
. We need to show that
\varphi
is a
2
-morphism of tangentads. Let us start by considering the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\circ H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H\circ\mathrm{T}^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\circ L\circ G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{L\circ\mathrm{T}\circ G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{L\circ G\circ\mathrm{T}^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\circ F^{c}\circ G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{c}\circ\mathrm{T}\circ G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{c}\circ G\circ\mathrm{T}^{\prime\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\gamma^{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi\mathrm{T}^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma^{c}\mathrm{T}^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\lambda^{c}_{(G,\beta)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda^{c}_{\mathrm{T}G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda^{c}_{(G,\beta)}\mathrm{T}^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{c}G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{c}\beta}

(3.1)

The two bottom trapeze diagrams commute since
\lambda^{c}
is a
2
-morphism of tangentads and so does the outer square since
\gamma^{c}
is a
2
-morphism of tangentads. The left and right triangles commute by definition of
\varphi
. Using the universal property of
\mathsf{U}[\lambda^{c}_{(G,\beta)}]_{\mathrm{T}^{\prime\prime}}
we prove that the top trapeze diagram commutes as well, implying that
\varphi
is a
2
-morphism of tangentads. Finally, the uniqueness of
\varphi
is implied by the universal property of
\mathsf{U}[\lambda^{c}_{(G,\beta)}]_{\mathrm{T}^{\prime\prime}}
.
∎

Remark 3.2
.

One could wonder why, in Lemma
3.1
, we consider colax tangent morphisms instead of lax ones. The reason lies in Diagram (
3.1
). Indeed, to prove the commutativity of the upper trapeze diagram, we invoked the universal property of the limit cone
\lambda^{c}_{(G,\beta)}\mathrm{T}^{\prime\prime}
. When one considers lax tangent morphisms, the distributive laws
\theta
,
\kappa
,
\alpha^{c}
, and
\beta
go in the opposite direction, so to prove the commutativity of the upper trapeze, one needs the cone
\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\lambda^{c}_{(G,\beta)}
to be a limit cone. This is true for tangent limits, but it would not be true in general.

Remark
3.2
suggests a weaker version of Lemma
3.1
in the context of lax tangent morphisms.

Proposition 3.3
.

The
2
-functor
\mathsf{U}\colon\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})\to{\mathbf{K}}
reflects pointwise tangent limits. Concretely, given a diagram
D\colon I\to\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})((\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}),(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}))
and a cone
\lambda^{c}\colon L\to D(c)
of
D
, if the cone

\displaystyle\mathsf{U}[\lambda^{c}]\colon\mathsf{U}[L]\to\mathsf{U}[D(c)]

is a pointwise limit cone of the diagram

\displaystyle\mathsf{U}[D]\colon I\xrightarrow{D}\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})((\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}),(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}))\xrightarrow{\mathsf{U}}{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{X}^{\prime})

and its universal property is preserved by all functors
{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbb{X},{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}}^{n})
, where
\mathrm{T}^{\prime}
is the tangent bundle
1
-morphism of
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
, then
\lambda^{c}
is a pointwise limit cone of
D
and it is preserved by all functors
\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})((\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}),(\mathrm{T}^{\prime},c^{\prime}))
.

Proof.

Consider a diagram
D\colon I\to\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})((\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}),(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}))
and let
\lambda^{c}\colon L\to D(c)
be a cone of
D
. Since
D
is a diagram in
\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})((\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}),(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}))
each
D(c)
consists of a lax tangent morphism
(F^{c},\alpha^{c})\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
, and each morphism
D(f\colon c\to d)
consists of a
2
-morphism of tangentads
\varphi^{f}\colon(F^{c},\alpha^{c})\to(F^{d},\alpha^{d})
. Similarly,
L
consists of a lax tangent morphism
(L,\kappa)\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
and
\lambda^{c}\colon(L,\kappa)\to(F^{c},\alpha^{c})
a
2
-morphism of tangentads.

Suppose that
\mathsf{U}[\lambda^{c}]\colon\mathsf{U}[L]\to\mathsf{U}[D(c)]
is a pointwise tangent limit cone of
{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{X}^{\prime})
. Being pointwise and tangent means that for each morphism
G\colon\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime}\to\mathbb{X}
and each positive integer
n
, the cone

\displaystyle{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}}^{n}\mathsf{U}[\lambda^{c}]_{G}\colon{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}}^{n}\circ\mathsf{U}[L]\circ G\to{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}}^{n}\circ\mathsf{U}[D(c)]\circ G

is a limit cone of
{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}}^{n}\circ\mathsf{U}[D]\circ{\mathbf{K}}(G,\mathbb{X}^{\prime})
in
{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime},\mathbb{X}^{\prime})
.

Consider a lax tangent morphism
(G,\beta)\colon(\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. We want to prove that

\displaystyle(\mathrm{T}^{\prime},c^{\prime})^{n}\lambda^{c}_{(G,\beta)}\colon(\mathrm{T}^{\prime},c^{\prime})^{n}\circ(L,\kappa)\circ(G,\beta)\to(\mathrm{T}^{\prime},c^{\prime})^{n}\circ(F^{c},\alpha^{c})\circ(G,\beta)

is a limit cone in
\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})((\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime}),(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}))
. Consider a cone
\gamma^{c}\colon(H,\theta)\to(\mathrm{T}^{\prime},c^{\prime})^{n}\circ(F^{c},\alpha^{c})\circ(G,\beta)
. Since
\mathsf{U}[\lambda^{c}]
is pointwise,
{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}}^{n}\mathsf{U}[\lambda^{c}_{(G,\beta)}]={\mathrm{T}^{\prime}}^{n}\mathsf{U}[\lambda^{c}]_{G}
is a limit cone in
{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime},\mathbb{X}^{\prime})
, thus, by its universal property we obtain a unique morphism of cones

\displaystyle\varphi

\displaystyle\colon\mathsf{U}[\gamma^{c}]\to{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}}^{n}\mathsf{U}[\lambda^{c}_{(G,\beta)}]

which corresponds to a
2
-morphism
\varphi\colon H\Rightarrow{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}}^{n}\circ L\circ G
of
{\mathbf{K}}
, satisfying

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}}^{n}\circ L\circ G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}}^{n}\circ F^{c}\circ G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}}^{n}\lambda^{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma^{c}}

for every
c\in I
. We need to show that
\varphi
is a
2
-morphism of tangentads. Let us start by considering the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H\circ\mathrm{T}^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\circ H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}}^{n}\circ L\circ G\circ\mathrm{T}^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}}^{n}\circ L\circ\mathrm{T}\circ G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}}^{n+1}\circ L\circ G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}}^{n}\circ F^{c}\circ G\circ\mathrm{T}^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}}^{n}\circ F^{c}\circ\mathrm{T}\circ G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}}^{n+1}\circ F^{c}\circ G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi\mathrm{T}^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma^{c}\mathrm{T}^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\gamma^{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}}^{n}L\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}}^{n}\lambda^{c}_{(G,\beta)}\mathrm{T}^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}}^{n}\kappa G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}}^{n}\lambda^{c}_{\mathrm{T}G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}}^{n+1}\lambda^{c}_{(G,\beta)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}}^{n}F^{c}\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}}^{n}\alpha^{c}G}

The two bottom trapeze diagrams commute since
\lambda^{c}
is a
2
-morphism of tangentads, and so does the outer square since
\gamma^{c}
is a
2
-morphism of tangentads. The left and right triangles commute by definition of
\varphi
. Using the universal property of
{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}}^{n}\mathsf{U}[\lambda^{c}_{(G,\beta)}]
, we prove that the top trapeze diagram commutes as well, implying that
\varphi
is a
2
-morphism of tangentads. Finally, the uniqueness of
\varphi
is implied by the universal property of
{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}}^{n}\mathsf{U}[\lambda^{c}_{(G,\beta)}]
.
∎

To extend the operation
\mathsf{TNG}
which sends a
2
-category
{\mathbf{K}}
into the
2
-category
\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})
of tangentads of
{\mathbf{K}}
, first, we need to introduce the “correct” type of
2
-functor which behaves well with the operation
\mathsf{TNG}
.

Definition 3.4
.

A
2
-functor
\Gamma\colon{\mathbf{K}}\to{\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}
is
pointwise pullback-preserving
if it preserves every pointwise pullback in each hom category
\mathsf{END}(\mathbb{X})
.

In the following, we denote by
\mathsf{TWOCAT}_{\mathrm{pp}}
the
2
-category of
2
-categories, whose
1
-morphisms are pointwise pullback-preserving
2
-functors, and whose
2
-morphisms are
2
-natural transformations.

In the following, we repeatedly make use of the following technical lemma.

Lemma 3.5
.

If a
2
-functor
\Gamma\colon{\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}\to{\mathbf{K}}
admits a left
2
-adjoint
\Omega\colon{\mathbf{K}}\to{\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}
,
\Gamma
preserves pointwise limits. Concretely, this means that if a diagram
D\colon I\to{\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{X}^{\prime})
admits a pointwise limit, so does the diagram:

\displaystyle\Gamma[D]\colon I\xrightarrow{D}{\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{X}^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\Gamma}{\mathbf{K}}(\Gamma[\mathbb{X}],\Gamma[\mathbb{X}^{\prime}])

In particular,
\Gamma
is pointwise pullback-preserving.

Proof.

Consider a diagram
D\colon I\to{\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{Y})
and let
\lambda^{c}\colon L\to D(c)
be a pointwise limit cone of
D
. The limit cone being pointwise means that for each morphism
G\colon\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{X}
, the cone

\displaystyle\lambda^{c}_{G}\colon L\circ G\to D(c)\circ G

is a limit cone for
{\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}(G,\mathbb{Y})\circ D\colon I\to{\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{Y})
. Showing that
\Gamma
preserves pointwise limits amounts to prove that for each
G\colon\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\to\Gamma[\mathbb{X}]
in
{\mathbf{K}}
, the cone

\displaystyle\Gamma[\lambda^{c}]_{G}\colon\Gamma[L]\circ G\to\Gamma[D(c)]\circ G

is a limit cone for:

\displaystyle I\xrightarrow{\Gamma[D]}{\mathbf{K}}(\Gamma[\mathbb{X}],\Gamma[\mathbb{Y}])\xrightarrow{{\mathbf{K}}(G,\mathbb{Y})}{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\Gamma[\mathbb{Y}])

Let
\eta\colon\mathsf{id}_{\mathbf{K}}\to\Gamma\circ\Omega
and
\varepsilon\colon\Omega\circ\Gamma\to\mathsf{id}_{{\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}}
denote the unit and the counit of the
2
-adjunction, respectively. We can start by invoking the
2
-adjunction and define
G^{\flat}
as follows:

\displaystyle G^{\flat}\colon\Omega[\mathbb{X}^{\prime}]\xrightarrow{\Omega[G]}\Omega\Gamma[\mathbb{X}]\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}\mathbb{X}

Since the limit cone
\lambda^{c}
is pointwise

\displaystyle\lambda^{c}_{G^{\flat}}\colon L\circ G^{\flat}\to D(c)\circ G^{\flat}

is a limit cone of:

\displaystyle D\circ G^{\flat}\colon I\xrightarrow{D}{\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{Y})\xrightarrow{{\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}(G^{\flat},\mathbb{Y})}{\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}(\Omega[\mathbb{X}],\mathbb{Y})

Furthermore, since
\Gamma
is a right
2
-adjoint it preserves limits, therefore

\displaystyle\Gamma[\lambda^{c}_{G^{\flat}}]\colon\Gamma[L\circ G^{\flat}]\to\Gamma[D(c)\circ G^{\flat}]

is a limit cone of
\Gamma[D\circ G^{\flat}]
. By employing the
2
-functoriality of
\Gamma
and the triangle identities, one can show that

\displaystyle I\xrightarrow{\Gamma[D\circ G^{\flat}]}{\mathbf{K}}(\Gamma\Omega[\mathbb{X}],\Gamma[\mathbb{Y}])\xrightarrow{{\mathbf{K}}(\eta,\Gamma[\mathbb{Y}])}{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbb{X},\Gamma[\mathbb{Y}])

is exactly
{\mathbf{K}}(G,\mathbb{Y})\circ\Gamma[D]
. So, we need to show that

\displaystyle\Gamma[\lambda^{c}_{G^{\flat}}]_{\eta}\colon\Gamma[L\circ G^{\flat}]\circ\eta\to\Gamma[D(c)\circ G^{\flat}]\circ\eta

is a limit cone for
{\mathbf{K}}(G,\mathbb{Y})\circ\Gamma[D]
. Consider a cone
\gamma^{c}\colon H\to\Gamma[D(c)\circ G^{\flat}]\circ\eta
in
{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\Gamma[\mathbb{Y}])
. Using triangle identities, one can see that:

\displaystyle\Gamma[\varepsilon]\circ\Gamma\Omega\left[\Gamma[D(c)\circ G^{\flat}]\circ\eta\right]=\Gamma[D(c)\circ G^{\flat}]

Therefore, by the universal property of
\Gamma[\lambda^{c}_{G^{\flat}}]
, we obtain a unique morphism of cones
\varphi
satisfying:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma[L\circ G^{\bullet}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma[D(c)\circ G^{\flat}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma[\varepsilon]\circ\Gamma\Omega[H]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma[\lambda^{c}_{G^{\flat}}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma[\varepsilon](\Gamma\Omega[\gamma^{c}])}

However, thanks to the triangle identities, the functor
{\mathbf{K}}(\eta,\Gamma[\mathbb{Y}])
sends this diagram to:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma[L\circ G^{\flat}]\circ\eta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma[D(c)\circ G^{\flat}]\circ\eta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma[\lambda^{c}_{G^{\flat}}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma[\varepsilon](\Gamma\Omega[\gamma^{c}])}

To prove uniqueness of
\varphi_{\eta}
, one can consider another morphism
\psi\colon H\to\Gamma[L\circ G^{\flat}]\circ\eta
of cones, use the counit to define a morphism
\Gamma[\varepsilon](\Gamma\Omega[\psi])\colon\Gamma[\varepsilon]\circ\Gamma\Omega[H]\to\Gamma[L\circ G^{\flat}]
of cones, which by the universality much coincide with
\varphi
. Using the triangle identites, one can show that
\psi=\Gamma[\varepsilon](\Gamma\Omega[\psi])_{\eta}=\varphi_{\eta}
.
∎

Lemma 3.6
.

A pointwise pullback-preserving
2
-functor
\Gamma\colon{\mathbf{K}}\to{\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}
sends every tangentad
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
of
{\mathbf{K}}
to a tangentad
(\Gamma[\mathbb{X}],\Gamma[\mathbb{T}])
of
{\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}
whose tangent structure is obtained by applying
\Gamma
to the tangent
1
-morphism
\mathrm{T}
and to the structural
2
-morphisms of
\mathbb{T}
.

Furthermore,
\Gamma
sends a (co)lax (strong, strict) tangent
1
-morphism
(F,\alpha)
to a (co)lax (strong, strict) tangent
1
-morphism
(\Gamma[F],\Gamma[\alpha])
. Finally, a tangent
2
-morphism
\varphi\colon(F,\alpha)\Rightarrow(G,\beta)
of (co)lax tangent
1
-morphisms defines a tangent
2
-morphism
\Gamma[\varphi]
.

Proof.

Let us consider a tangentad
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
of
{\mathbf{K}}
, which corresponds to an object
\mathbb{X}
of
{\mathbf{K}}
together with a Leung functor
\mathfrak{L}[\mathbb{T}]\colon\mathsf{WEIL}_{1}\to\mathsf{END}_{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbb{X})
. A
2
-functor
\Gamma\colon{\mathbf{K}}\to{\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}
induces a strong monoidal functor
\mathsf{END}_{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbb{X})\to\mathsf{END}_{{\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}}(\Gamma[\mathbb{X}])
for every object
\mathbb{X}
of
{\mathbf{K}}
. Moreover, since
\Gamma
is pointwise pullback-preserving, the composition

\displaystyle\mathsf{WEIL}_{1}\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{L}[\mathbb{T}]}\mathsf{END}_{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbb{X})\to\mathsf{END}_{{\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}}(\Gamma[\mathbb{X}])

is a strong monoidal functor which preserves the fundamental tangent limits, namely, a Leung functor. By unwrapping this construction, one finds out that this tangentad is precisely
(\Gamma[\mathbb{X}],\Gamma[\mathbb{T}])
.

It is also not hard to see that
\mathsf{TNG}[\Gamma]
sends (co)lax (strong/strict) tangent morphisms to (co)lax (strong/strict) tangent morphisms, since the compatibilities between the distributive laws and the tangent structures are equational.

Now, consider a
2
-morphism
\varphi\colon\Gamma\Rightarrow\Gamma^{\prime}
and let
\mathsf{TNG}[\varphi]_{(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})}
to be just
\varphi_{\mathbb{X}}\colon(\Gamma[\mathbb{X}],\Gamma[\mathbb{T}])\to(\Gamma^{\prime}[\mathbb{X}],\Gamma^{\prime}[\mathbb{T}])
. Thanks to the
2
-naturality,
\varphi_{\mathbb{X}}
is a strict tangent morphism between
(\Gamma[\mathbb{X}],\Gamma[\mathbb{T}])
and
(\Gamma^{\prime}[\mathbb{X}],\Gamma^{\prime}[\mathbb{T}])
.
∎

Lemma
3.7
induces a
2
-functor

\displaystyle\mathsf{TNG}\colon\mathsf{TWOCAT}_{\mathrm{pp}}\to\mathsf{TWOCAT}

where
\mathsf{TWOCAT}
denotes the
2
-category of
2
-categories with all
2
-functors. Adopting a similar approach, one obtains three other
2
-functors

\displaystyle\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}\colon\mathsf{TWOCAT}_{\mathrm{pp}}\to\mathsf{TWOCAT}

\displaystyle\mathsf{TNG}_{\cong}\colon\mathsf{TWOCAT}_{\mathrm{pp}}\to\mathsf{TWOCAT}

\displaystyle\mathsf{TNG}_{=}\colon\mathsf{TWOCAT}_{\mathrm{pp}}\to\mathsf{TWOCAT}

which send a
2
-category
{\mathbf{K}}
to the
2
-categories
\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}({\mathbf{K}})
,
\mathsf{TNG}_{\cong}({\mathbf{K}})
, and
\mathsf{TNG}_{=}({\mathbf{K}})
, respectively.

3.2
The comonad structure of tangentads

Lemma
3.6
establishes that the operation which sends a
2
-category
{\mathbf{K}}
to the
2
-category
\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}({\mathbf{K}})
of tangentads extends to a functor. In this section, we show that not only this functor restricts to an endofunctor on the
2
-category
\mathsf{TWOCAT}_{\mathrm{pp}}
of
2
-categories and pointwise pullback-preserving
2
-functors, but it also comes equipped with a comonad structure.

For starters, let us prove a technical but useful lemma.

Lemma 3.7
.

A
2
-functor
\Gamma\colon{\mathbf{K}}\to\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}({\mathbf{K}}^{\prime})
is pointwise pullback-preserving if and only if so is the composite:

\displaystyle{\mathbf{K}}\xrightarrow{\Gamma}\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}({\mathbf{K}}^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\mathsf{U}_{\mathsf{co}}}{\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}

Proof.

First, let us suppose that
\Gamma
is pointwise pullback-preserving. By Proposition
2.21
,
\mathsf{U}_{\mathsf{co}}
admits a left
2
-adjoint
\mathsf{Inc}_{\mathsf{co}}
. Thus, by Lemma
3.5
,
\mathsf{U}_{\mathsf{co}}
preserves pointwise limits, so in particular is pointwise pullback-preserving. Since pointwise pullback-preserving
2
-functors are closed under composition,
\mathsf{U}_{\mathsf{co}}\circ\Gamma
is pointwise pullback-preserving.

Conversely, suppose that the composition
\mathsf{U}_{\mathsf{co}}\circ\Gamma
is pointwise pullback-preserving. Consider a pullback diagram
D\colon I\to\mathsf{END}_{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbb{X})
which admits a pointwise limit cone
\lambda^{c}\colon L\to D(c)
. Its image along
\mathsf{U}_{\mathsf{co}}\circ\Gamma
, namely, the diagram

\displaystyle\mathsf{U}_{\mathsf{co}}\Gamma[D]

\displaystyle\colon I\to\mathsf{END}_{{\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}}(\mathsf{U}_{\mathsf{co}}\Gamma(\mathbb{X}))

admits a pointwise limit cone

\displaystyle\mathsf{U}_{\mathsf{co}}\Gamma[\lambda^{c}]

\displaystyle\colon\mathsf{U}_{\mathsf{co}}\Gamma[L]\to\mathsf{U}_{\mathsf{co}}\Gamma[D(c)]

since
\mathsf{U}_{\mathsf{co}}\circ\Gamma
preserves pointwise pullbacks. However, by Lemma
3.1
,
\mathsf{U}_{\mathsf{co}}
reflects pointwise limits, thus the cone
\Gamma[\lambda^{c}]\colon\Gamma[L]\to\Gamma[D(c)]
must be a pointwise limit cone in
\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}({\mathbf{K}}^{\prime})
, proving that
\Gamma
is pointwise pullback-preserving.
∎

Thanks to Lemma
3.7
, we can show that
\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}(\Gamma)
is pointwise pullback-preserving provided that
\Gamma
is.

Lemma 3.8
.

If
\Gamma\colon{\mathbf{K}}\to{\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}
is a pointwise pullback-preserving
2
-functor, the
2
-functor
\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}(\Gamma)\colon\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}({\mathbf{K}})\to\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}}^{\prime})
is also pointwise pullback-preserving.

Proof.

Let us consider a tangentad
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
of
{\mathbf{K}}
, four lax tangent morphisms

\displaystyle(A,\alpha),(B,\beta),(C,\gamma),(D,\delta)\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})

and four tangent
2
-morphisms

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\colon(B,\beta)\to(A,\alpha)

\displaystyle g\colon

\displaystyle(C,\gamma)\to(A,\alpha)

\displaystyle\pi_{1}

\displaystyle\colon(D,\delta)\to(B,\beta)

\displaystyle\pi_{2}

\displaystyle\colon(D,\delta)\to(C,\gamma)

making the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(D,\delta)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(C,\gamma)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(B,\beta)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(A,\alpha)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

a pointwise pullback diagram of
\mathsf{END}_{\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. We need to prove that the
2
-functor
\mathsf{TNG}(\Gamma)
preserves such a pullback. In order to simplify notation, we decorate each term by
′
to indicate the application of
\Gamma
on it, e.g.,
A^{\prime}
indicates
\Gamma(A)
. With this notation, we need to prove that the following diagram is a pointwise pullback diagram of
\mathsf{END}_{\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}}^{\prime})}(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
, where
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})=\mathsf{TNG}(\Gamma)(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(D^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(C^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(B^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(A^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}

Consider two tangent
2
-morphisms
b\colon(K,\kappa)\to(B^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
and
c\colon(K,\kappa)\to(C^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})
for which
f^{\prime}\circ b=g^{\prime}\circ c
. Since
\Gamma
preserves pointwise pullbacks, by forgetting the tangent structure we obtain a
2
-morphism
d\colon K\to D
of
{\mathbf{K}}
which makes the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}

Therefore, the
2
-functor
\mathsf{U}_{\mathsf{co}}\circ\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}(\Gamma)
preserves pointwise pullbacks. By Lemma
3.7
, we conclude that also
\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}(\Gamma)
must preserve pointwise pullbacks.
∎

Proposition 3.9
.

The
2
-functor
\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}\colon\mathsf{TWOCAT}_{\mathrm{pp}}\to\mathsf{TWOCAT}
splits along the inclusion

\displaystyle\mathsf{TWOCAT}_{\mathrm{pp}}\to\mathsf{TWOCAT}

by defining a
2
-endofunctor
\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}\colon\mathsf{TWOCAT}_{\mathrm{pp}}\to\mathsf{TWOCAT}_{\mathrm{pp}}
.

Remark 3.10
.

Since a strong tangent morphism is also a colax tangent morphism, the splitting of Proposition
3.9
restricts to strong and strict tangent morphisms. In particular, this yields two
2
-endofunctors
\mathsf{TNG}_{\cong}
and
\mathsf{TNG}_{=}
of
\mathsf{TWOCAT}_{\mathrm{pp}}
.

In order to equip
\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}
with a comonad structure, we need two ingredients: a counit
\varepsilon\colon\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}({\mathbf{K}})\to{\mathbf{K}}
, and a comultiplication
\Delta\colon\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}({\mathbf{K}})\to\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}(\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}({\mathbf{K}}))
. To define the counit, we can use the forgetful
2
-functor
\mathsf{U}_{\mathsf{co}}\colon\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}({\mathbf{K}})\to{\mathbf{K}}
which forgets the tangent structure. To define comultiplication, we first need the following lemma.

Lemma 3.11
.

A tangentad
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
of
{\mathbf{K}}
is a tangentad in the
2
-category
\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}({\mathbf{K}})
of tangentads of
{\mathbf{K}}
. In particular, the tangent bundle
1
-morphism of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
in
\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}({\mathbf{K}})
is
(\mathrm{T},c)\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, and the structural
2
-morphisms are the same as the ones of
\mathbb{T}
.

Proof.

The proof is a straightforward, yet tedious, computation that we leave to the reader to spell out.
∎

Lemma
3.11
induces a
2
-functor:

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathsf{co}}\colon\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}({\mathbf{K}})\to\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}(\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}({\mathbf{K}}))

To understand how
\Delta_{\mathsf{co}}
is defined, first notice that an object of
\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}^{2}({\mathbf{K}})
can be equivalently described as an object
\mathbb{X}
of
{\mathbf{K}}
together with two tangent structures
\mathbb{T}
and
\mathbb{T}^{\prime}
on
\mathbb{X}
and a distributive law
\gamma\colon\mathrm{T}\circ\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\circ\mathrm{T}
, compatible with the two tangent structures. Moreover, a morphism of
\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}^{2}({\mathbf{K}})
from
(\mathbb{X}_{\circ},\mathbb{T}_{\circ},\mathbb{T}_{\circ}^{\prime},\gamma_{\circ})
to
(\mathbb{X}_{\bullet},\mathbb{T}_{\bullet},\mathbb{T}_{\bullet}^{\prime},\gamma_{\bullet})
consists of a colax tangent morphism
(F,\alpha)\colon(\mathbb{X}_{\circ},\mathbb{T}_{\circ})\nrightarrow(\mathbb{X}_{\bullet},\mathbb{T}_{\bullet})
, together with a
2
-morphism
\beta\colon\mathrm{T}_{\bullet}^{\prime}\circ F\Rightarrow F\circ\mathrm{T}_{\circ}^{\prime}
, compatible with the tangent structures. Finally, a
2
-morphism of
\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}^{2}({\mathbf{K}})
from
(F,\alpha,\beta)
to
(F^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
consists of a
2
-morphism
\varphi\colon F\Rightarrow F^{\prime}
compatible with the distributive laws
\alpha
,
\alpha^{\prime}
,
\beta
, and
\beta^{\prime}
.

Therefore, the
2
-functor
\Delta_{\mathsf{co}}
sends a tangentad
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
to:

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>\colon\!=(\mathbb{X};\mathbb{T},\mathbb{T},c)

Moreover, it sends a colax tangent morphism
(F,\alpha)
to

\displaystyle\Delta(F,\alpha)\>\colon\!=(F,\alpha,\alpha)

and a
2
-morphism
\varphi
to:

\displaystyle\Delta(\varphi)\>\colon\!=\varphi

Lemma 3.12
.

The
2
-functor
\Delta_{\mathsf{co}}\colon\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}({\mathbf{K}})\to\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}(\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}({\mathbf{K}}))
preserves pointwise pullbacks.

Proof.
