It is easy to see that the composite
(\mathsf{U}_{\mathsf{co}})_{\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}}\circ\Delta_{\mathsf{co}}\colon\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}({\mathbf{K}})\to\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}({\mathbf{K}})
is the identity
2
-functor, which trivially preserves pointwise pullbacks. Therefore, by Lemma
3.7
,
\Delta_{\mathsf{co}}
preserves pointwise pullbacks.
∎

Theorem 3.13
.

The
2
-endofunctor
\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}
on the
2
-category
\mathsf{TWOCAT}_{\mathrm{pp}}
comes equipped with a
2
-comonad structure whose counit is the forgetful
2
-functor
\mathsf{U}_{\mathsf{co}}\colon\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}({\mathbf{K}})\to{\mathbf{K}}
and whose comultiplication is the
2
-functor
\Delta_{\mathsf{co}}\colon\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}({\mathbf{K}})\to\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}(\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}({\mathbf{K}}))
.

Proof.

Lemma
3.12
proves that
\Delta_{\mathsf{co}}
is pointwise pullback-preserving. Furthermore,
\mathsf{U}_{\mathsf{co}}
is also pointwise pullback-preserving since it is a
2
-right adjoint by Proposition
2.20
and thanks to Lemma
3.5
. Let us prove that
\mathsf{U}_{\mathsf{co}}
and
\Delta_{\mathsf{co}}
define a comonad structure for
\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}
. First, notice that
(\mathsf{U}_{\mathsf{co}})_{\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}}
and
\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}(\mathsf{U}_{\mathsf{co}})
project
(\mathbb{X};\mathbb{T},\mathbb{T}^{\prime},\gamma)
to
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
and
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
, respectively. Similarly, they send a morphism
(F,\alpha;\beta)
to
(F,\alpha)
and
(F,\beta)
, respectively and a
2
-morphism
\varphi
to itself. Thus, it is not hard to see that,
\Delta_{\mathsf{co}}
composed with either
(\mathsf{U}_{\mathsf{co}})_{\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}}
or
\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}(\mathsf{U}_{\mathsf{co}})
is just the identity. Let us prove that
\Delta_{\mathsf{co}}
is coassociative. Consider a tangentad
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. Thus:

\displaystyle\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}[\Delta_{\mathsf{co}}](\Delta_{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}))=\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}[\Delta_{\mathsf{co}}](\mathbb{X};\mathbb{T},\mathbb{T},c)=(\Delta_{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T});\Delta_{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbb{T},c))

\displaystyle\quad=(\mathbb{X};\mathbb{T},\mathbb{T},c;\mathbb{T},c,c)=(\mathbb{X};\mathbb{T},\mathbb{T},\mathbb{T},c,c,c)

where we denote the generic object of
\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}^{3}({\mathbf{K}})
as
(\mathbb{X};\mathbb{T},\mathbb{T}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime},\gamma,\gamma^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime\prime})
. Conversely:

\displaystyle(\Delta_{\mathsf{co}})_{\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}}(\Delta_{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}))=(\Delta_{\mathsf{co}})_{\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}}(\mathbb{X};\mathbb{T},\mathbb{T},c)=(\mathbb{X};\mathbb{T},\mathbb{T},\mathbb{T},c,c,c)

One can prove a similar result for colax tangent morphisms and
2
-morphisms, concluding that
\Delta_{\mathsf{co}}
is coassociative.
∎

Remark 3.14
.

In
[
19
]
, Street showed that the
2
-functor
\mathsf{MND}
which sends a
2
-category
{\mathbf{K}}
to the associated
2
-category of monads of
{\mathbf{K}}
has a
2
-monad structure, whose unit sends an object
\mathbb{X}
of
{\mathbf{K}}
to the trivial monad
(\mathbb{X},\mathds{1})
on
\mathbb{X}
and whose multiplication takes a distributive law
(\mathbb{X};S,S^{\prime},\gamma)
between two monads (which is equivalent to a monad in
\mathsf{MND}({\mathbf{K}})
) and composes the two monads together by the distributive law
S^{\prime}\circ_{\gamma}S
.
\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}
, and similarly
\mathsf{TNG}_{\cong}
and
\mathsf{TNG}_{=}
, has a unit, corresponding to the
2
-functor
\mathsf{Inc}
. Moreover, the objects of
\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}^{2}({\mathbf{K}})
look similar to distributive laws: they are pairs of tangent structures together with a distributive law of tangent structures between them. So it is natural to wonder whether or not,
\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}
could come with a multiplication. However, by composing two tangent structures on an object
\mathbb{X}
with a distributive law one does not obtain a tangent structure on
\mathbb{X}
. A counterexample is offered by the composition of the tangent bundle functor
\mathrm{T}
with itself via
c
. The result is not a tangent structure on
\mathbb{X}
. Therefore,
\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}
fails to be a
2
-monad.

In future work, we would like to explore the classification of lax, colax, and pseudo coalgebras of the
2
-comonad
\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}
.

3.3
Cartesian tangentads

In
[
2
, Definition 2.8]
, Cockett and Cruttwell introduced Cartesian tangent categories as tangent categories with finite products (in particular, with a terminal object), preserved by the tangent bundle functor.

This section is devoted to extending this notion for tangentads. For starters, we recall the notion of a Cartesian object in a
2
-category. In this section, the ambient
2
-category
{\mathbf{K}}
is assumed to have a strict
2
-terminal object denoted by
\ast
and finite strict
2
-products.

Definition 3.15
.

A
Cartesian object
of
{\mathbf{K}}
is an object
\mathbb{X}
of
{\mathbf{K}}
for which the unique
1
-morphism
!\colon\mathbb{X}\to\ast
and the diagonal
1
-morphism
\Delta\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}
admit right adjoints in
{\mathbf{K}}
, denoted by

\displaystyle\mathds{1}

\displaystyle\colon\ast\to\mathbb{X}

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\colon\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}

respectively.

Cartesian objects of
{\mathbf{K}}
form a
2
-category, denoted by
\mathsf{CART}({\mathbf{K}})
, whose
1
-morphisms are
1
-morphisms
F\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
of
{\mathbf{K}}
such that, the two canonical
2
-morphisms:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ast}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{X}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\mathds{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{1}^{\prime}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\times\mathbb{X}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{X}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\times F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\times}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times^{\prime}}

are invertible. Notice that
\alpha^{\mathds{1}}\colon\mathrm{T}\circ\mathds{1}\Rightarrow\mathds{1}^{\prime}
and
\alpha^{\times}\colon F\circ\times\Rightarrow\times\circ(F\times F)
are defined as follows:

\displaystyle\alpha^{\mathds{1}}\colon F\circ\mathds{1}\xrightarrow{\eta^{\mathds{1}}F\mathds{1}}\mathds{1}\>\circ\>!\circ F\circ\mathds{1}=\mathds{1}\>\circ\>!\circ\mathds{1}\xrightarrow{\mathds{1}\varepsilon^{\mathds{1}}}\mathds{1}

\displaystyle\alpha^{\times}\colon F\circ\times\xrightarrow{\eta^{\times}F\times}\times\circ\Delta\circ F\circ\times=\times\circ(F\times F)\circ\Delta\circ\times\xrightarrow{\times(F\times F)\varepsilon^{\times}}\times\circ(F\times F)

where:

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\eta^{\mathds{1}}\colon\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}\Rightarrow\mathds{1}\>\circ\>!

\displaystyle\eta^{\times}\colon\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}\Rightarrow\times\circ\Delta

(3.2)

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{\mathds{1}}\colon!\circ\mathds{1}\Rightarrow\mathsf{id}_{\mathds{1}}

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{\times}\colon\Delta\circ\times\Rightarrow\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}}

are the units and the counits of the adjunctions
\mathds{1}\leftrightarrows\mathbb{X}\colon!
and
\times\colon\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}\leftrightarrows\mathbb{X}\colon\Delta
.

Furthermore,
2
-morphisms
\varphi\colon F\Rightarrow G\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
of Cartesian objects of
{\mathbf{K}}
are
2
-morphisms of
{\mathbf{K}}
that commute with the
2
-isomorphisms
\alpha^{\mathds{1}}
and
\alpha^{\times}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F\circ\mathds{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathds{1}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G\circ\mathds{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi\mathds{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F\circ\times}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\times^{\prime}\circ(F\times F)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G\circ\times}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\times^{\prime}\circ(G\times G)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\times}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi\times}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times^{\prime}(\varphi\times\varphi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\times}}

Proposition 3.16
.

A Cartesian tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is equivalent to a tangentad in the
2
-category of Cartesian objects
\mathsf{CART}({\mathbf{K}})
of
{\mathbf{K}}
.

Proposition 3.17
.

If
{\mathbf{K}}
has finite
2
-products and a
2
-terminal object, so does the
2
-category
\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})
. Moreover, under these conditions, the
2
-category
\mathsf{CART}(\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}}))
of Cartesian objects in the
2
-category of tangentads of
{\mathbf{K}}
is isomorphic to the
2
-category
\mathsf{TNG}(\mathsf{CART}({\mathbf{K}}))
of tangentads in the
2
-category of Cartesian objects of
{\mathbf{K}}
.

Proof.

First, it is easy to show that, if
{\mathbf{K}}
has a terminal object
\ast
, then
\ast\>\colon\!=(\ast,\mathds{1})
, the trivial tangentad on the terminal object
\ast
of
{\mathbf{K}}
, is terminal in
\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})
, and that if
{\mathbf{K}}
has finite products, then
(\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
defines the Cartesian product between
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
and
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
in
\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})
, where the tangent bundle functor of
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T}^{\prime}
is
\mathrm{T}\times\mathrm{T}^{\prime}
and the structural
2
-morphisms are defined in a similar manner.

Let us consider a tangentad
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
in the
2
-category
\mathsf{CART}({\mathbf{K}})
of Cartesian objects of
{\mathbf{K}}
and let us show that
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is a Cartesian object in
\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})
. The tangent bundle
1
-morphism
\mathrm{T}\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}
preserves the Cartesian structure of
\mathbb{X}
. In particular, the
2
-morphisms
\alpha^{\mathds{1}}\colon\mathrm{T}\circ\ast\Rightarrow\ast
and
\alpha^{\times}\colon\mathrm{T}\circ\times\Rightarrow\times\circ(\mathrm{T}\times\mathrm{T})
are invertible. This allows us to define the following two strong tangent morphisms:

\displaystyle\ast\xrightarrow{(\mathds{1},{\alpha^{\mathds{1}}}^{-1})}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})

\displaystyle(\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T})\xrightarrow{(\times,{\alpha^{\times}}^{-1})}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})

The compatibility between the distributive laws and the structural
2
-morphisms of the tangent structures are a direct consequence of the structural
2
-morphisms being
2
-morphisms in
\mathsf{CART}({\mathbf{K}})
. Moreover, by using the triangle identities, it is easy to show that the units and the counits of Equation (
3.2
) are compatible with
{\alpha^{\mathds{1}}}^{-1}
and
{\alpha^{\times}}^{-1}
. This shows that:

\displaystyle(\mathds{1},{\alpha^{\mathds{1}}}^{-1})\colon\ast\leftrightarrows(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\colon!

\displaystyle(\times,{\alpha^{\times}}^{-1})\colon(\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T})\leftrightarrows(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\colon\Delta

form two adjunctions in
\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})
. Therefore,
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is a Cartesian object in
\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})
.

Conversely, consider a Cartesian object of
\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})
. This consists of a tangentad
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
of
{\mathbf{K}}
for which the
1
-morphisms
\alpha^{\mathds{1}}\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to\ast
and
\Delta\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T})
admit right adjoints
(\ast,\kappa)\colon\ast\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
and
(\times,\beta)\colon(\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
in
\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})
, respectively. Since the forgetful
2
-functor
\mathsf{U}\colon\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})\to{\mathbf{K}}
preserves adjunctions,
\mathbb{X}
is a Cartesian object in
{\mathbf{K}}
. The goal is to show that the two lax distributive laws:

\displaystyle\kappa\colon\mathds{1}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}\circ\mathds{1}

\displaystyle\beta\colon\times\circ(\mathrm{T}\times\mathrm{T})\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}\circ\times

invert
\alpha^{\mathds{1}}
and
\alpha^{\times}
, respectively, which are induced by the Cartesian sructure of
\mathbb{X}
.

Let us start with
\kappa
. First, notice that the unit
\eta^{\mathds{1}}
of the adjunction
(\mathds{1},\kappa)\dashv\>!
is compatible with
\kappa
, since the adjunction is in
\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathds{1}\>\circ\>!\circ\mathrm{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathds{1}\>\circ\>!}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}\circ\mathds{1}\>\circ\>!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta\mathrm{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa!}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}\eta^{\mathds{1}}}

By the universal property of the terminal object
\ast
of
{\mathbf{K}}
,
\kappa\alpha^{\mathds{1}}\colon\mathds{1}\Rightarrow\mathds{1}
is the identity. Let us show that also
\alpha^{\times}\kappa\colon\mathrm{T}\circ\mathds{1}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}\circ\mathds{1}
is the identity:

\displaystyle\quad\kappa\circ\>!

\displaystyle(!=\varepsilon^{\mathds{1}}\>\circ\mathds{1}\>\circ\eta_{\mathrm{T}\mathds{1}}^{\mathds{1}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\quad\kappa\circ\varepsilon^{\mathds{1}}\>\circ\mathds{1}\>\circ\eta_{\mathrm{T}\mathds{1}}^{\mathds{1}}

\displaystyle(\kappa\circ\varepsilon^{\mathds{1}}\>\circ\mathds{1}=\varepsilon^{\mathds{1}}\>\circ\mathrm{T}\mathds{1}\>\circ\kappa_{!\mathds{1}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\quad\varepsilon^{\mathds{1}}\>\circ\mathrm{T}\mathds{1}\>\circ\kappa_{!\mathds{1}}\circ\eta^{\mathds{1}}_{\mathrm{T}\mathds{1}}

\displaystyle(!\circ\mathds{1}=!\circ\mathrm{T}\mathds{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\quad\varepsilon^{\mathds{1}}\>\circ\mathrm{T}\mathds{1}\>\circ\kappa_{!\mathrm{T}\mathds{1}}\circ\eta^{\mathds{1}}_{\mathrm{T}\mathds{1}}

\displaystyle(\kappa_{!}\circ\eta^{\mathds{1}}_{\mathrm{T}}=\mathrm{T}\eta^{\mathds{1}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\quad\varepsilon^{\mathds{1}}\>\circ\mathrm{T}\mathds{1}\>\circ\mathrm{T}\eta^{\mathds{1}}_{\mathds{1}}

\displaystyle(\text{triangle identity})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\quad\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}\mathds{1}}

Let us now prove that
\beta
inverts
\alpha^{\times}
. Let us start by noticing that the unit
\eta^{\times}
and the counit
\varepsilon^{\times}
of the adjunction
\times\dashv\Delta
are compatible with
\beta
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\times\circ\Delta\circ\mathrm{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\times\circ(\mathrm{T}\times\mathrm{T})\circ\Delta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}\circ\Delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{\times}\mathrm{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}\eta^{\times}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta\circ\times\circ(\mathrm{T}\times\mathrm{T})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}\times\mathrm{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta\circ\mathrm{T}\circ\times}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathrm{T}\times\mathrm{T})\circ\Delta\circ\times}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon(\mathrm{T}\times\mathrm{T})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{T}\times\mathrm{T})\varepsilon}

Thus:

\displaystyle\quad\alpha^{\times}\circ\beta

\displaystyle(\alpha^{\times}=(\times(\mathrm{T}\times\mathrm{T})\varepsilon^{\times})\circ\eta^{\times}_{\mathrm{T}\times})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\quad(\times(\mathrm{T}\times\mathrm{T})\varepsilon^{\times})\circ\eta^{\times}_{\mathrm{T}\times}\circ\beta

\displaystyle(\eta^{\times}_{\mathrm{T}\times}\circ\beta=\Delta\beta\circ\times\circ\eta^{\times}_{\times(\mathrm{T}\times\mathrm{T})})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\quad(\times(\mathrm{T}\times\mathrm{T})\varepsilon^{\times})\circ\Delta\beta\circ\times\circ\eta^{\times}_{\times(\mathrm{T}\times\mathrm{T})}

\displaystyle(((\mathrm{T}\times\mathrm{T})\times\varepsilon^{\times})\circ\Delta\beta=\varepsilon^{\times}_{\mathrm{T}\times\mathrm{T}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\quad((\mathrm{T}\times\mathrm{T})\times\varepsilon^{\times})\circ\eta^{\times}_{\times(\mathrm{T}\times\mathrm{T})}

\displaystyle(\text{triangle identity})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\quad\mathsf{id}_{\times(\mathrm{T}\times\mathrm{T})}

Moreover:

\displaystyle\quad\beta\circ\alpha^{\times}

\displaystyle(\alpha^{\times}=\varepsilon^{\times}\circ(\mathrm{T}\times\mathrm{T})\circ\times\circ\eta^{\times}_{\mathrm{T}\times})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\quad\beta\circ\varepsilon^{\times}\circ(\mathrm{T}\times\mathrm{T})\circ\times\circ\eta^{\times}_{\mathrm{T}\times}

\displaystyle(\beta\circ\varepsilon^{\times}\circ(\mathrm{T}\times\mathrm{T})\circ\times=(\mathrm{T}\times\varepsilon^{\times})\circ\beta_{\Delta\times})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\quad(\mathrm{T}\times\varepsilon^{\times})\circ\beta_{\Delta\times}\circ\eta^{\times}_{\mathrm{T}\times}

\displaystyle\beta_{\Delta}\circ\eta^{\times}_{\mathrm{T}}=\mathrm{T}\eta^{\times}_{\times})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\quad(\mathrm{T}\times\varepsilon^{\times})\circ\mathrm{T}\eta^{\times}_{\times}

\displaystyle(\text{triangle identity})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\quad\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}\times}

This proves that
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is a tangentad in
\mathsf{CART}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. This correspondence extends to a
2
-isomorphism between the
2
-categories
\mathsf{CART}(\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}}))
and
\mathsf{TNG}(\mathsf{CART}({\mathbf{K}}))
.
∎

Propositions
3.16
and
3.17
suggest defining a Cartesian tangentad as follows.

Definition 3.18
.

A
Cartesian tangentad
in
{\mathbf{K}}
is a Cartesian object of the
2
-category
\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})
of tangentads of
{\mathbf{K}}
, or equivalently, a tangentad of the
2
-category
\mathsf{CART}({\mathbf{K}})
of Cartesian objects of
{\mathbf{K}}
.

3.4
Adjunctable and representable tangentads

[
2
, Proposition 5.17]
states that the opposite category of a tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
comes equipped with a “
dual
” tangent structure provided the tangent bundle functor
\mathrm{T}
and each functor
\mathrm{T}_{n}
admit left adjoints,
\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}
, and
\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}_{n}
, respectively.

In this section, we formalize this construction for tangentads in a
2
-category which admits a suitable notion of “
opposite
” objects. Let us start by introducing this concept.

Definition 3.19
.

A
2
-category with
opposite objects
comprises a
2
-category
{\mathbf{K}}
, a
2
-equivalence
(-)^{\mathsf{op}}\colon{\mathbf{K}}^{\mathsf{co}}\to{\mathbf{K}}
from the
2
-cocategory of
{\mathbf{K}}
, namely, with inverted
2
-morphisms, to
{\mathbf{K}}
, and a
2
-isomorphism:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{K}}^{\mathsf{co}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{K}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{K}}^{\mathsf{co}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)^{\mathsf{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((-)^{\mathsf{op}})^{\mathsf{co}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}

Example 3.20
.

The
2
-category
\mathsf{CAT}
of categories admits opposite objects once equipped with the
2
-functor
(-)^{\mathsf{op}}
which sends each category to its opposite, where
((-)^{\mathsf{op}})^{\mathsf{co}}\circ(-)^{\mathsf{op}}=\mathsf{id}_{\mathsf{CAT}}
.

Example 3.21
.

Let
{\mathbf{K}}
be a
2
-category with opposite objects
(-)^{\mathsf{op}}
, and consider the full
2
-subcategory
\mathsf{LMND}({\mathbf{K}})
of the
2
-category
\mathsf{MND}({\mathbf{K}})
of monads of
{\mathbf{K}}
spanned by those monads
(\mathbb{X},S)
of
{\mathbf{K}}
whose underlying endomorphism
S\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}
admits a right adjoint
S^{\bullet}\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}
. Let
\eta\colon\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}\Rightarrow S^{\bullet}\circ S
and
\varepsilon\colon S\circ S^{\bullet}\Rightarrow\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}
denote the unit and the counit of the adjunction, respectively. Using mates, one can define a comonad structure on
S^{\bullet}
as follows:

\displaystyle 1^{\bullet}\colon S^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{1S^{\bullet}}S\circ S^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}

\displaystyle\mu^{\bullet}\colon S^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\eta S^{\bullet}}S^{\bullet}\circ S\circ S^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{S^{\bullet}\eta SS^{\bullet}}S^{\bullet}\circ S^{\bullet}\circ S\circ S\circ S^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{S^{\bullet}S^{\bullet}\mu S^{\bullet}}S^{\bullet}\circ S^{\bullet}\circ S\circ S^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{S^{\bullet}S^{\bullet}\varepsilon}S^{\bullet}\circ S^{\bullet}

where
1\colon\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}\Rightarrow S
and
\mu\colon S^{2}\Rightarrow S
denote the unit and the multiplication of
S
, respectively. Since
{\mathbf{K}}
admits opposite objects,
(-)^{\mathsf{op}}
sends
\mathbb{X}
to
\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{op}}
,
S^{\bullet}
to
(S^{\bullet})^{\mathsf{op}}\colon\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{op}}\to\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{op}}
, and the comonad of
S^{\bullet}
to a monad structure:

\displaystyle(1^{\bullet})^{\mathsf{op}}\colon\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{op}}}\Rightarrow(S^{\bullet})^{\mathsf{op}}

\displaystyle(\mu^{\bullet})^{\mathsf{op}}\colon(S^{\bullet})^{\mathsf{op}}\circ(S^{\bullet})^{\mathsf{op}}\to(S^{\bullet})^{\mathsf{op}}

on
(S^{\bullet})^{\bullet}
. Furthermore,
(-)^{\mathsf{op}}
sends adjunctions to (co)adjunctions, therefore,
S^{\mathsf{op}}
becomes a right adjoint to
(S^{\bullet})^{\mathsf{op}}
, where the unit and the counit of the adjunction are given respectively by
\varepsilon^{\mathsf{op}}
and
\eta^{\mathsf{op}}
(notice that the unit becomes the counit and vice versa). Let us define
(\mathbb{X};S,1,\mu)^{\mathsf{op}}\>\colon\!=(\mathbb{X}^{\bullet};(S^{\bullet})^{\mathsf{op}},(1^{\bullet})^{\mathsf{op}},(\mu^{\bullet})^{\mathsf{op}})
.

Given a morphism
(F,\alpha)\colon(\mathbb{X},S)\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},S^{\prime})
of monads, let the underlying
{\mathbf{K}}

1
-morphism of the corresponding morphism of monads
(F,\alpha)^{\mathsf{op}}\colon(\mathbb{X},S)^{\mathsf{op}}\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},S^{\prime})^{\mathsf{op}}
be
F^{\mathsf{op}}
and the corresponding distributive law to be
(\alpha^{\bullet})^{\mathsf{op}}\colon(S^{\bullet})^{\mathsf{op}}\circ F^{\mathsf{op}}\Rightarrow F^{\mathsf{op}}\circ(S^{\bullet})^{\mathsf{op}}
, where
\alpha^{\bullet}
is the mate of
\alpha\colon S\circ F\Rightarrow F\circ S
along the adjunction:

\displaystyle\alpha^{\bullet}\colon F\circ S^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\eta FS^{\bullet}}S^{\bullet}\circ S\circ F\circ S^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{S^{\bullet}\alpha S^{\bullet}}S^{\bullet}\circ F\circ S\circ S^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{S^{\bullet}\circ F\varepsilon}S^{\bullet}\circ F

Since the mate of a pasting diagram is the pasting diagram of the mates of each subdiagram provided the mate of each of them is well-defined (cf.
[
13
]
),
(F,\alpha^{\bullet})\colon(\mathbb{X},S^{\bullet})\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},{S^{\prime}}^{\bullet})
becomes a morphism of comonads and therefore,
(F^{\mathsf{op}},(\alpha)^{\mathsf{op}})
is a morphism of monads. Finally, if
\varphi\colon(F,\alpha)\Rightarrow(G,\beta)
is a
2
-morphism of monads, one can show that
\varphi
is also a morphism of comonads
\varphi\colon(F,\alpha^{\bullet})\Rightarrow(G,\beta^{\bullet})\colon(\mathbb{X},S^{\bullet})\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},{S^{\prime}}^{\bullet})
. Thus,
\varphi^{\mathsf{op}}\colon(F,\alpha)^{\mathsf{op}}\Rightarrow(G,\beta)^{\mathsf{op}}\colon(\mathbb{X},S)^{\mathsf{op}}\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},S^{\prime})^{\mathsf{op}}
becomes a morphism of monads.

Finally, we need to provide a
2
-isomorphism
\xi\colon\mathsf{id}_{{\mathbf{K}}^{\mathsf{co}}}\Rightarrow((-)^{\mathsf{op}})^{\mathsf{co}})\circ(-)^{\mathsf{op}}
. However,
(\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{op}},{S^{\bullet}}^{\mathsf{op}})^{\mathsf{op}}
is given by
(\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{op}})^{\mathsf{op}}
and
(((S^{\bullet})^{\mathsf{op}})^{\bullet})^{\mathsf{op}}
. However, since mates are preserved by
2
-functors, the monad becomes
(((S^{\bullet})^{\bullet})^{\mathsf{op}})^{\mathsf{op}}
. By using triangle identities, one can show that
(S^{\bullet})^{\bullet}
is the original monad
S
. Therefore,
\mathsf{LMND}({\mathbf{K}})
admits opposite objects provided that
{\mathbf{K}}
does.

Notice that, the
2
-subcategory
\mathsf{RMND}({\mathbf{K}})
of
\mathsf{MND}({\mathbf{K}})
of monads which admit a left adjoint does not admit opposite objects, since the distributive law of the
1
-morphisms does not admit mate. One might hope to consider the
2
-category
\mathsf{RMND}_{\cong}({\mathbf{K}})
of monads of
{\mathbf{K}}
admitting a left adjoint, whose
1
-morphisms are strong morphisms of monads, namely, with invertible distributive law, and whose
2
-morphisms are just usual
2
-morphisms of monads. However, the mate of the distributive law fails to be invertible, since in general, the inverse does not admit any mate.

Example 3.22
.

The
2
-category
\mathsf{INDX}(\mathbb{X})
of indexed categories over a fixed indexing category
\mathbb{X}
admits opposite objects. Concretely, the opposite of an indexed category
I\colon\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{op}}\to\mathsf{CAT}
which sends each
A
of
\mathbb{X}
to
\mathbb{X}_{A}
and each
f\colon A\to B
to
f^{\ast}\colon\mathbb{X}_{B}\to\mathbb{X}_{A}
can be defined as the indexed category
I^{\bullet}\colon\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{op}}\to\mathsf{CAT}
which sends
A
to
\mathbb{X}_{A}^{\mathsf{op}}
and
f\colon A\to B
to
(f^{\ast})^{\mathsf{op}}\colon\mathbb{X}_{B}^{\mathsf{op}}\to\mathbb{X}_{A}^{\mathsf{op}}
. Similarly, the opposite of an indexed functor
F\colon I\to I^{\prime}
which sends each
A
to a functor
F_{A}\colon\mathbb{X}_{A}\to\mathbb{X}_{A}^{\prime}
is the indexed functor
F^{\bullet}\colon I^{\bullet}\to{I^{\prime}}^{\bullet}
which sends each
A
to
F_{A}^{\mathsf{op}}\mathbb{X}_{A}^{\mathsf{op}}\to{\mathbb{X}_{A}^{\prime}}^{\mathsf{op}}
. Finally, the opposite of an indexed natural transformation
\varphi\colon F\Rightarrow G\colon I\to I^{\prime}
which sends
A
to a natural transformation
\varphi_{A}\colon F_{A}\Rightarrow G_{A}\colon\mathbb{X}_{A}\to\mathbb{X}_{A}^{\prime}
is the indexed natural transformation
\varphi^{\bullet}\colon F^{\bullet}\Rightarrow G^{\bullet}\colon I^{\bullet}\to{I^{\prime}}^{\bullet}
which sends
A
to
\varphi^{\mathsf{op}}_{A}\colon F_{A}^{\mathsf{op}}\leftarrow G_{A}^{\mathsf{op}}
.

Example 3.23
.

Thanks to the Grothendieck
2
-equivalence
\mathsf{INDX}(\mathbb{X})\simeq\mathsf{FIB}(\mathbb{X})
, the construction of Example
3.22
induces opposite objects on the
2
-category
\mathsf{FIB}(\mathbb{X})
of cloven fibrations over the fixed base category
\mathbb{X}
. In particular, if
\Pi\colon\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{X}
is a fibration over
\mathbb{X}
, the fibres of
\Pi^{\bullet}
are the opposite categories of the fibres of
\Pi
. A more general construction of the
dual
of a (non-necessarily cloven) fibration is described in
[
12
]
.

Definition 3.24
.

A tangent structure
\mathbb{T}
on an object
\mathbb{X}
of a
2
-category
{\mathbf{K}}
is
adjunctable
provided that the tangent bundle
1
-morphism
\mathrm{T}
and each
1
-morphism
\mathrm{T}_{n}
admit left adjoints
\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}
and
\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}_{n}
in the
2
-category
{\mathbf{K}}
, respectively. By extension, a tangentad
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is adjunctable provided its tangent structure is.

Definition 3.25
.

An
op-tangent structure
on an object
\mathbb{X}
of a
2
-category
{\mathbf{K}}
is a tangent structure on
\mathbb{X}
in the
2
-cocategory
{\mathbf{K}}^{\mathsf{co}}
of
{\mathbf{K}}
. An op-tangent structure
admits conegatives
if the corresponding tangent structure on
{\mathbf{K}}^{\mathsf{co}}
admits negatives.

Proposition 3.26
.

Let
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
be an adjunctable tangentad, where

\displaystyle\eta_{n}

\displaystyle\colon\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}_{n}\circ\mathrm{T}_{n}^{\bullet}

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{n}

\displaystyle\colon\mathrm{T}_{n}^{\bullet}\circ\mathrm{T}_{n}\Rightarrow\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}

denote the units and the counits of the adjunctions
\mathrm{T}_{n}^{\bullet}\dashv\mathrm{T}_{n}
(
\eta\>\colon\!=\eta_{1}
and
\varepsilon\>\colon\!=\varepsilon_{1}
), respectively. Thus,
\mathbb{X}
carries the structure of an op-tangentad
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}^{\bullet})
so defined:

​
[
Tangent bundle
1
-morphism
]
:

The tangent bundle
1
-morphism of
\mathbb{T}^{\bullet}
is the left adjoint
\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}
to
\mathrm{T}
;

​
[
Coprojection
]
:

The coprojection
p^{\bullet}\colon\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}
is the mate of the projection
p\colon\mathrm{T}\Rightarrow\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}
:

\displaystyle p^{\bullet}\colon\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}\xrightarrow{\eta}\mathrm{T}\circ\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{p\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}}\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}

​
[
Cozero morphism
]
:

The cozero morphism
z^{\bullet}\colon\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\Rightarrow\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}
is the mate of the zero morphism
z\colon\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}
:

\displaystyle z^{\bullet}\colon\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}z}\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\circ\mathrm{T}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}

​
[
Cosum morphism
]
:

The cosum morphism
s^{\bullet}\colon\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}_{2}^{\bullet}
is the mate of the sum morphism
s\colon\mathrm{T}_{2}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}
:

\displaystyle s^{\bullet}\colon\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\eta_{2}}\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\circ\mathrm{T}_{2}\circ\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}_{2}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}s\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}_{2}}\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\circ\mathrm{T}\circ\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}_{2}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}_{2}}\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}_{2}

​
[
Vertical colift
]
:

The vertical colift
l^{\bullet}\colon{\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}}^{2}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}
is the mate of the vertical lift
l\colon\mathrm{T}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{2}
:

\displaystyle l^{\bullet}\colon\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\circ\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\eta}\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\circ\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\circ\mathrm{T}\circ\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}l\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}}\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\circ\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\circ\mathrm{T}\circ\mathrm{T}\circ\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\varepsilon\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}}\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\circ\mathrm{T}\circ\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}}\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}

​
[
Canonical coflip
]
:

The canonical coflip
c^{\bullet}\colon{\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}}^{2}\Rightarrow{\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}}^{2}
is the mate of the canonical flip
c\colon\mathrm{T}^{2}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{2}
:

\displaystyle c^{\bullet}\colon\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\circ\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\eta}\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\circ\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\circ\mathrm{T}\circ\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\mathrm{T}\eta\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}}\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\circ\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\circ\mathrm{T}\circ\mathrm{T}\circ\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\circ\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\to

\displaystyle\qquad\xrightarrow{\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}c\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}}\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\circ\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\circ\mathrm{T}\circ\mathrm{T}\circ\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\circ\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\varepsilon\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}}\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\circ\mathrm{T}\circ\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\circ\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}}\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\circ\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}

Furthermore, if
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
has negatives,
\mathbb{T}^{\bullet}
admits conegatives:

​
[
Conegation
]
:

The conegation
n^{\bullet}\colon\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}
is the mate of the negation
n\colon\mathrm{T}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}
:

\displaystyle n^{\bullet}\colon\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\eta}\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\circ\mathrm{T}\circ\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}n\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}}\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\circ\mathrm{T}\circ\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}}\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}

Proof.

The proof of this result is a straightforward generalization of that of
[
2
, Proposition 5.17]
. In particular, since mates preserve pasting diagrams, all equational axioms hold. To prove that
\mathrm{T}_{n}^{\bullet}
is the
n
-fold pushout of
p^{\bullet}
of itself, consider
n

2
-morphisms
f_{1},\dots,f_{n}\colon\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\to H
such that
f_{i}\circ p^{\bullet}=f_{j}\circ p^{\bullet}
, for each
i,j=1,\dots,n
. Let us define:

\displaystyle f_{k}^{\bullet}\colon\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}\xrightarrow{\eta}\mathrm{T}\circ\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{T}f_{k}}\mathrm{T}\circ H

Since mates preserve pasting diagrams:

\displaystyle p\circ f_{i}^{\bullet}=(p^{\bullet}\circ f_{i})^{\bullet}=(p^{\bullet}\circ f_{j})^{\bullet}=p\circ f_{j}^{\bullet}

Thus, we can define the unique morphism
\langle f_{1}^{\bullet},\dots,f_{n}^{\bullet}\rangle\colon\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}\to\mathrm{T}_{n}\circ H
. It is not hard to prove that the mate of the morphism

\displaystyle[f_{1},\dots,f_{n}]\colon\mathrm{T}_{n}^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{T}_{n}^{\bullet}\langle f_{1}^{\bullet},\dots,f_{n}^{\bullet}\rangle}\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}_{n}\circ\mathrm{T}_{n}\circ H\xrightarrow{\varepsilon_{n}H}H

is the unique morphism, for which
[f_{1},\dots,f_{n}]\circ\pi_{k}^{\bullet}=f_{k}
, where
\pi_{k}^{\bullet}\colon\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}\to\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}_{n}
is the mate of
\pi_{k}\colon\mathrm{T}_{n}\to\mathrm{T}
. Similarly, the universality of the vertical lift is preserved by mates.
∎

When a
2
-category admits opposite objects, the op-tangent structure
\mathbb{T}^{\bullet}
of Proposition
3.26
of an adjunctable tangentad
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
becomes a tangent structure on the opposite object of
\mathbb{X}
. To see this, recall that a tangentad
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
can be defined by a Leung functor:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}[\mathbb{T}]\colon\mathsf{WEIL}_{1}\to\mathsf{END}_{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbb{X})={\mathbf{K}}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{X})

Similarly, an op-tangentad
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}^{\bullet})
can be regarded as a Leung functor:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}[\mathbb{T}^{\bullet}]\colon\mathsf{WEIL}_{1}\to\mathsf{END}_{{\mathbf{K}}^{\mathsf{co}}}(\mathbb{X})={\mathbf{K}}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{X})

When
{\mathbf{K}}
admits opposite objects, we have an equivalence of monoidal categories
{\mathbf{K}}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{X})\simeq{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{op}},\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{op}})
. Thus, every op-tangentad on
\mathbb{X}
defines a tangentad on
\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{op}}
whose Leung functor is given by:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}[\mathbb{T}]\colon\mathsf{WEIL}_{1}\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{L}[\mathbb{T}^{\bullet}]}\mathsf{END}_{{\mathbf{K}}^{\mathsf{co}}}(\mathbb{X})={\mathbf{K}}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{X})\simeq{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{op}},\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{op}})=\mathsf{END}_{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{op}})

Lemma 3.27
.

If
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}^{\bullet})
is an op-tangentad of
{\mathbf{K}}
and
{\mathbf{K}}
admits opposite objects, then
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
defines a tangentad, where:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}[\mathbb{T}]\colon\mathsf{WEIL}_{1}\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{L}[\mathbb{T}^{\bullet}]}\mathsf{END}_{{\mathbf{K}}^{\mathsf{co}}}(\mathbb{X})={\mathbf{K}}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{X})\simeq{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{op}},\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{op}})=\mathsf{END}_{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbb{X})

Proof.

Since
(-)^{\mathsf{op}}
is an equivalence, the induced functor
{\mathbf{K}}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{X})\to{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{op}},\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{op}})
is a strong monoidal functor and preserves pointwise limits; thus,
\mathfrak{L}[\mathbb{T}]
is a Leung functor.
∎

Theorem 3.28
.

If the
2
-category
{\mathbf{K}}
admits opposite objects, so does the full
2
-subcategory
\mathsf{ADJTNG}({\mathbf{K}})
of
\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})
of adjunctable tangentads, where the opposite of an adjunctable tangentad
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is defined by
(\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{op}},(\mathbb{T}^{\bullet})^{\mathsf{op}})
and
\mathbb{T}^{\bullet}
is the op-tangent structure of Proposition
3.26
.

Proof.

Since
(-)^{\mathsf{op}}
sends adjunctions to (co)adjunctions, if
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is adjunctable, so is
(\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{op}},(\mathbb{T}^{\bullet})^{\mathsf{op}})
. Moreover, by employing the triangle identities of the unit and the counit, one can show that
(\mathbb{T}^{\bullet})^{\bullet}
coincides with
\mathbb{T}
. Thus,
((\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})^{\mathsf{op}})^{\mathsf{op}}\cong(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. Given a lax morphism of adjunctable tangentads
(F,\alpha)\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
, by taking the mate
\alpha^{\bullet}
of the distributive law
\alpha\colon F\circ\mathrm{T}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\circ F
, one defines a colax op-tangent morphism
(F,\alpha^{\bullet})\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}^{\bullet})\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},{\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}^{\bullet})
between the corresponding op-tangent morphisms. In particular,
\alpha^{\bullet}
is of type
{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}}^{\bullet}\circ F\Rightarrow F\circ\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}
. By taking the opposite, one obtains a lax tangent morphism:

\displaystyle(F^{\mathsf{op}},(\alpha^{\bullet})^{\mathsf{op}})\colon(\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{op}},(\mathbb{T}^{\bullet})^{\mathsf{op}})\to({\mathbb{X}^{\prime}}^{\mathsf{op}},({\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}^{\bullet})^{\mathsf{op}})

Finally, a
2
-morphism
\varphi\colon(F,\alpha)\Rightarrow(G,\beta)
of tangentads defines a
2
-morphism of tangentads
\varphi^{\mathsf{op}}\colon(F^{\mathsf{op}},(\alpha^{\bullet})^{\mathsf{op}})\Rightarrow(G^{\mathsf{op}},(\beta^{\bullet})^{\mathsf{op}})
.
∎

An important class of adjunctable tangent categories is the one of
representable
tangent categories, whose tangent bundle functor is a representable functor. Here, we extend this notion to Cartesian tangentads.

Definition 3.29
.

A
representable tangentad
of a Cartesian
2
-category
{\mathbf{K}}
is a Cartesian tangentad
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
of
{\mathbf{K}}
equipped with a sequence of
1
-morphisms
D_{n}\colon\ast\to\mathbb{X}
of
{\mathbf{K}}
from the terminal object
\ast
of
{\mathbf{K}}
to
\mathbb{X}
such that, for every positive integer
n
, the
1
-morphism:

\displaystyle D_{n}\times-\colon\mathbb{X}\cong\ast\times\mathbb{X}\xrightarrow{D_{n}\times\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}}\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}\xrightarrow{\times}\mathbb{X}

is a left adjoint to the
1
-morphism
\mathrm{T}_{n}\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}
.

Corollary 3.30
.

When
{\mathbf{K}}
is a Cartesian
2
-category with opposite objects, the opposite of a representable tangentad is an adjunctable tangentad.

Let
\mathrm{El}(\mathbb{X})
denote the category of global elements of
\mathbb{X}
, namely, the category whose objects are
1
-morphisms
a\colon\ast\to\mathbb{X}
and morphisms are
2
-morphisms
a\Rightarrow b\colon\ast\to\mathbb{X}
.

Definition 3.31
.

A
microscopic element
of a Cartesian object
\mathbb{X}
of a Cartesian
2
-category
{\mathbf{K}}
consists of an element
D\colon\ast\to\mathbb{X}
together with the following data:

​
[
Coprojection
]
:

A coprojection
\zeta\colon\mathds{1}\to D
, where
\mathds{1}\colon\ast\to\mathbb{X}
denotes the terminal element of
\mathbb{X}
, and for which the
n
-fold pointwise pushout
D_{n}\colon\mathds{1}\to\mathbb{X}
of
\zeta
along itself is well-defined in the category
\mathrm{El}(\mathbb{X})
of elements of
\mathbb{X}
and preserved by each functor
D^{n}\times-
, with
D^{n}\>\colon\!=D\times\dots\times D\colon\mathds{1}\to\mathbb{X}
;

​
[
Cosum morphism
]
:

A cosum
\delta\colon D\to D_{2}
, which makes
\zeta
a cocommutative comonoid of the coslice category
\mathds{1}/\mathrm{El}(\mathbb{X})
;

​
[
Multiplication
]
:

A multiplication
\mu\colon D\times D\to D
, which is coassociative, cocommutative, compatible with the coprojection, coadditive with respect to the cosum, and which satisfies the following condition:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D\times D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle!\zeta,\mathsf{id}_{D}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathsf{id}_{D},!\zeta\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}

Furthermore, the multiplication is universal, namely, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D\times D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathsf{id}_{D},\zeta\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta\iota_{1}}

is a pushout diagram, where:

\displaystyle\xi\colon D\times D\xrightarrow{\mathsf{id}_{D}\times\delta}D\times D_{2}\xrightarrow{[\mu\iota_{1},\pi_{1}\iota_{2}]}D_{2}

A microscopic object
has negatives
when it comes equipped with an extra morphism:

​
[
Conegation
]
:

\kappa\colon D\to D
such that:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[\mathsf{id}_{D},\kappa]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}

An
infinitesimal element
(with negatives) of
\mathbb{X}
is a microscopic element
D
(with negatives) of
\mathbb{X}
, such that for every positive integer
n
,
D_{n}\times-
admits a left adjoint.

As proved in
[
2
, Proposition 5.7]
, representable tangent structures are equivalent to infinitesimal objects in the underlying category. We can extend this result for tangentads.

Proposition 3.32
.

If
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is a representable tangentad (with negatives) and
D\colon\ast\to\mathbb{X}
represents the tangent bundle morphism,
D
comes equipped with the structure of an infinitesimal element (with negatives) of
\mathbb{X}
. Moreover, if
D
is an infinitesimal element of
\mathbb{X}
(with negatives), then the left adjoint of
D\times-
defines a representable tangent structure on
\mathbb{X}
.

Proof.

If
\mathbb{T}
is representable,
\mathrm{T}
is exponentiable; thus, the structural morphisms of
\mathbb{T}
contravariantly translate into structural morphisms on
D
, which equip
D
with an infinitesimal structure.

Conversely, one can see that
D\times-
together with the structure maps listed above define an op-tangent structure on
\mathbb{X}
, namely, a tangent structure
[D\times-]
on
\mathbb{X}
in
{\mathbf{K}}^{\mathsf{co}}
. Moreover, since each
D_{n}\times-
admits a right adjoint in
{\mathbf{K}}
, such a tangent structure is adjunctable in
{\mathbf{K}}^{\mathsf{co}}
. Thus, by Proposition
3.26
, we define an op-tangent structure on
\mathbb{X}
in
{\mathbf{K}}^{\mathsf{co}}
, adjoint to
[D\times-]
, namely, a tangent structure on
\mathbb{X}
in
{\mathbf{K}}
, whose morphisms
\mathrm{T}_{n}
are right adjoint to
D_{n}\times-
. Thus, such a tangent structure is representable.
∎

3.5
Tangentads with negatives as right Kan extensions

In
[
2
, Section 3.3]
, Cockett and Cruttwell showed that, under mild conditions, a tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
admits a subtangent structure
\mathbb{T}^{-}
of
\mathbb{T}
with negatives. Here, we would like to extend this construction in the formal context of tangentads and reinterpret it formally as a suitable right Kan extension.

Let us start by recalling Cockett and Cruttwell’s construction. Let us assume
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
to be a tangent category for which, for every object
M
and for each positive integer
k
, the tangent pullback of
s\colon\mathrm{T}_{2}M\to\mathrm{T}M
along
z\colon M\to\mathrm{T}M
exists:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{-}_{k}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}_{2k}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}_{k}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{k}^{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{k}}

(3.3)

The tangent structure
\mathbb{T}^{-}
is obtained by restricting
\mathbb{T}
along the natural transformation:

\displaystyle\varepsilon\colon\mathrm{T}^{-}M\xrightarrow{e}\mathrm{T}_{2}M\xrightarrow{\pi_{1}}\mathrm{T}M

Furthermore,
\mathbb{T}^{-}
comes equipped with a negation morphism defined as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{-}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}_{2}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{-}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}_{2}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}

The main result of this section consists of showing that
\mathbb{T}^{-}
can be constructed via a right Kan extension.

Proposition 3.33
.

If a tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
admits the tangent pullbacks of Equation (
3.3
), the Leung functor:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}[\mathbb{T}]\colon\mathsf{WEIL}_{1}\to\mathsf{END}(\mathbb{X})

admits a pointwise right Kan extension:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}^{-}[\mathbb{T}]\colon\mathsf{WEIL}_{1}^{-}\to\mathsf{END}(\mathbb{X})

along the inclusion
\mathsf{WEIL}_{1}\to\mathsf{WEIL}_{1}^{-}
and the corresponding tangent structure with negatives is precisely
\mathbb{T}^{-}
.

Proof.

Let us start by defining
\mathfrak{L}^{-}[\mathbb{T}]
as the Leung functor associated with the tangent structure with negatives
\mathbb{T}^{-}
, defined by Cockett and Cruttwell.

Since
\mathsf{WEIL}_{1}
is generated by all Weil algebras
W_{n}
, a monoidal natural transformation
\varepsilon
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{WEIL}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{END}(\mathbb{X})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{WEIL}_{1}^{-}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{L}[\mathbb{T}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{L}^{-}[\mathbb{T}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}

is fully specified by natural transformations:

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{n}\colon\mathfrak{L}^{-}[\mathbb{T}](W_{n})\to\mathfrak{L}[\mathbb{T}](W_{n}^{-})

Let us define each
\varepsilon_{n}
as follows:

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{n}\colon\mathrm{T}_{n}^{-}M\xrightarrow{e_{n}}\mathrm{T}_{2n}M\xrightarrow{(\pi_{1})_{n}}\mathrm{T}M

As shown by Cockett and Cruttwell,
\varepsilon_{n}
makes
\mathbb{T}^{-}
into a subtangent structure of
\mathbb{T}
, thus
\varepsilon
is a well-defined monoidal natural transformation. Let us show that
\varepsilon
satisfies the universal property of a right Kan extension. Consider another monoidal natural transformation:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{WEIL}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{END}(\mathbb{X})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{WEIL}_{1}^{-}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{L}[\mathbb{T}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{L}^{-}[\mathbb{T}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}

Evaluated on the Weil algebra
W
,
\theta
is a natural transformation:

\displaystyle\theta\colon\mathrm{T}^{-}M\to\mathrm{T}M

Let us define
\xi\colon\mathrm{T}^{-}M\to\mathrm{T}^{-}M
as the morphism induced by the universal property of the tangent pullbacks (
3.3
):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{-}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{-}_{2}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{-}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}_{2}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathsf{id},n\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}

Thus, we shall compute:

\displaystyle\quad\varepsilon\circ\xi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\quad\pi_{1}\circ e\circ\xi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\quad\pi_{1}\circ\theta_{2}\circ\langle\mathsf{id},n\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\quad\theta

Therefore, by extending
\xi
to each
W_{n}
in the obvious way, we obtain a monoidal natural transformation
\xi\colon\mathfrak{L}^{-}[\mathbb{T}]\Rightarrow\mathfrak{L}^{-}[\mathbb{T}]
such that
\varepsilon\circ\xi=\theta
. Finally, suppose that
\xi^{\prime}\colon\mathfrak{L}^{-}[\mathbb{T}]\Rightarrow\mathfrak{L}^{-}[\mathbb{T}]
satisfies the same equation as
\xi
. Thus:

\displaystyle\quad e\circ\xi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\quad\pi_{1}\circ\theta_{2}\circ\langle\mathsf{id},n\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\quad\pi_{1}\circ(\xi^{\prime}e)_{2}\circ\langle\mathsf{id},n\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\quad e\circ\xi^{\prime}\circ\pi_{1}\circ\langle\mathsf{id},n\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\quad e\circ\xi^{\prime}

Moreover,
p^{-}\circ\xi^{\prime}=p^{-}
, since
\xi^{\prime}
needs to be compatible with the tangent structure
\mathbb{T}^{-}
. Therefore, by the universal property of the tangent pullback (
3.3
),
\xi^{\prime}=\xi
.
∎

Definition 3.34
.

A
right deniable
tangentad is a tangentad
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
whose Leung functor
\mathfrak{L}[\mathbb{T}]
admits a pointwise right Kan extension along the inclusion
\mathsf{WEIL}_{1}\to\mathsf{WEIL}_{1}^{-}
as a Leung functor.

Definition
3.34
suggests another possibility of equipping a tangent structure with negatives.

Definition 3.35
.

A
left deniable
tangentad is a tangentad
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
whose Leung functor
\mathfrak{L}[\mathbb{T}]
admits a pointwise left Kan extension along the inclusion
\mathsf{WEIL}_{1}\to\mathsf{WEIL}_{1}^{-}
as a Leung functor.

Left deniability is yet to be explored. Future work will investigate this concept.

4
Applications of the formal approach

In this section, we re-interpret some of the constructions of tangent category theory, such as tangent fibrations, tangent monads, or reverse tangent categories, from the viewpoint of tangentads. We exploit this reformulation to prove some results: we show that a tangent monad admits the construction of algebras provided the underlying monad does; we recall the Grothendieck equivalence between tangent fibrations and tangent indexed categories; we prove that tangent split restriction categories are equivalent to
\mathscr{M}
-tangent categories.

4.1
Tangent monads and the construction of algebras

In
[
8
]
, Cockett, Lemay, and Lucyshyn-Wright introduced the notion of a tangent monad. In this section, we prove that tangent monads are precisely tangentads in the
2
-category
\mathsf{MND}
of monads. We employ this characterization to prove that the tangent category of algebras of a tangent monad is precisely the Eilenberg-Moore object of the tangent monad, as defined by Street in
[
19
]
.

Since the
2
-category of monads
\mathsf{MND}({\mathbf{K}})
plays an important role in this section, let us start by recalling this construction. The objects of
\mathsf{MND}({\mathbf{K}})
are tuples
(\mathbb{X};S,\eta,\mu)
formed by an object
\mathbb{X}
of
{\mathbf{K}}
together with a monad
(S,\eta,\mu)
on
\mathbb{X}
, which is a monoid in the monoidal category
\mathsf{END}(\mathbb{X})
.

A
1
-morphism of
\mathsf{MND}({\mathbf{K}})
from a monad
(\mathbb{X},S)
to a monad
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},S^{\prime})
consists of a
1
-morphism
F\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
of
{\mathbf{K}}
together with a
2
-morphism
\alpha\colon S^{\prime}\circ F\Rightarrow S\circ F
, satisfying the following compatibility with the unit
\eta\colon\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}\Rightarrow S
and the multiplication
\mu\colon S^{2}\Rightarrow S
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S^{\prime}\circ F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F\circ S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{\prime}F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\eta}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S^{\prime 2}\circ F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S^{\prime}\circ F\circ S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F\circ S^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S^{\prime}\circ F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F\circ S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu\mathrm{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

Finally, a
2
-morphism
\varphi\colon(F,\alpha)\Rightarrow(G,\beta)\colon(\mathbb{X},S)\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},S^{\prime})
consists of a
2
-morphism
\varphi\colon F\Rightarrow G
of
{\mathbf{K}}
, compatible with the distributive laws:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S^{\prime}\circ F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F\circ S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S^{\prime}\circ G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G\circ S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

Definition 4.1
.

A
tangent monad
in a
2
-category
{\mathbf{K}}
consists of a monad in the
2
-category
\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})
of tangentads of
{\mathbf{K}}
.

Let us unwrap this definition. A monad of
\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})
consists of a tangentad
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
of
{\mathbf{K}}
equipped with a monad
(S,\eta,\mu)
on the category
\mathbb{X}
with a distributive law
\alpha\colon S\circ\mathrm{T}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}\circ S
between the monad
S
and the tangent bundle functor.

Moreover,
\alpha
is required to be compatible with the tangent structure
\mathbb{T}
and with the unit
\eta\colon\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}\Rightarrow S
and the multiplication
\mu\colon S^{2}\Rightarrow S
of the monad:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S\circ\mathrm{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}\circ S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta\mathrm{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}\eta}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S^{2}\circ\mathrm{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S\circ\mathrm{T}\circ S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}\circ S^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S\circ\mathrm{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}\circ S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu\mathrm{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

When the ambient
2
-category
{\mathbf{K}}
is the
2
-category
\mathsf{CAT}
of categories, tangent monads correspond precisely to the notion of tangent monads introduced by Cockett, Lemay, and Lucyshyn-Wright
[
8
, Definition 19]
.

Proposition 4.2
.

The
2
-category
\mathsf{TNG}(\mathsf{MND}({\mathbf{K}}))
of tangentads of the
2
-category of monads of a
2
-category
{\mathbf{K}}
is isomorphic to the
2
-category
\mathsf{MND}(\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}}))
of monads of the
2
-category of tangentads of
{\mathbf{K}}
.

Proof.

First, consider a monad
S
on an object
\mathbb{X}
of
{\mathbf{K}}
. A tangent structure on
S
comprises a morphism
(\mathrm{T},\alpha)\colon(\mathbb{X},S)\to(\mathbb{X},S)
of monads, which is a
1
-morphism
\mathrm{T}\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}
of
{\mathbf{K}}
together with a
2
-morphism
\alpha\colon S\circ\mathrm{T}\Rightarrow S\circ\mathrm{T}
of
{\mathbf{K}}
compatible with the multiplication and the unit of
S
. The structural
2
-morphisms of the tangent structure are
2
-morphisms
p,z,s,l,
and
c
, respectively, of
{\mathbf{K}}
, compatible with the distributive law
\alpha
, and satisfying the axioms of a tangent structure.

The tuple
(\mathrm{T},p,z,s,l,c)
defines a tangent structure of
\mathbb{X}
, and
(S,\alpha)
becomes a monad over
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
in the
2
-category
\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})
of tangentads over
{\mathbf{K}}
. So, every object
(\mathbb{X},S;\mathbb{T},\alpha)
of
\mathsf{TNG}(\mathsf{MND}({\mathbf{K}}))
defines an object
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};S,\alpha)
of
\mathsf{MND}(\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}}))
.

Let
(F,\varphi;\beta)\colon(\mathbb{X},S;\mathbb{T},\alpha)\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},S^{\prime};\mathbb{T}^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
be a lax tangent morphism of
\mathsf{TNG}(\mathsf{MND}({\mathbf{K}}))
. This is a
1
-morphism
F\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
of
{\mathbf{K}}
together with a distributive law
\varphi\colon S^{\prime}\circ F\Rightarrow F\circ S
, compatible with the monad structures, and a lax distributive law
\beta\colon F\circ\mathrm{T}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\circ F
, compatible with
\varphi,\alpha
, and
\alpha^{\prime}
.

This corresponds to a morphism
(F,\beta;\varphi)\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};S,\alpha)\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime};S^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
of monads of
\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})
. Finally, take into consideration a lax
2
-morphism
\theta\colon(F,\varphi;\beta)\Rightarrow(G,\psi;\gamma)
between two lax tangent
1
-morphisms
(F,\varphi;\beta),(G,\psi;\gamma)\colon(\mathbb{X},S;\mathbb{T},\alpha)\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},S^{\prime};\mathbb{T}^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
.

This consists of a
2
-morphism
\theta\colon F\Rightarrow G
, compatible with
\varphi
and
\psi
, and with
\alpha
and
\alpha^{\prime}
. However, this is also a
2
-morphism
\theta\colon(F,\beta;\varphi)\Rightarrow(G,\gamma;\psi)
between the corresponding morphisms of monads of
\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})
.
∎

Remark 4.3
.

In light of Proposition
4.2
, it is natural to wonder whether or not
\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}(\mathsf{MND}({\mathbf{K}}))
and
\mathsf{MND}(\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}({\mathbf{K}}))
are also isomorphic
2
-categories, for a
2
-category
{\mathbf{K}}
. However, this is not the case. Notice that objects of
\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}(\mathsf{MND}({\mathbf{K}}))
are tangentads over the
2
-category of monads over
{\mathbf{K}}
. Thus, the objects of
\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}(\mathsf{MND}({\mathbf{K}}))
are the same objects as those of
\mathsf{TNG}(\mathsf{MND}({\mathbf{K}}))
. On the contrary, the objects of
\mathsf{MND}(\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}({\mathbf{K}}))
are monads over the
2
-category
\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}({\mathbf{K}})
, that are tuples
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};S,\beta)
, where
\beta\colon\mathrm{T}\circ S\Rightarrow S\circ\mathrm{T}
, since
(S,\beta)\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\nrightarrow(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is a colax tangent
1
-morphism. To fix this discrepancy, one can consider the
2
-category
\mathsf{MND}_{\mathsf{co}}({\mathbf{K}})
of monads, colax
1
-morphisms of monads
(F,\beta)\colon(\mathbb{X},S)\nrightarrow(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},S^{\prime})
which are
1
-morphisms
F\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
together with a
2
-morphism:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{X}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{X}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

compatible with the multiplication and the unit of the monads, and
2
-morphisms
\theta\colon(F,\beta)\Rightarrow(G,\gamma)
which consists of
2
-morphisms
\theta\colon F\Rightarrow G
, compatible with
\beta
and
\gamma
. Adopting a dual argument, one can show that
\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}(\mathsf{MND}_{\mathsf{co}}({\mathbf{K}}))
and
\mathsf{MND}_{\mathsf{co}}(\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}({\mathbf{K}}))
are isomorphic
2
-categories.

Street in
[
19
]
established the universal property of the Eilenberg-Moore category of a monad, a.k.a. the category of algebras of a monad, and formalized this construction for arbitrary monads.

When
{\mathbf{K}}
is the
2
-category
\mathsf{CAT}
of categories,
[
2
, Proposition 20]
establishes that the category of algebras
\mathsf{ALG}(S)
of a tangent monad
(S,\alpha)
over a tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
comes equipped with a tangent structure strictly preserved by the forgetful functor
\mathsf{U}\colon\mathsf{ALG}(S)\to\mathbb{X}
.

Concretely, the tangent bundle functor
\mathrm{T}^{S}\colon\mathsf{ALG}(S)\to\mathsf{ALG}(S)
of the tangent structure
\mathbb{T}^{S}
on the category of algebras
\mathsf{ALG}(S)
of a tangent monad
(S,\alpha)
sends and algebra
A
of
S
with structure map
\theta\colon SA\to A
to the algebra
\mathrm{T}A
with structure map:

\displaystyle S\mathrm{T}A\xrightarrow{\alpha}\mathrm{T}SA\xrightarrow{\mathrm{T}\theta}\mathrm{T}A

Moreover, it sends a morphism
f\colon A\to B
of algebras of
S
to
\mathrm{T}f
. The structural natural transformations of
\mathbb{T}^{S}
are defined by the corresponding structural natural transformations of
\mathbb{T}
. When
\mathbb{T}
has negatives, so does
\mathbb{T}^{S}
, with negation
n
as in
\mathbb{T}
.

Thanks to the formal approach, we show that this construction corresponds to Street’s formal notion of the Eilenberg-Moore object of a tangent monad. First, recall that a
2
-category
{\mathbf{K}}

admits the construction of algebras
when the
2
-functor

\displaystyle\mathsf{Inc}\colon{\mathbf{K}}\to\mathsf{MND}({\mathbf{K}})

which sends each object
\mathbb{X}
of
{\mathbf{K}}
to the trivial monad
(\mathbb{X},\mathds{1})
,
\mathds{1}
be the identity on
\mathbb{X}
, admits a right adjoint
\mathsf{ALG}\colon\mathsf{MND}({\mathbf{K}})\to{\mathbf{K}}
. When
{\mathbf{K}}
is the
2
-category
\mathsf{CAT}
of categories,
\mathsf{ALG}
is precisely the
2
-functor which sends a monad
(\mathbb{X},S)
to the category of algebras
\mathsf{ALG}(S)
of
S
.

Theorem 4.4
.

If the
2
-category
{\mathbf{K}}
admits the construction of algebras, so does the
2
-category
\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})
of tangentads of
{\mathbf{K}}
. In particular, the Eilenberg-Moore object
\mathsf{ALG}(S)
of a tangent monad
(S,\alpha)
on a tangentad
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
of
{\mathbf{K}}
comes equipped with a tangent structure strictly preserved by the forgetful
1
-morphism
\mathsf{U}\colon(\mathsf{ALG}(S),\mathbb{T}^{S})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.

Proof.

First, notice that by definition the
2
-functor
\mathsf{ALG}\colon\mathsf{MND}({\mathbf{K}})\to{\mathbf{K}}
is a right adjoint, thus, by Lemma
3.5
, it preserves pointwise limits. Furthermore, the
2
-functor
\mathsf{Inc}\colon{\mathbf{K}}\to\mathsf{MND}({\mathbf{K}})
is also a right adjoint, as proved in
[
19
, Theorem 1]
. In particular, it is the right adjoint of the forgetful
2
-functor
\mathsf{MND}({\mathbf{K}})\to{\mathbf{K}}
which sends a pair
(\mathbb{X},S)
to
\mathbb{X}
.

Therefore, by Lemma
3.5
, both
\mathsf{Inc}
and
\mathsf{ALG}
are pointwise pullback-preserving
2
-functors and they form an adjunction
\mathsf{Inc}\dashv\mathsf{ALG}
in
\mathsf{TWOCAT}_{\mathrm{pp}}
.

As a
2
-functor,
\mathsf{TNG}
preserves adjunctions, thus
\mathsf{TNG}(\mathsf{Inc})\colon\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})\leftrightarrows\mathsf{TNGMND}({\mathbf{K}})\colon\mathsf{TNG}(\mathsf{ALG})
forms an adjunction. Thanks to Proposition
4.2
, the
2
-category
\mathsf{TNGMND}({\mathbf{K}})
of tangent monads is isomorphic to the
2
-category
\mathsf{MND}\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})
of monads in the
2
-category of tangentads of
{\mathbf{K}}
. Therefore,
\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})
admits the construction of algebras.

To prove that the right adjoint
\mathsf{U}\colon(\mathsf{ALG}(S),\mathbb{T}^{S})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is a strict tangent morphism, first recall the construction of the adjunction
F\colon\mathbb{X}\leftrightarrows\mathsf{ALG}(S)\colon\mathsf{U}
associated to a generic monad, as in
[
19
]
. The
2
-functors
\mathsf{ALG}
and
\mathsf{Inc}
form an adjunction
\mathsf{Inc}\dashv\mathsf{ALG}
, whose counit, for every monad
S
over an object
\mathbb{X}
, is a morphism of monads
(\mathsf{ALG}(S),\mathds{1}_{\mathsf{ALG}(S)})\to(\mathbb{X},S)
, where
\mathds{1}_{\mathsf{ALG}(S)}
denotes the trivial monad over
\mathsf{ALG}(S)
. In particular, the underlying
1
-morphism of the counit is the morphism
\mathsf{U}\colon\mathsf{ALG}(S)\to\mathbb{X}
, which represents the right adjoint in the adjunction
F\dashv\mathsf{U}
, associated with the monad
S
.

Notice that the counit of the induced adjunction
\mathsf{ALG}\colon\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})\leftrightarrows\mathsf{MND}(\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}}))\colon\mathsf{Inc}
is precisely given by
\mathsf{TNG}(\varepsilon)
. By definition, given a natural transformation
\theta\colon F\Rightarrow G
from a (pointwise pullback-preserving)
2
-functor
F\colon{\mathbf{K}}\to{\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}
to another
2
-functor
G\colon{\mathbf{K}}\to{\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}
, the corresponding natural transformation
\mathsf{TNG}(\theta)\colon\mathsf{TNG}(F)\Rightarrow\mathsf{TNG}(G)
is defined, for every tangentad
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
of
{\mathbf{K}}
, as the tangent morphism
(F\mathbb{X},F\mathbb{T})\to(G\mathbb{X},G\mathbb{T})
, whose underlying
1
-morphism is
\theta\colon F\mathbb{X}\to G\mathbb{X}
.

The distributive law is just the identity, since
\theta(F\mathrm{T})=(G\mathrm{T})\theta
. In particular, this applies to the counit of the adjunction
\mathsf{Inc}\dashv\mathsf{ALG}
and therefore, the right adjoint
\mathsf{U}\colon(\mathsf{ALG}(S),\mathbb{T}^{S})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is a strict tangent morphism.
∎

Remark 4.5
.

One of the key facts employed in the proof of Theorem
4.4
is that
\mathsf{TNGMND}({\mathbf{K}})\cong\mathsf{MND}(\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}}))
. One could wonder if, assuming that
{\mathbf{K}}
admits the construction of algebras, then also
\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}({\mathbf{K}})
would admit this construction. However, as pointed out in Remark
4.3
,
\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}(\mathsf{MND}({\mathbf{K}}))
is not isomorphic to
\mathsf{MND}(\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}({\mathbf{K}}))
.

As a consequence of Theorem
4.4
, a tangent monad on a tangent category admits the construction of algebras. The next corollary shows that the Eilenberg-Moore object of a tangent monad is precisely the tangent category of algebras of the tangent monad, as described by Cockett, Lemay, and Lucyshyn-Wright.

Corollary 4.6
.

The
2
-category
\mathsf{TNGCAT}
of tangent categories admits the construction of algebras. Moreover, the Eilenberg-Moore object
(\mathsf{ALG}(S),\mathbb{T}^{S})
of a tangent monad
(S,\alpha)
on a given tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is precisely the tangent category described by Cockett, Lemay, and Lucyshyn-Wright in
[
8
]
.

Proof.

Thanks to
[
19
, Theorem 7]
, the
2
-category
\mathsf{CAT}
of categories admits the construction of algebras and the
2
-functor
\mathsf{ALG}
sends a monad to the corresponding Eilenberg-Moore category. By Theorem
4.4
also
\mathsf{TNG}(\mathsf{CAT})
admits the construction of algebras. However, as noticed in Example
2.18
,
\mathsf{TNG}(\mathsf{CAT})
is the
2
-category
\mathsf{TNGCAT}
of tangent categories.

To prove the second part, recall that the
2
-functor
\mathsf{ALG}\colon\mathsf{MND}\to\mathsf{CAT}
sends a morphism of monads
(F,\alpha)\colon(\mathbb{X},S)\to(\mathbb{X},S)
to the functor
\mathsf{ALG}(F,\alpha)
which sends an algebra
A
of
S
with structure map
\theta\colon SA\to A
, to the algebra
FA
of
S^{\prime}
with structure map:

\displaystyle S^{\prime}FA\xrightarrow{\alpha}FSA\xrightarrow{F\theta}FA

Moreover,
\mathsf{ALG}
sends a natural transformation
\varphi\colon(F,\alpha)\Rightarrow(G,\beta)
between two morphisms of monads
(F,\alpha),(G,\beta)\colon(\mathbb{X},S)\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},S^{\prime})
, to the natural transformation
\mathsf{ALG}(F,\alpha)\Rightarrow\mathsf{ALG}(G,\beta)
, defined by
\varphi
.

Recall also that the
2
-functor:

\displaystyle\mathsf{ALG}\colon\mathsf{MND}(\mathsf{TNGCAT})\to\mathsf{TNGCAT}

defined in Theorem
4.4
, under the identification
\mathsf{MND}(\mathsf{TNGCAT})=\mathsf{TNGMND}
, sends a tangentad
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};S,\alpha)
of
\mathsf{MND}
, whose tangent bundle morphism
(\mathrm{T},\alpha)\colon(\mathbb{X},S)\to(\mathbb{X},S)
is given by the tangent bundle functor
\mathrm{T}\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}
together with the distributive law
\alpha\colon S\circ\mathrm{T}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}\circ S
, to the tangentad
(\mathsf{ALG}(\mathbb{X},S),\mathsf{ALG}(\mathbb{T}))
. The tangent bundle morphism is given by
\mathsf{ALG}(\mathrm{T},\alpha)
which sends an algebra
A
of
S
with structure map
\theta\colon SA\to A
to the algebra
\mathrm{T}A
with structure map:

\displaystyle S\mathrm{T}A\xrightarrow{\alpha}\mathrm{T}SA\xrightarrow{\mathrm{T}\theta}\mathrm{T}A

Moreover, it sends a morphism
f\colon A\to B
of algebras of
S
to
\mathrm{T}f
. Finally, all the natural transformations
p,z,s,l
, and
c
are precisely given by the corresponding natural transformations of
\mathbb{T}
.
∎

Remark 4.7
.

Corollary
4.6
explains why
\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}({\mathbf{K}})
does not admit the construction of algebras. Take
{\mathbf{K}}
to be the
2
-category
\mathsf{CAT}
of categories and a monad
(S,\beta)
over
\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}(\mathsf{CAT})
, which consists of a monad
S
over a category
\mathbb{X}
, together with a colax distributive law
\beta\colon\mathrm{T}\circ S\Rightarrow S\circ\mathrm{T}
. Let
A
be an algebra with structure map
SA\to A
of
S
. In order to lift the tangent structure to
\mathsf{ALG}(S)
, we employed the distributive law
\alpha\colon S\circ\mathrm{T}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}\circ S
and defined the tangent bundle of
A
as the algebra
\mathrm{T}A
with structure map
\mathrm{T}\theta\circ\alpha\colon S\mathrm{T}A\to\mathrm{T}SA\to\mathrm{T}A
. However, the colax distributive law
\beta
is pointing in the wrong direction.

Street, in
[
19
]
, noticed that a distributive law
\gamma\colon S^{\prime}\circ S\Rightarrow S\circ S^{\prime}
between two monads
(S,\eta,\mu)
and
(S^{\prime},\eta^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
can be regarded as a monad in the
2
-category
\mathsf{MND}
of monads. Since a tangent monad is a tangentad of
\mathsf{MND}
, we can introduce the notion of a distributive law between tangent monads as a tangent monad in the
2
-category
\mathsf{MND}
of monads, namely, a monad in the
2
-category
\mathsf{TNG}(\mathsf{MND})\cong\mathsf{MND}(\mathsf{TNG})
.

Definition 4.8
.

A
distributive law
of tangent monads of
{\mathbf{K}}
consists of a tangent monad in the
2
-category
\mathsf{MND}({\mathbf{K}})
of monads of
{\mathbf{K}}
.

Let us unpack Definition
4.8
. Given two tangent monads
(S,\alpha)
and
(S^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
on a tangentad
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
of
{\mathbf{K}}
, a distributive law
\gamma\colon(S^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})\circ(S,\alpha)\Rightarrow(S,\alpha)\circ(S^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
of tangent monads consists of a
2
-morphism
\gamma\colon S^{\prime}\circ S\Rightarrow S\circ S^{\prime}
, compatible with the monad structures:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S\circ S^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S^{\prime}\circ S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\eta^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{\prime}S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta S^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}\eta}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S^{\prime 2}\circ S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S^{\prime}\circ S\circ S^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S\circ S^{\prime 2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S^{\prime}\circ S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S\circ S^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S^{\prime}\circ S^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S\circ S^{\prime}\circ S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S^{2}\circ S^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu^{\prime}S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma S^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\mu^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu S^{\prime}}

and with the distributive laws:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S^{\prime}\circ S\circ\mathrm{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S^{\prime}\circ\mathrm{T}\circ S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}\circ S^{\prime}\circ S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S\circ S^{\prime}\circ\mathrm{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S\circ\mathrm{T}\circ S^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}\circ S\circ S^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma\mathrm{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime}S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\alpha^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha S^{\prime}}

A distributive law of monads allows one to compose two monads together. In particular, if
(S,\eta,\mu)
and
(S^{\prime},\eta^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
are two monads on an object
\mathbb{X}
of a
2
-category
{\mathbf{K}}
and
\gamma\colon S^{\prime}\circ S\Rightarrow S\circ S^{\prime}
is a distributive law between them, then
S\circ S^{\prime}
comes equipped with the structure of a monad of
{\mathbf{K}}
whose unit and multiplication are defined as follows:

\displaystyle\eta\circ_{\gamma}\eta^{\prime}\colon\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}\xrightarrow{\eta}S\xrightarrow{S\eta^{\prime}}S\circ S^{\prime}

\displaystyle\mu\circ\gamma\mu^{\prime}\colon S\circ S^{\prime}\circ S\circ S^{\prime}\xrightarrow{S\gamma S^{\prime}}S^{2}\circ S^{\prime 2}\xrightarrow{\mu S^{\prime 2}}S\circ S^{\prime 2}\xrightarrow{S\mu^{\prime}}S\circ S^{\prime}

We can harness this formal argument to immediately prove that a distributive law of two tangent monads forms a new tangent monad.

Proposition 4.9
.

Given two tangent monads
(S,\alpha)
and
(S^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
on a tangentad
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
of
{\mathbf{K}}
and a distributive law
\gamma\colon(S^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})\circ(S,\alpha)\Rightarrow(S,\alpha)\circ(S^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
of tangent monads, the composition monad
S\circ_{\gamma}S^{\prime}
induced by the distributive law
\gamma
is again a tangent monad on
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.

4.2
Tangent fibrations

In
[
3
, Section 5]
, Cockett and Cruttwell showed that each tangent category equipped with a class
\mathscr{D}
of morphisms stable under tangent pullbacks and under the tangent bundle functor defines a fibration whose fibre over an object
M
contains the maps of
\mathscr{D}
whose target is
M
. Furthermore, such a fibration is compatible with the tangent bundle functors.

Cockett and Cruttwell distilled the property of this class of fibrations into the notion of a tangent fibration. In
[
15
]
, we proved that tangent fibrations are equivalent to tangentads in the
2
-category
\mathsf{FIB}
of fibrations and exploited this classification to construct a genuine Grothendieck construction between tangent fibrations and tangent indexed categories.

This section is dedicated to recalling the notion of a tangent fibration and showing that tangent fibrations are precisely tangentads in the
2
-category
\mathsf{FIB}
of fibrations.

For starters, let us briefly recall that a fibration
\Pi\colon\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{X}
between two categories consists of a functor for which every morphism
f\colon M\to M^{\prime}
of the base category
\mathbb{X}
admits a Cartesian lift
\varphi_{f}^{E^{\prime}}\colon f^{\ast}E^{\prime}\to E^{\prime}
for every object
E^{\prime}
on the fibre over
M^{\prime}
, namely,
\Pi(E^{\prime})=M^{\prime}
.

A Cartesian morphism
\varphi\colon E^{\prime}\to E^{\prime\prime}
is a morphism of the total category
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
for which any morphism
\psi\colon E\to E^{\prime\prime}
of
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
and any morphism
g\colon\Pi(E)\to\Pi(E^{\prime})
making the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Pi(E^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Pi(E)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Pi(E^{\prime\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi(\varphi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi(\psi)}

induce a unique morphism
\xi\colon E\to E^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
such that
\Pi(\xi)=g
and the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

A fibration
\Pi\colon\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{X}
equipped with a choice of Cartesian lift
\varphi_{f}^{E^{\prime}}\colon f^{\ast}E^{\prime}\to E^{\prime}
for each morphism
f\colon M\to M^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{X}
and each object
E^{\prime}
of the fibre over
M^{\prime}
is known as a
cloven fibration
and the choice
(f,E^{\prime})\mapsto\varphi_{f}^{E^{\prime}}
is called the
cleavage
of the fibration.

In the following, every fibration is assumed to be cloven and we denote by
\varphi_{f}^{E^{\prime}}
the Cartesian lift of
f\colon M\to M^{\prime}
along
E^{\prime}\in\Pi^{-1}(M^{\prime})
defined by the cleavage. Moreover, we omit the superscript
E^{\prime}
when
E^{\prime}
is clear from the context. A fibration
\Pi\colon\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{X}
is denoted as a triple
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\Pi)
.

Suppose
\Pi_{\circ}\colon\mathbb{X}_{\circ}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{X}_{\circ}
and
\Pi_{\bullet}\colon\mathbb{X}_{\bullet}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{X}_{\bullet}
are two fibrations and
F^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{X}^{\prime}_{\circ}\to\mathbb{X}^{\prime}_{\bullet}
and
F\colon\mathbb{X}_{\circ}\to\mathbb{X}_{\bullet}
are two functors such that
\Pi_{\bullet}\circ F^{\prime}=F\circ\Pi_{\circ}
. Given a morphism
f\colon M\to M^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{X}_{\circ}
, one can lift
Ff\colon FM\to FM^{\prime}
to
\mathbb{X}_{\bullet}^{\prime}
and obtain a Cartesian lift
\varphi_{Ff}\colon(Ff)^{\ast}(F^{\prime}E^{\prime})\to F^{\prime}E^{\prime}
of
Ff
over
F^{\prime}E^{\prime}\in\mathbb{X}_{\bullet}^{\prime}
.

However, the cleavage of
\Pi_{\circ}
also defines a Cartesian lift
\varphi_{f}\colon f^{\ast}E^{\prime}\to E^{\prime}
of
f
over
E^{\prime}
. Therefore, since
\Pi_{\bullet}F^{\prime}\varphi_{f}=F\Pi_{\circ}\varphi_{f}=Ff
, also
F^{\prime}\varphi_{f}
is a lift of
Ff
. By employing the universal property of the Cartesian lift
\varphi_{Ff}
, we obtain a unique morphism
\xi_{f}\colon F^{\prime}(f^{\ast}E^{\prime})\dashrightarrow(Ff)^{\ast}(F^{\prime}E^{\prime})
making the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(Ff)^{\ast}(F^{\prime}E^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}(f^{\ast}E^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}E^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{Ff}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}\varphi_{f}}

A morphism of fibrations
(F,F^{\prime})\colon(\mathbb{X}_{\circ},\mathbb{X}_{\circ}^{\prime},\Pi_{\circ})\to(\mathbb{X}_{\bullet},\mathbb{X}_{\bullet}^{\prime},\Pi_{\bullet})
consists of a pair of functors
F\colon\mathbb{X}_{\circ}\to\mathbb{X}_{\bullet}
and
