F^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{X}_{\circ}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{X}_{\bullet}^{\prime}
strictly commuting with the two fibrations, namely,
\Pi_{\bullet}\circ F^{\prime}=F\circ\Pi_{\circ}
, and preserving the Cartesian lifts, meaning the unique morphism
\xi_{f}\colon F^{\prime}(f^{\ast}E^{\prime})\dashrightarrow(Ff)^{\ast}(F^{\prime}E^{\prime})
induced by the universality of
\varphi_{Ff}
is an isomorphism for every morphism
f\colon M\to M^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{X}_{\circ}
and every object
E^{\prime}\in\Pi_{\circ}^{-1}(M^{\prime})
.

Definition 4.10
.

Given two tangent categories
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
and
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, a
tangent fibration

\Pi\colon(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
consists of a fibration
\Pi\colon\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{X}
whose underlying functor is a strict tangent morphism and such that the tangent bundle functors preserve the Cartesian lifts, namely,
(\mathrm{T},\mathrm{T}^{\prime})\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\Pi)\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\Pi)
is a morphism of fibrations.

To compare tangent fibrations with tangentads, let us recall the definition of the
2
-category
\mathsf{FIB}
of fibrations.

​
[
Objects
]
:

An object of
\mathsf{FIB}
consists of a (cloven) fibration
\Pi\colon\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{X}
;

​
[
1
-morphisms
]
:

A
1
-morphism is a morphism of fibrations
(F,F^{\prime})\colon(\mathbb{X}_{\circ},\mathbb{X}_{\circ}^{\prime},\Pi_{\circ})\to(\mathbb{X}_{\bullet},\mathbb{X}_{\bullet}^{\prime},\Pi_{\bullet})
;

​
[
2
-morphisms
]
:

A
2
-morphism
(F,F^{\prime})\Rightarrow(G,G^{\prime})\colon(\mathbb{X}_{\circ},\mathbb{X}_{\circ}^{\prime},\Pi_{\circ})\to(\mathbb{X}_{\bullet},\mathbb{X}_{\bullet}^{\prime},\Pi_{\bullet})
consists of pair of natural transformations
\varphi\colon F\Rightarrow G
and
\varphi^{\prime}\colon F^{\prime}\Rightarrow G^{\prime}
such that:

\displaystyle\Pi_{\bullet}\varphi=\varphi^{\prime}_{\Pi_{\circ}}

Proposition 4.11
.

A tangent fibration
\Pi\colon(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
between two tangent categories is equivalent to a tangentad in the
2
-category
\mathsf{FIB}
of fibrations.

Proof.

A tangentad in the
2
-category
\mathsf{FIB}
consists of a fibration
\Pi\colon\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{X}
together with an endofunctor
(\mathrm{T},\mathrm{T}^{\prime})\colon\Pi\to\Pi
of fibrations and a family of
2
-morphisms of fibrations
(p,p^{\prime})\colon(\mathrm{T},\mathrm{T}^{\prime})\Rightarrow\mathsf{id}_{\Pi}
,
(z,z^{\prime})\colon\mathsf{id}_{\Pi}\Rightarrow(\mathrm{T},\mathrm{T}^{\prime})
,
(s,s^{\prime})\colon(\mathrm{T}_{2},\mathrm{T}_{2}^{\prime})\Rightarrow(\mathrm{T},\mathrm{T}^{\prime})
,
(l,l^{\prime})\colon(\mathrm{T},\mathrm{T}^{\prime})\Rightarrow(\mathrm{T}^{2},{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}}^{2})
, and
(c,c^{\prime})\colon(\mathrm{T}^{2},{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}}^{2})\Rightarrow(\mathrm{T}^{2},{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}}^{2})
, satisfying suitable axioms. Notice that the
n
-fold pullback of the projection
(p,p^{\prime})
along itself consists of the pair of functors
(\mathrm{T}_{n},\mathrm{T}_{n}^{\prime})
. To prove this statement, we need to show that
(\mathrm{T}_{n},\mathrm{T}_{n}^{\prime})
preserves Cartesian lifts. Let us consider the diagram in the base category
\mathbb{X}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}_{n}A^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}_{n}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}A^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}_{n}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}f}

where
f\colon A^{\prime}\to A
is a morphism of
\mathbb{X}
and let
E
be an object in the fibre over
A
. We need to show that the morphism
\xi_{n}\colon\mathrm{T}_{n}^{\prime}(f^{\ast}E)\to(\mathrm{T}_{n}f)^{\ast}\mathrm{T}^{\prime}_{n}E
induced by the universal property of the Cartesian lift
\varphi_{\mathrm{T}_{n}f}
is invertible. First, notice that the
k
-th projection
\pi_{k}\colon\mathrm{T}_{n}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}_{n}
induces a unique morphism
\xi_{\pi_{k}}\colon(\mathrm{T}_{n}f)^{\ast}\mathrm{T}_{n}^{\prime}E\to(\mathrm{T}f)^{\ast}\mathrm{T}^{\prime}E
, making the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathrm{T}_{n}f)^{\ast}\mathrm{T}_{n}^{\prime}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}_{n}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathrm{T}f)^{\ast}\mathrm{T}^{\prime}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\mathrm{T}_{n}f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{\pi_{k}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{k}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\mathrm{T}f}}

Since
(\mathrm{T},\mathrm{T}^{\prime})
preserves Cartesian lifts, the unique morphism
\xi\colon\mathrm{T}^{\prime}(f^{\ast}E)\to(\mathrm{T}f)^{\ast}(\mathrm{T}^{\prime}E)
is invertible, therefore, we can define a family of morphisms:

\displaystyle(\mathrm{T}_{n}f)^{\ast}(\mathrm{T}_{n}^{\prime}E)\xrightarrow{\xi_{\pi_{k}}}(\mathrm{T}f)^{\ast}(\mathrm{T}^{\prime}E)\xrightarrow{\xi}\mathrm{T}^{\prime}(f^{\ast}E)

By employing the universal property of the pullback
\mathrm{T}_{n}^{\prime}
, we obtain a morphism
(\mathrm{T}_{n}f)^{\ast}(\mathrm{T}_{n}^{\prime}E)\to(\mathrm{T}_{n}f)^{\ast}\mathrm{T}^{\prime}E
which inverts
\xi_{n}
.

Since the
n
-fold pullback of the projection along itself is the morphism of fibrations
(\mathrm{T}_{n},\mathrm{T}_{n}^{\prime})
, it is not hard to prove that
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
and
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
are tangent categories. Furthermore, since
\Pi\circ\mathrm{T}^{\prime}=\mathrm{T}\circ\Pi
,
\Pi p^{\prime}=p_{\Pi}
,
\Pi z^{\prime}=z_{\Pi}
,
\Pi s^{\prime}=s_{\Pi}
,
\Pi l^{\prime}=l_{\Pi}
, and
\Pi c^{\prime}=c_{\Pi}
,
\Pi
is a strict tangent morphism. Therefore, a tangentad of
\mathsf{FIB}
is a tangent fibration.

Conversely, the data of a tangent fibration can easily be rearranged to produce a tangentad of
\mathsf{FIB}
.
∎

4.3
Tangent indexed categories and the Grothendieck construction

In
[
15
]
, A Grothendieck construction in the context of tangent categories was introduced by lifting the
2
-equivalence between (cloven) fibrations and indexed categories to tangentads. In particular, tangent indexed categories were introduced as tangentads in the
2
-category
\mathsf{INDX}
of indexed categories as the “correct” notion of indexed categories in the context of tangent category theory. In this section, we briefly recall this definition and the Grothendieck equivalence.

For starters, recall that
\mathsf{INDX}
is the
2
-category so defined:

​
[
Objects
]
:

An object of
\mathsf{INDX}
consists of a pair
(\mathbb{X},\mathscr{I})
formed by a category
\mathbb{X}
and an indexed category, a.k.a. a pseudofunctor,
\mathscr{I}\colon\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{op}}\to\mathsf{CAT}
;

​
[
1
-morphisms
]
:

A
1
-morphism
(F,\{F^{A}\}_{A},\{\xi^{f}\}_{f})
of
\mathsf{INDX}
from
(\mathbb{X}_{\circ},\mathscr{I}_{\circ})
to
(\mathbb{X}_{\bullet},\mathscr{I}_{\bullet})
comprises a functor
F\colon\mathbb{X}_{\circ}\to\mathbb{X}_{\bullet}
together with a collection of functors
F^{A}\colon\mathbb{X}_{\circ}^{A}\to\mathbb{X}_{\bullet}^{FA}
, indexed by the objects
A
of
\mathbb{X}_{\circ}
, where
\mathbb{X}_{\circ}^{A}\>\colon\!=\mathscr{I}_{\circ}(A)
and
\mathbb{X}_{\bullet}^{FA}\>\colon\!=\mathscr{I}_{\bullet}(FA)
, and a collection of
2
-morphisms
\xi^{f}\colon F^{A}\circ f^{\ast}_{\circ}\Rightarrow(Ff)^{\ast}_{\bullet}\circ F^{B}
, called
distributors
, indexed by the morphisms
f\colon A\to B
of
\mathbb{X}_{\circ}
, where
f^{\ast}_{\circ}\>\colon\!=\mathscr{I}_{\circ}(f)
and
(Ff)^{\ast}_{\bullet}\>\colon\!=\mathscr{I}_{\bullet}(Ff)
. Moreover, the
\xi^{f}
are compatible with the unitors and compositors of the indexed categories as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{A}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(F\mathsf{id}_{A})^{\ast}_{\bullet}F^{A}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{A}\mathsf{id}_{A\circ}^{\ast}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathsf{id}_{FA})^{\ast}_{\bullet}F^{A}E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Unit}_{\bullet}F^{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{A}\mathsf{Unit}_{\circ}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{\mathsf{id}_{A}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{A}f^{\ast}_{\circ}g^{\ast}_{\circ}K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(Ff)^{\ast}_{\bullet}F^{B}g^{\ast}_{\circ}K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(Ff)^{\ast}_{\bullet}(Fg)^{\ast}_{\bullet}F^{C}K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{A}(g\circ f)^{\ast}K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(F(g\circ f))^{\ast}_{\bullet}F^{C}K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(FfFg)^{\ast}_{\bullet}F^{C}K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{f}g^{\ast}_{\circ}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{A}\mathsf{Comp}_{\circ}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Ff)^{\ast}_{\bullet}\kappa^{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Comp}_{\bullet}F^{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{g\circ f}}

where

, and

and

and

, and

These morphisms also need to satisfy the axioms of a tangentad. For the sake of completeness, let us state the main result of
[
14
]
which motivated Definition
4.12
.

Theorem 4.13

(Grothendieck construction in the context of tangent categories)
.

The Grothendieck correspondence between fibrations and indexed categories lifts to a
2
-equivalence between the
2
-category
\mathsf{TNGFIB}
of tangent fibrations and the
2
-category
\mathsf{TNGINDX}\>\colon\!=\mathsf{TNG}(\mathsf{INDX})
of tangent indexed categories:

\displaystyle\mathsf{TNGFIB}\simeq\mathsf{TNGINDX}

Similarly, the
2
-categories
\mathsf{TNGFIB}_{\mathsf{co}}
and
\mathsf{TNGINDX}_{\mathsf{co}}\>\colon\!=\mathsf{TNG}_{\mathsf{co}}(\mathsf{INDX})
are equivalent:

\displaystyle\mathsf{TNGFIB}_{\mathsf{co}}\simeq\mathsf{TNGINDX}_{\mathsf{co}}

Moreover, these two equivalences can be restricted to strong and strict morphisms, yielding two other equivalences:

\displaystyle\mathsf{TNGFIB}_{\cong}\simeq\mathsf{TNGINDX}_{\cong}\>\colon\!=\mathsf{TNG}_{\cong}(\mathsf{INDX})

\displaystyle\mathsf{TNGFIB}_{=}\simeq\mathsf{TNGINDX}_{=}\>\colon\!=\mathsf{TNG}_{=}(\mathsf{INDX})

4.4
Reverse tangent categories

Reverse tangent categories were introduced by Cruttwell and Lemay in
[
6
]
to axiomatize the operation of reverse differentiation in the context of tangent categories. Reverse differentiation, categorically introduced in
[
9
]
, is a formalization of the back-propagation operation employed in machine-learning techniques. While Cartesian reverse differential categories describe reverse differentiation in the context of Euclidean spaces, reverse tangent categories extend this operation for arbitrary locally linear geometric spaces of parameters. Reverse tangent categories also axiomatize the cotangent bundle of a manifold in the context of tangent category theory.

In this section, we recall the definition of a reverse tangent category and show that reverse tangent categories form a special class of tangentads in the
2
-category of dagger fibrations. In Example
3.23
, we have mentioned that the
2
-category
\mathsf{FIB}
of fibrations comes with opposite objects, where the involution
(-)^{\mathsf{op}}
sends a fibration to its dual.

Concretely, the fibres of the dual fibration of a cloven fibration
\Pi
are the opposite of the corresponding fibres of
\Pi
. Kock in
[
12
]
discusses the notion of dual fibrations for arbitrary non-necessarily cloven fibrations. For the sake of simplicity, throughout the section, we assume that every fibration is cloven. However, we expect every result to extend easily in the general context.

In
[
3
]
, Cockett and Cruttwell showed that (display) differential bundles of a tangent category can be organized into a tangent fibration. For a definition of differential bundles, we refer to the cited paper.

In
[
15
]
, the author showed that his construction yields an adjunction between tangent fibrations and indexed tangent categories, which, however, is not an equivalence, in contrast with the genuine Grothendieck equivalence between tangent fibrations and tangent indexed categories.

In general, the opposite of a tangent category is not again a tangent category, unless the tangent bundle functor and all
n
-fold pullbacks along the projection admit left adjoints (cf.
[
2
, Proposition 5.17]
). This makes the dual of a tangent fibration, not a tangent fibration anymore.

Despite that the dual of a tangent fibration cannot be equipped with a tangent structure,
[
10
, Lemma 32]
establishes that each morphism of fibrations
(F,F^{\prime})\colon\Pi_{\circ}\to\Pi_{\bullet}
, induces a morphism of fibrations
(F,F^{\prime})^{\bullet}=(F,F^{\prime\bullet})\colon\Pi_{\circ}^{\bullet}\to\Pi_{\bullet}^{\bullet}
between the dual fibrations. In the context of cloven fibrations,
(F,F^{\prime})^{\bullet}
is the morphism of fibrations which corresponds to the morphism of indexed categories:

\displaystyle(\mathbb{X}_{\circ})_{M}^{\bullet}=(\mathbb{X}_{\circ})_{M}^{\mathsf{op}}\xrightarrow{F_{M}^{\mathsf{op}}}(\mathbb{X}_{\bullet})_{FM}^{\mathsf{op}}=(\mathbb{X}_{\bullet})_{FM}^{\bullet}

via the Grothendieck construction, where
(\mathbb{X}_{\circ})_{M}=\Pi_{\circ}^{-1}(M)
denotes the fibre over an object
M\in\mathbb{X}_{\circ}
of
\Pi_{\circ}
,
(\mathbb{X}_{\bullet})_{FM}=\Pi_{\bullet}^{-1}(FM)
is the fibre over
FM
of
\Pi_{\bullet}
, and
F_{M}\colon(\mathbb{X}_{\circ})_{M}\to(\mathbb{X}_{\bullet})_{FM}
indicates the functor induced by
(F,F^{\prime})
between the fibres.

Thanks to this correspondence of morphisms of fibrations, the tangent bundle functor
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\colon\mathsf{DB}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to\mathsf{DB}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
induces a functor
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\bullet}\colon\mathsf{DB}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to\mathsf{DB}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.

It is important to notice that the equivalence of categories
(-)^{\mathsf{op}}\colon\mathsf{CAT}\to\mathsf{CAT}
does not preserve natural transformations. In particular,
(-)^{\mathsf{op}}
extends to a
2
-equivalence between
\mathsf{CAT}
and
\mathsf{CAT}^{\mathsf{co}}
, where the latter denotes the
2
-category of categories, functors, and the opposite of natural transformations. This is the main reason why the dual of a tangent fibration is not, in general, a tangent fibration, since the dualization does not preserve the structural natural transformations.

Let us recall the definition of a reverse tangent category, introduced by Cruttwell and Lemay in
[
6
]
.

Definition 4.14
.

A
reverse tangent category
consists of a tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
and an involution
(-)^{\ast}\colon\mathsf{DB}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to\mathsf{DB}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
on the tangent fibration
\mathsf{DB}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
of differential bundles of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
(cf.
[
3
, Corollary 5.9]
). Concretely, the involution consists of a morphism of fibrations between the fibration
\mathsf{DB}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
and its dual fibration
\mathsf{DB}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
whose base functor is the identity and whose total functor is the identity on objects, together with a natural isomorphism
\xi\colon(-)^{\ast}\circ(-)^{\ast}\Rightarrow\mathsf{id}_{\mathsf{DB}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})}
. Moreover, the following diagram is required to commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{DB}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{DB}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{DB}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{DB}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}^{\bullet}}

A dagger fibration, introduced in
[
9
]
, consists of a fibration equipped with an involution. Let us recall this definition.

Definition 4.15
.

A
dagger
(
cloven
)
fibration
consists of a (cloven) fibration
\Pi\colon\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{X}
together with a morphism
(-)^{\ast}\colon\Pi\to\Pi^{\bullet}
of fibrations, called the
involution
, between
\Pi
and its dual fibration
\Pi^{\bullet}
, whose base functor is the identity and whose total functor
(-)^{\ast}\colon\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{X}^{\prime\bullet}
is the identity on objects, and with a natural isomorphism
\xi\colon(-)^{\ast}\circ(-)^{\ast}\Rightarrow\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}^{\prime}}
. When the fibration is assumed to be cloven, the involution is required to preserve the cleavage, by sending Cartesian cloven morphisms to Cartesian cloven morphisms.

Remark 4.16
.

In
[
9
, Definition 33]
, the isomorphism
\xi\colon(-)^{\ast}\circ(-)^{\ast}\Rightarrow\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}^{\prime}}
of Definition
4.15
was assumed to be the identity. However, to compare dagger fibrations with the notion of reverse tangent categories proposed in
[
6
]
, we needed to weaken this condition and assume
\xi
to be a, non-necessarily trivial, isomorphism.

In order to compare reverse tangent categories with tangentads of dagger fibrations, first we need to introduce
1
and
2
-morphisms of dagger fibrations.

Definition 4.17
.

A
morphism of dagger fibrations
from a dagger fibration
\Pi_{\circ}\colon\mathbb{X}_{\circ}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{X}_{\circ}
with involution
(-)_{\circ}^{\ast}
to a dagger fibration
\Pi_{\bullet}\colon\mathbb{X}_{\bullet}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{X}_{\bullet}
with involution
(-)_{\bullet}^{\ast}
, consists of a morphism
(F,F^{\prime})\colon\Pi_{\circ}\to\Pi_{\bullet}
of fibrations for which the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{X}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{X}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{X}^{\prime\bullet}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{X}^{\prime\bullet}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime\bullet}}

Definition 4.18
.

A
2
-morphism of dagger fibrations

(\varphi,\varphi^{\prime})\colon(F,F^{\prime})\Rightarrow(G,G^{\prime})\colon\Pi_{\circ}\to\Pi_{\bullet}
consists of a
2
-morphism of fibrations
(\varphi,\varphi^{\prime})\colon(F,F^{\prime})\Rightarrow(G,G^{\prime})
.

Dagger fibrations together with their morphisms and
2
-morphisms form a
2
-category denoted by
{\dagger}\!-\!\mathsf{FIB}
.

Proposition 4.19
.

A reverse tangent category is a tangentad in the
2
-category
{\dagger}\!-\!\mathsf{FIB}
of dagger fibrations.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.11
, tangent fibrations are equivalent to tangentads in the
2
-category
\mathsf{FIB}
of fibrations. Moreover, the forgetful
2
-functor
{\dagger}\!-\!\mathsf{FIB}\to\mathsf{FIB}
which forgets the dagger structure preserves the
1
and the
2
-morphisms. Therefore, the underlying fibration of a tangentad of
{\dagger}\!-\!\mathsf{FIB}
is a tangent fibration. Moreover, such a fibration comes equipped with an involution
(-)^{\ast}
. Furthermore, the tangent bundle functors
(\mathrm{T},\mathrm{T}^{\prime})\colon\Pi\to\Pi
need to be compatible with the involution. Finally, a reverse tangent fibration is precisely a tangent category whose fibration
\mathsf{DB}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
of differential bundles comes equipped with an involution, compatible with the tangent bundle functors. Therefore, the dagger fibration of differential bundles of a reverse tangent category is a tangentad in
{\dagger}\!-\!\mathsf{FIB}
.
∎

It is important to realize that reverse tangent categories only count for a small family of tangentads of
{\dagger}\!-\!\mathsf{FIB}
. Indeed, in general, the underlying fibration of a tangentad of
{\dagger}\!-\!\mathsf{FIB}
will not be the fibration of differential bundles of a tangent category. This suggests the following definition.

Definition 4.20
.

A
dagger tangent fibration
consists of a tangentad of the
2
-category
{\dagger}\!-\!\mathsf{FIB}
of dagger fibrations.

4.5
Display and étale tangent categories

Often in tangent category theory, one needs to work with a class of morphisms stable under tangent pullbacks and under the tangent bundle functor. Such a class of morphisms is called a tangent display system. In
[
7
, Definition 2.2]
, Cruttwell and the author proposed an alternative approach to the “pullback problem” by looking at
tangent display maps
which form the maximal tangent display system of a tangent category. We suggest the reader consult
[
7
]
for details.

In this section, we show that tangent display systems can be regarded as tangentads in the
2
-category of display systems. Let us start by recalling that a display system
\mathscr{D}
in a category
\mathbb{X}
consists of a collection of morphisms of
\mathbb{X}
, called display maps, stable under pullbacks. Concretely, this means that, for each morphism
q\colon E\to M
of
\mathscr{D}
and each morphism
f\colon N\to M
of
\mathbb{X}
, the pullback of
q
along
f

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

exists and the corresponding morphism
q^{\prime}\colon f^{\ast}E\to N
belongs to
\mathscr{D}
. A
display category
consists of a category equipped with a display system. In a display category
(\mathbb{X},\mathscr{D})
, each pullback diagram whose vertical morphisms are display maps is called a
display pullback
.

A display functor from a display category
(\mathbb{X},\mathscr{D})
to a display category
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathscr{D}^{\prime})
consists of a functor
F\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
which sends each display map of
(\mathbb{X},\mathscr{D})
to a display map of
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathscr{D}^{\prime})
and which preserves display pullbacks, namely, the image of a display pullback of
(\mathbb{X},\mathscr{D})
along
F
is a display pullback of
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathscr{D}^{\prime})
.

In order to organize display categories and display functors into a
2
-category, we can choose between at least two different options of
2
-morphisms. In particular, we denote by
\mathscr{D}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}
the
2
-category of display categories, display functors, and natural transformations between display functors. Moreover,
\mathscr{D}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}^{\ast}
denotes the
2
-category with the same objects and
1
-morphisms of
\mathscr{D}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}
and whose
2
-morphisms are
display natural transformations
, namely, natural transformations
\varphi\colon F\Rightarrow G\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathscr{D})\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathscr{D}^{\prime})
between display functors for which the naturality diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FE}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GE}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GM}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Gq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

are display pullbacks for each display map
q\colon E\to M
of
(\mathbb{X},\mathscr{D})
.

Tangent display systems are the tangent categorical analog of a display system in a tangent category.

Definition 4.21
.

In a tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, a
tangent display system
consists of a collection
\mathscr{D}
of morphisms of
\mathbb{X}
, called
tangent display maps
, stable under tangent pullbacks and the tangent bundle functor. Concretely, for each
q\colon E\to M
of
\mathscr{D}
and each morphism
f\colon N\to M
of
\mathbb{X}
, the tangent pullback of
q
along
f
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

exists and the corresponding morphism
q^{\prime}\colon f^{\ast}E\to N
belongs to
\mathscr{D}
. Furthermore, the tangent bundle functor sends tangent display maps to tangent display maps. A
display tangent category

(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};\mathscr{D})
comprises a tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
and a tangent display system of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.

The formal approach does not recover the notion of a tangent display system, exactly. Instead, it produces a stronger version.

Definition 4.22
.

A tangent display system
\mathscr{D}
of a tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is
strong
when each
n
-fold pullback
\mathrm{T}_{n}
of the projection along itself sends each tangent display map to a tangent display map. A
strong display tangent category
is a display tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}:\mathscr{D})
whose tangent display system is strong.

Proposition 4.23
.

Strong tangent display categories are equivalent to tangentads in the
2
-category
\mathscr{D}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}
.

Proof.

Is it not hard to see that a tangentad of
\mathscr{D}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}
consists of a tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
together with a display system
\mathscr{D}
. Furthermore, since the tangent bundle functor is a display functor, it has to send each display map to another display map and each display pullback to a display pullback. In particular, this implies that
\mathscr{D}
is stable under the tangent bundle functor and for each display map
q\colon E\to M
and each
f\colon N\to M
the pullback of
q
along
f
is preserved by all iterates of
\mathrm{T}
and the corresponding morphism
q^{\prime}\colon f^{\ast}E\to N
also belongs to
\mathscr{D}
. Finally, since each
\mathrm{T}_{n}
is a display functor,
\mathscr{D}
is also stable under
\mathrm{T}_{n}
. This implies that
\mathscr{D}
is a strong tangent display system for
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.
∎

Despite the discrepancy between display tangent categories and tangentads in the
2
-category of display categories, one can “strengthen” a tangent display system by formally closing it by all functors
\mathrm{T}_{n}
, concretely, given a display tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};\mathscr{D})
let
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathscr{D}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
be the collection of all morphisms of
\mathbb{X}
of type
\mathrm{T}_{k_{1}}\dots\mathrm{T}_{k_{n}}q
for each
q\in\mathscr{D}
and each collection of non-negative integers
k_{1},\dots,k_{n}
.

Lemma 4.24
.

Given a display tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};\mathscr{D})
,
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathscr{D}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
defines a strong tangent display system for
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. Moreover, the inclusion
\mathscr{D}\subseteq\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathscr{D}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
induces a strict display tangent morphism
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};\mathscr{D})\hookrightarrow(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathscr{D}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)
.

Proof.

Let us suppose that
q\colon E\to M
admits all tangent pullbacks and let us prove that so does each
\mathrm{T}_{n}q\colon\mathrm{T}_{n}E\to\mathrm{T}_{n}M
. Consider a morphism
f\colon N\to\mathrm{T}_{n}M
and each diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}_{n}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}_{n}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}_{n}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{k}}

Since
q
admits all tangent pullback, the pullback of
\mathrm{T}q
along
\pi_{k}\circ f
is well-defined for each
k=1,\dots,n
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}_{n}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}_{n}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}_{n}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{k}}

By the universal property, we get a morphism
P_{k}\dashrightarrow\mathrm{T}_{n}E
. Since the outer square and the right square are tangent pullback diagrams, by
[
7
, Lemma 2.3]
also the left square is a tangent pullback diagram, proving that
\mathrm{T}_{n}q
admits all tangent pullbacks.

In particular, each
\mathrm{T}_{k_{1}}\dots\mathrm{T}_{k_{n}}q
admits all tangent pullbacks. If
\mathrm{T}_{k_{1}}\dots\mathrm{T}_{k_{n}}q
is a morphism of
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathscr{D}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
, then
\mathrm{T}_{n}(\mathrm{T}_{k_{1}}\dots\mathrm{T}_{k_{n}}q)=\mathrm{T}_{n}\mathrm{T}_{k_{1}}\dots\mathrm{T}_{k_{n}}q
which, by definition, is another morphism of
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathscr{D}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
. This proves that
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathscr{D}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
is a strong tangent display system. Finally, by taking
k_{1}=k_{n}=0
, it is immediate to see that
\mathscr{D}\subseteq\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathscr{D}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
and that this inclusion defines a strict display tangent morphism.
∎

One can also recover classes of display étale maps as tangentads. For starters, recall the notion of an étale map in tangent category, as introduced by MacAdam in
[
17
]
.

Definition 4.25
.

An
étale map
in a tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
consists of a morphism
q\colon E\to M
for which the naturality diagram of the projection with
q
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

is a tangent pullback. In a display tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};\mathscr{D})
, a
display étale map
is a display map which is also an étale map. Finally, a display tangent category is an
étale tangent category
if each of its display maps is étale.

Étale maps recover the usual notion of étale maps in differential geometry (cf.
[
7
, Proposition 2.29]
).

Lemma 4.26
.

A morphism
q\colon E\to M
of a tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is étale if and only if the naturality diagrams of
q
with all of the structural natural transformations of
\mathbb{T}
are tangent pullback diagrams.

Proof.

The proof of this lemma can be found in
[
7
, Lemma 3.35]
.
∎

Lemma 4.27
.

If a tangent display system
\mathscr{D}
contains only étale maps of a tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, then
\mathscr{D}
is strong.

Proof.

Using a similar argument as the one used in Lemma
4.26
one can prove that the naturality diagrams of the projections
\pi_{k}\colon\mathrm{T}_{n}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}
of an étale map are tangent pullback diagrams. From this, it is not hard to realize that
\mathrm{T}_{n}
preserves the tangent display maps of
\mathscr{D}
since
\mathscr{D}
is stable under the tangent bundle functor and tangent pullbacks.
∎

Proposition 4.28
.

Étale tangent categories are equivalent to tangentads in the
2
-category
\mathscr{D}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}^{\ast}
.

Proof.

Proposition
4.23
proved that a display tangent category is equivalent to a tangentad of
\mathscr{D}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}
. Since
\mathscr{D}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}^{\ast}
is a
2
-subcategory of
\mathscr{D}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}
, a tangentad of
\mathscr{D}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}^{\ast}
is also a display tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};\mathscr{D})
. Moreover, since the
2
-morphisms of
\mathscr{D}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}^{\ast}
are display natural transformations, each structural
2
-morphism of the tangent structure
\mathbb{T}
is display. In particular, the projection is display, meaning that the naturality diagram of the projection with each tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
is a tangent pullback, which implies that
q
is étale. Finally, Lemma
4.26
shows also that if
q\colon E\to M
is a display étale map of a display tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};\mathscr{D})
, all the naturality diagrams of each structural natural transformation of
\mathbb{T}
with
q
is a tangent pullback, meaning that the structural natural transformations of
\mathbb{T}
are display natural transformations, making
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};\mathscr{D})
into a tangentad of
\mathscr{D}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}^{\ast}
.
∎

One can employ the formal approach to prove that each display tangent category defines a tangent fibration. Let us start by recalling that each display category
(\mathbb{X},\mathscr{D})
is associated with a fibration
\Pi\colon\mathscr{D}\to\mathbb{X}
which sends each display map to its codomain, where
\mathscr{D}
denotes the category whose objects are display maps of
(\mathbb{X},\mathscr{D})
and whose morphisms are commutative squares in
\mathbb{X}
. In particular, the Cartesian lift of a morphism
f\colon N\to M
of
\mathbb{X}
on a display map
q\colon E\to M
is the second projection
\varphi_{f}\colon f^{\ast}E\to E
of the pullback diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{\ast}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Furthermore, each display functor
F\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathscr{D})\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathscr{D}^{\prime})
defines a morphism of fibrations:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{D}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{X}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

In particular, such a morphism preserves the Cartesian lifts since
F
sends display pullbacks to display pullbacks. Finally, a natural transformation
\varphi\colon F\Rightarrow G\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathscr{D})\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathscr{D}^{\prime})
defines a natural transformation between morphisms of fibrations:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{D}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{X}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

Therefore, we obtain a
2
-functor:

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}\colon\mathscr{D}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}\to\mathsf{FIB}

Lemma 4.29
.

The
2
-functor
\mathscr{F}
is pointwise pullback-preserving (see Definition
3.4
).

Proof.

Consider four display endofunctors
F,G,H,K\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathscr{D})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathscr{D})
and four natural transformations
\varphi\colon F\to G
,
\psi\colon H\to G
,
\pi_{1}\colon K\to F
, and
\pi_{2}\colon K\to H
such that:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

is a pointwise pullback diagram in the category
\mathsf{END}(\mathbb{X},\mathscr{D})
. Thus, the display functor
K
sends an object
M
of
\mathbb{X}
to the object
KM
of
\mathbb{X}
such that:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces KM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GM}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

is a pullback diagram of
\mathbb{X}
. The
2
-functor
\mathscr{F}
sends a display functor
F
to a
1
-morphism of fibrations which coincide with
F
on the base and which is the restriction of
F
to the display maps on the total categories. Thus,
\mathscr{F}
sends
K
to a
1
-morphism of fibrations which is
K
on the base and the restriction of
K
on display maps on the total categories. Furthermore, it sends a natural transformation
\theta
to a
2
-morphism of fibrations which coincides with
\theta
on the base and that is defined by the naturality square diagrams of
\theta
on the total categories. Therefore, for each display map
q\colon E\to M
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces KE}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HE}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces KM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FE}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GE}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Kq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Hq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Gq}

is a pullback diagram in
\mathscr{D}
. Thus,
\mathscr{F}
preserves pointwise pullbacks.
∎

Thanks to Lemma
4.29
, we can now revisit Cockett and Cruttwell’s claim according to which each display tangent category defines a tangent fibration as a direct application of the formal approach.

Theorem 4.30
.

Each display tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};\mathscr{D})
defines a tangent fibration
\Pi\colon(\mathscr{D},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.

Proof.

Thanks to Lemma
4.29
, the
2
-functor
\mathscr{F}
preserves pointwise pullbacks, therefore, we can apply
\mathsf{TNG}
on
\mathscr{F}
and obtain a
2
-functor
\mathsf{TNG}(\mathscr{D}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT})\to\mathsf{TNG}(\mathsf{FIB})
.

By Proposition
4.11
and Proposition
4.23
the tangentads of
\mathscr{D}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}
are strong tangent display categories and the ones of
\mathsf{FIB}
are tangent fibrations. Furthermore, each display tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};\mathscr{D})
can be made into a strong display tangent category by Lemma
4.24
. Thus, each display tangent category can be sent to a strong display tangent category which is sent by
\mathsf{TNG}(\mathscr{F})
to a tangent fibration.
∎

4.6
Tangent restriction categories and their
\mathscr{M}
-tangent categories

A
restriction category
is a categorical context for partial maps. Concretely, this is a category equipped with an assignment for each morphism
f\colon M\to N
of an idempotent
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muf\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\colon M\to M
, called the
restriction idempotent
of
f
, satisfying the following axioms:

(a)

For all
f\colon M\to N
:

\displaystyle f\circ\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muf\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu=f

(b)

Two idempotents
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muf\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
and
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mug\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
commutes provided
f
and
g
share the same domain;

(c)

For all
f
and
g
:

\displaystyle\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mug\circ\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muf\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mug\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\circ\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muf\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu

provided
f
and
g
share the same domain;

(d)

For all
f
and
g
:

\displaystyle\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mug\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\circ f=f\circ\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mug\circ f\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu

provided the codomain of
f
coincides with the domain of
g
.

We invite the reader consult
[
4
]
.

The archetypal example of a restriction category is the category of sets and partial maps, for which the idempotent
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muf\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\colon M\to M
of a morphism
f\colon M\to N
is the partial function which is the identity on the domain of
f
and not defined elsewhere. Total maps of a restriction category are those morphisms whose restriction idempotent is the identity.

A tangent restriction category is a restriction category equipped with a tangent structure compatible with the restriction structure. First, recall the notion of a restriction pullback. We suggest the interested reader consult
[
5
]
.

Definition 4.31
.

In a restriction category, a
restriction pullback
between two maps
f\colon B\to A
and
g\colon C\to A
consists of an object
B\times_{A}^{R}C
together with two total maps
\pi_{1}\colon B\times_{A}^{R}C\to B
and
\pi_{2}\colon B\times_{A}^{R}C\to C
such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\times_{A}^{R}C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

commutes on the nose, and for every pair of maps
b\colon X\to B
and
c\colon X\to C
such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mub\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muc\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

there exists a unique morphism
\langle b,c\rangle^{R}\colon X\to B\times_{A}^{R}C
such that:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\times_{A}^{R}C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muc\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mub\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle b,c\rangle^{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}

A restriction functor
F
is a functor which preserves the restriction idempotents:

\displaystyle F\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muf\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muFf\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu

There are at least two candidates to define
2
-morphisms of restriction categories. A
total natural transformation

\varphi\colon F\Rightarrow G
between two restriction functors consists of a natural transformation
\varphi_{M}\colon FM\to GM
, natural in
M
, such that each morphism
\varphi_{M}
is total, namely,
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\varphi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{M}=\mathsf{id}_{FM}
.

A
restriction natural transformation

\varphi\colon F\Rightarrow G
is a collection of total maps
\varphi_{M}\colon FM\to GM
, indexed by the object of the domain restriction category, such that the following diagram commutes for each morphism
f\colon M\to N
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FN}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GN}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muFf\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ff}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Gf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

We denote by
\mathsf{RSTR}
and by
\mathsf{RSTR}_{\mathsf{lax}}
the
2
-categories of restriction categories, restriction functors, and total natural and restriction natural transformations, respectively.

In
[
2
]
, Cockett and Cruttwell introduced the concept of a tangent restriction category.

Definition 4.32
.

A
tangent restriction category
consists of a restriction category
\mathbb{X}
equipped with a
tangent restriction structure
, which consists of a restriction functor
\mathrm{T}\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}
together with a collection of total natural transformations
p\colon\mathrm{T}\Rightarrow\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}
,
z\colon\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}
,
s\colon\mathrm{T}_{2}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}
,
l\colon\mathrm{T}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{2}
,
c\colon\mathrm{T}^{2}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{2}
satisfying the same axioms of Definition
2.5
, where, however, the fundamental tangent limits are replaced with restriction pullbacks whose universality is preserved by all iterates of
\mathrm{T}
.

One could hope to compare tangent restriction categories with tangentads of either one of the
2
-categories
\mathsf{RSTR}
or
\mathsf{RSTR}_{\mathsf{lax}}
. However, in both scenarios, the fundamental tangent limits in the definition of tangentads do not match with the restriction pullbacks of Definition
4.32
. In particular, one finds that the fundamental tangent limits are total pullbacks, namely, pullback diagrams in the subcategory of total maps.

Despite this incompatibility, in
[
5
]
, Cockett and Lack showed that in a split restriction category, restriction limits coincide with ordinary limits on total maps. By specializing their result for restriction pullbacks, we conclude that in a split restriction category, restriction pullbacks coincide with total pullbacks, namely, pullback diagrams in the subcategory of total maps.

This observation leads to a characterization of tangent split restriction categories, namely, tangent restriction categories whose restriction idempotents split, as tangentads. First, let us introduce the appropriate
2
-category.
\mathsf{SRSTR}
denotes the
2
-category of split restriction categories, restriction functors, and total natural transformations. Notice that since the splitting of an idempotent is always unique up to isomorphism, each restriction functor preserves the splittings.

Lemma 4.33
.

Let
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
be a tangent restriction category and consider the
n
-fold restriction pullback
\mathrm{T}_{n}\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}
of the projection along itself.

(a)

The projections
\pi_{k}\colon\mathrm{T}_{n}A\to\mathrm{T}A
are total natural transformations;

(b)

The functors
\mathrm{T}_{n}\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}
are restriction functors.

Proof.

Let us consider a morphism
f\colon A\to B
. Let us compute the following:

\displaystyle\quad\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}f\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\circ\pi_{i}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\quad\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muf\circ p\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\circ\pi_{i}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\quad\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muf\circ p\circ\pi_{i}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\quad\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muf\circ p\circ\pi_{j}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\quad\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}f\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\circ\pi_{j}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu

for any
i,j=1,\dots,n
, where we used that
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}f\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mup\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\circ\mathrm{T}f\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mup\circ\mathrm{T}f\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muf\circ p\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
, since
p
is a total natural transformation. So, in particular:

\displaystyle\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}f\circ\pi_{i}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}f\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\circ\pi_{i}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}f\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\circ\pi_{j}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}f\circ\pi_{j}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu

Let us prove that
\pi_{i}\colon\mathrm{T}_{n}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}
is natural. First, recall that, by definition of an
n
-fold restriction pullback

\displaystyle\pi_{i}\circ\mathrm{T}_{n}f=\mathrm{T}f\circ\pi_{i}\circ e_{i}

where
e_{i}
denotes the composition of all idempotents
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}f\circ\pi_{j}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
for every
j\neq i
. However, since
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}f\circ\pi_{j}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}f\circ\pi_{i}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
, we obtain that:

\displaystyle\quad\pi_{i}\circ\mathrm{T}_{n}f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\quad\mathrm{T}f\circ\pi_{i}\circ\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}f\circ\pi_{i}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\quad\mathrm{T}f\circ\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}f\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\circ\pi_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\quad\mathrm{T}f\circ\pi_{i}

where we used that
f\circ\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mug\circ f\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mug\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\circ f
provided that
f
and
g
are composable. This shows that
\pi_{i}
is natural. Let us now prove that
\mathrm{T}_{n}
is a restriction functor. First, notice that, by the universal property of the restriction pullback,
\mathrm{T}_{n}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muf\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\colon\mathrm{T}_{n}A\to\mathrm{T}_{n}A
is the unique morphism satisfying

\displaystyle\pi_{i}\circ\mathrm{T}_{n}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muf\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}f\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\circ\pi_{i}\circ e_{i}=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}f\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\circ\pi_{i}

for every
i=1,\dots,n
. Let us show that also
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}_{n}f\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
satisfies the same equations. First, notice that, since
\pi_{i}
is a total natural transformation:

\displaystyle\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}_{n}f\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\pi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{i}\circ\mathrm{T}_{n}f\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\pi_{i}\circ\mathrm{T}_{n}f\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}f\circ\pi_{i}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu

Therefore, we can compute:

\displaystyle\pi_{i}\circ\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}_{n}f\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu=\pi_{i}\circ\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}f\circ\pi_{i}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}f\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\circ\pi_{i}

This proves that
\mathrm{T}_{n}
preserves restriction idempotents.
∎

Proposition 4.34
.

Split tangent restriction categories are
2
-equivalent to tangentads in the
2
-category
\mathsf{SRSTR}
of split restriction categories, restriction functors, and total natural transformations.

Proof.

A tangentad in
\mathsf{SRSTR}
consists of a split restriction category
\mathbb{X}
together with a restriction endofunctor
\mathrm{T}\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}
and the structural total natural transformations
p
,
z
,
s
,
l
, and
c
, satisfying the axioms of a tangent structure. In particular, the fundamental tangent limits, in this context, are total pullbacks. Since
\mathbb{X}
is a split restriction category, these total pullbacks become restriction pullbacks. Thus, a tangentad in
\mathsf{SRSTR}
is a tangent split restriction category.

Conversely, consider a tangent split restriction category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. This consists of a split restriction category
\mathbb{X}
, a restriction endofunctor
\mathrm{T}
and a collection of structural total natural transformations
p
,
z
,
s
,
l
, and
c
. Thanks to Lemma
4.33
, the projections
\pi_{i}\colon\mathrm{T}_{n}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}
of the
n
-fold restriction pullbacks of the projection along itself are total natural transformations and each
\mathrm{T}_{n}
is a restriction functor. Thus, since restriction pullbacks in a split restriction category are equivalent to total pullbacks,
\mathrm{T}_{n}
is the pointwise
n
-fold pullback of
p\colon\mathrm{T}\Rightarrow\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}
in the category
\mathsf{END}(\mathbb{X})
. This proves that a tangent split restriction category is a tangentad of
\mathsf{SRSTR}
.
∎

Despite the discrepancy between tangent restriction categories and tangentads, one can formally split the restriction idempotents of a tangent restriction category.

Lemma 4.35
.

For each tangent restriction category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
the split restriction category
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathbb{X}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
obtained by formally splitting the restriction idempotents of
\mathbb{X}
(cf.
[
4
, Proposition 2.26]
)comes equipped with a tangent restriction structure
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathbb{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
. Moreover, the inclusion restriction functor
\mathbb{X}\hookrightarrow\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathbb{X}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
lifts to a strict tangent restriction morphism
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\hookrightarrow(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathbb{X}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu,\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathbb{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)
.

Proof.

The objects of
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathbb{X}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
are pairs
(M,e)
formed by an object
M
of
\mathbb{X}
together with a restriction idemponent
e\colon M\to M
on
M
. Morphisms
(M,e)\to(M^{\prime},e^{\prime})
of
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathbb{X}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
are morphisms
f\colon M\to M^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{X}
which intertwin with the restriction idempotents, namely,
e\circ f=f\circ e^{\prime}
. The tangent bundle functor
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
on
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathbb{X}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
sends
(M,e)
to
(\mathrm{T}M,\mathrm{T}e)
and each
f
to
\mathrm{T}f
. The structural natural transformations are defined as in
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, since by naturality they commute with the corresponding restriction idempotents. The
n
-fold restriction pullback
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{n}
sends
(M,e)
to
(\mathrm{T}_{n}M,\mathrm{T}_{n}e)
and each
f
to
\mathrm{T}_{n}f
. Since the inclusion
\mathbb{X}\hookrightarrow\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathbb{X}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
preserves total pullbacks, namely, pullbacks on total maps, and since restriction pullbacks in a tangent split restriction category are equivalent to total pullbacks, it is not hard to prove that
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{n}
is indeed a restriction
n
-fold tangent pullback. Using a similar argument, one can prove the universal property of the vertical lift. Finally, the formal splitting preserves equational axioms. Therefore,
(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathbb{X}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu,\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathbb{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)
is a tangent restriction category and the inclusion
\mathbb{X}\hookrightarrow\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathbb{X}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
extends to a strict tangent morphism
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\hookrightarrow(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathbb{X}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu,\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathbb{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)
.
∎

In
[
5
, Theorem 3.4]
, Cockett and Lack proved that split restriction categories are
2
-equivalent to
\mathscr{M}
-categories. Proposition
4.34
suggests lifting such a
2
-equivalence to tangent split restriction categories, by applying the
2
-functor
\mathsf{TNG}
to Cockett and Lack’s
2
-equivalence. First, recall their construction.

Definition 4.36
.

A
display system of monics
of a category
\mathbb{X}
consists of a collection
\mathscr{M}
of morphisms of
\mathbb{X}
satisfying the following conditions:

•

Each morphism
f\in\mathscr{M}
is a monomorphism of
\mathbb{X}
;

•

\mathscr{M}
forms a display system of
\mathbb{X}
;

•

\mathscr{M}
is closed under composition;

•

\mathscr{M}
contains all isomorphisms of
\mathbb{X}
.

An
\mathscr{M}
-category
consists of a category
\mathbb{X}
equipped with a display system of monics.

Remark 4.37
.

In
[
4
]
, a display system of monics is called a
stable
system of monics.

Definition 4.38
.

An
\mathscr{M}
-functor
from an
\mathscr{M}
-category
(\mathbb{X},\mathscr{M})
to an
\mathscr{M}
-category
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathscr{M}^{\prime})
consists of a functor
F\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
which sends each monic of
\mathscr{M}
to a monic of
\mathscr{M}^{\prime}
and which preserves each pullback diagram of type

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

where
m
is a monic of
\mathscr{M}
.

Definition 4.39
.

An
\mathscr{M}
-natural transformation
from an
\mathscr{M}
-functor
F\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathscr{M})\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathscr{M}^{\prime})
to an
\mathscr{M}
-functor
G\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathscr{M})\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathscr{M}^{\prime})
consists of a natural transformation
\varphi\colon F\Rightarrow G
for which, for every monic
m
of
\mathscr{M}
, the naturality diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FB}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GB}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fm}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Gm}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

(4.1)

is a pullback diagram.

Remark 4.40
.

Notice that, the pullback diagram of Equation (
4.1
) is preserved by every
\mathscr{M}
-functor from
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathscr{M}^{\prime})
, since
Gm
is a monic in
\mathscr{M}^{\prime}
.

\mathscr{M}
-categories,
\mathscr{M}
-functors, and
\mathscr{M}
-natural transformations form a
2
-category denoted by
\mathscr{M}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}
.

Cockett and Lack proved that
\mathscr{M}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}
and
\mathsf{SRSTR}
are
2
-equivalent. In particular, each
\mathscr{M}
-category
(\mathbb{X},\mathscr{M})
is equivalent to a split restriction category
\mathsf{Par}(\mathbb{X},\mathscr{M})
whose objects are the same as the one of
\mathbb{X}
and morphisms
f\colon A\nrightarrow B
are (classes of isomorphisms of) spans:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[f]}

whose left leg
m_{f}
is a monic of
\mathscr{M}
.

Our goal is to extend this result to tangent split restriction categories. The first step is introducing the correct notion of a display system of monics in this context.

Definition 4.41
.

A
tangent display system of monics
of a tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
consists of a collection
\mathscr{M}
of monomorphisms of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
satisfying the following conditions:

•

Each monomorphism
m
of
\mathscr{M}
is an étale map of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
;

•

\mathscr{M}
forms a tangent display system of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
;

•

\mathscr{M}
is closed under composition;

•

\mathscr{M}
contains all isomorphisms of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.

An
tangent
\mathscr{M}
-category
consists of a tangent category equipped with a tangent display system of monics.

Remark 4.42
.

Thanks to Lemma
4.27
, a tangent display system of monics is automatically a strong tangent display system.

Proposition 4.43
.

\mathscr{M}
-tangent categories are equivalent to tangentads in the
2
-category
\mathscr{M}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}
of
\mathscr{M}
-categories,
\mathscr{M}
-functors, and
\mathscr{M}
-natural transformations.

Proof.

A tangentad in the
2
-category
\mathscr{M}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}
of
\mathscr{M}
-categories consists of an
\mathscr{M}
-category
(\mathbb{X},\mathscr{M})
together with an
\mathscr{M}
-endofunctor
\mathrm{T}\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathscr{M})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathscr{M})
together with the structural
\mathscr{M}
-natural transformations
p
,
z
,
s
,
l
, and
c
. Since
\mathrm{T}
preserves the monics of
\mathscr{M}
and the display pullbacks,
\mathscr{M}
becomes a tangent display system. Furthermore, since
p
is
\mathscr{M}
-natural, each monic of
\mathscr{M}
becomes an étale map. Therefore, tangentads of
\mathscr{M}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}
are
\mathscr{M}
-tangent categories. Conversely, Lemma
4.26
establishes that a map is étale if and only if all naturality squares with the structural natural transformations of a tangent category are tangent pullbacks. Thus, the structural natural transformations of a tangent
\mathscr{M}
-category are
\mathscr{M}
-natural transformations. Thus, each tangent
\mathscr{M}
-category is a tangentad in
\mathscr{M}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}
.
∎

Let
\mathscr{M}\text{-}\mathsf{TNGCAT}
denote
\mathsf{TNG}(\mathscr{M}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT})
.

Theorem 4.44
.

The
2
-category
\mathscr{M}\text{-}\mathsf{TNGCAT}
of
\mathscr{M}
-tangent categories is
2
-equivalent to the
2
-category
\mathsf{TNGSRSTR}
of tangent split restriction categories. In particular, each tangent
\mathscr{M}
-category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};\mathscr{M})
is sent to the tangent split restriction category
\mathsf{Par}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};\mathscr{M})
whose underlying split restriction category is
\mathsf{Par}(\mathbb{X},\mathscr{M})
and whose tangent structure is induced by
\mathbb{T}
.

Proof.

To prove this result, one only needs to apply the
2
-functor
\mathsf{TNG}
to the
2
-equivalence of
[
4
, Theorem 3.4]
and use Propositions
4.34
and
4.43
.
∎

Remark 4.45
.

In
[
7
, Definition 3.42]
, the monics of a tangent display system of monics are called
open subobjects
. This is due to the classification of open subsets in the tangent category of finite-dimensional smooth manifolds as tangent monic (display) étale maps
[
7
, Lemma 3.41]
. We suggest the reader consult
[
7
]
for details.

Thanks to Remark
4.45
, we can interpret Theorem
4.44
by saying that the restriction domain of a partial map in a tangent restriction categories is always an open subobject to the domain. This also means that the tangent category of smooth partial functions between smooth manifolds defined over arbitrary domains does not form a tangent restriction category. To fix this, one might consider a more general concept.

Definition 4.46
.

A
quasi-tangent restriction category
consists of a restriction category
\mathbb{X}
whose split completion
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathbb{X}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
(cf. Lemma
4.35
) comes equipped with a tangent structure
\mathbb{T}
in the
2
-category
\mathsf{SRSTR}_{\mathsf{lax}}
.

Concretely, a quasi-tangent restriction category consists of a restriction category, a restriction endofunctor
\mathrm{T}\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}
and restriction natural transformations
p
,
z
,
s
,
l
, and
c
, satisfying the usual axioms of a tangentad and for which the fundamental tangent limits are restriction pullbacks. In future work, we will study this generalization.

4.7
Infinitesimal objects are tangentads of spans

Infinitesimal objects were introduced by Cockett and Cruttwell in
[
2
, Definition 5.6]
to classify representable tangent categories, namely, Cartesian tangent categories whose tangent bundle functor is representable. In particular,
[
2
, Proposition 5.7]
shows that the "exponentialization" of an infinitesimal object produces a representable tangent structure and vice versa.

In this section, we first show that infinitesimal objects of a Cartesian category
\mathbb{X}
are tangentads in the
2
-co-category of (classes of isomorphisms of) spans of
\mathbb{X}
. Secondly, we interpret the relationship between infinitesimal objects and representable tangent categories as an equivalence between tangent structures.

In Definition
3.31
, we introduced infinitesimal elements in the context of tangentads. In the context of tangent categories, infinitesimal elements are called infinitesimal objects
[
2
, Definition 5.6]
. Moreover, we call a microscopic element of a tangent category, a
microscopic object
.

Remark 4.47
.

In the original definition of an infinitesimal object
[
2
, Definition 5.6]
, the
n
-fold pushout of
\zeta
along itself was not required to be preserved by the functors
D^{n}\times(-)
since this condition is already implied by requiring
D^{n}
and
D_{n}
to be exponentiable objects. Notice that this condition is necessary to express the compatibility between the multiplication
\mu
and the cosum
\delta
.

Moreover, in the original definition, each object
D^{n}\>\colon\!=D\times\dots\times D
was required to be exponentiable. However, when
D
is exponentiable, namely,
D\times-
admits a left adjoint, each
((\dots(-)^{D}\dots)^{D})^{D}
defines a left adjoint for
D^{n}\times-
, making
D^{n}
exponentiable.

Cockett and Cruttwell showed that if
D
is an infinitesimal object of a Cartesian category
\mathbb{X}
, then
\mathrm{T}\>\colon\!=(-)^{D}\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}
defines a tangent bundle functor,
(-)^{\zeta}\colon\mathrm{T}\Rightarrow\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}
defines the projection,
(-)^{!}\colon\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}
defines the zero morphism,
(-)^{\delta}\colon\mathrm{T}_{2}=(-)^{D\star D}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}
defines the sum,
(-)^{\mu}\colon\mathrm{T}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{2}=(-)^{D\times D}
defines the vertical lift, and finally,
(-)^{\tau}\colon\mathrm{T}^{2}\to\mathrm{T}^{2}
defines the canonical flip, where
\tau\colon D\times D\to D\times D
is the canonical symmetry
\langle\pi_{2},\pi_{1}\rangle
.

This suggests interpreting an infinitesimal object as a tangentad in a suitable
2
-category. The first suggestion to pick the correct ambient
2
-category comes from noticing that the tangent bundle
1
-morphism should be the object
D
. A
2
-category in which morphisms are objects is the category of spans. Since we do not want to require the existence of all pullbacks, we decide to consider only spans whose legs are display morphisms, namely, morphisms which admit all pullbacks. We call
display spans
such spans.

Given a category
\mathbb{X}
, let denote by
\mathsf{DSPAN}(\mathbb{X})
the (strict)
2
-category whose objects are objects of
\mathbb{X}
,
1
-morphisms are isomorphism classes of display spans, and
2
-morphisms are isomorphism classes of morphisms of spans. The second hint comes from realizing that the exponential operation
M^{(-)}
is contravariant. This suggests to inverts the direction of the
2
-morphisms of
\mathsf{DSPAN}(\mathbb{X})
, namely, to consider the
2
-category
\mathsf{DSPAN}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbb{X})
.

Proposition 4.48
.

Microscopic objects of a Cartesian category
\mathbb{X}
are equivalent to tangentads in the
2
-category
\mathsf{DSPAN}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbb{X})
over the terminal object
\mathds{1}
of
\mathbb{X}
.

Proof.

Let us consider a display span over the terminal object:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{r}}

Since
\mathds{1}
is terminal,
q_{l},q_{r}\colon D\to\mathds{1}
must coincide with the unique morphism
!\colon D\to\mathds{1}
. The projection corresponds to a morphism of
\mathbb{X}
of type
\zeta\colon\mathds{1}\to D
, the zero morphism is just
!\colon D\to\mathds{1}
, the
n
-fold pullbacks of the projection along itself correspond to the
n
-fold pushouts
D\star\dots\star D
of
\zeta
along itself, the sum morphism corresponds to
\delta\colon D\to D\star D
, the vertical lift corresponds to an associative and commutative multiplication
\mu\colon D\times D\to D
, since
D\times D
represents the composition of the span
D
with itself. Finally, the canonical flip coincides with an isomorphism
c\colon D\times D\to D\times D
. However, since
(D\times!)\circ c=\mathrm{T}p\circ c\mathrm{T}=p_{\mathrm{T}}=!\times D
, we have that
\pi_{1}\circ c=\pi_{2}
. However,
\tau
is the unique isomorphism such that
\pi_{1}\circ\tau=\pi_{2}
, thus
c=\tau
. One can easily see that the equational axioms of a microscopic object correspond exactly to the equational axioms of a tangent structure on
\mathds{1}
in
\mathsf{DSPAN}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbb{X})
of type
(D,\zeta,!,\delta,\mu,\tau)
. Finally, since
\mathsf{END}_{\mathsf{DSPAN}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbb{X})}(\mathds{1})\cong\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{op}}
as a category, the universality of the vertical lift and the existence of the
n
-fold tangent pullbacks correspond precisely to the existence of the
n
-fold pushouts of
\zeta\mathds{1}\to D
(preserved by
D^{n}\times(-)
) and the coequalizer axiom for the multiplication
\mu
. This shows the equivalence between tangentads of
\mathsf{DSPAN}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbb{X})
and microscopic objects of
\mathbb{X}
.
∎

To obtain infinitesimal objects as tangentads, one must consider exponentiable objects of
\mathbb{X}
.

Proposition 4.49
.

Infinitesimal objects of a Cartesian category
\mathbb{X}
are equivalent to tangentads in the
2
-category
\mathsf{DSPAN}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbb{X})
over the terminal object
\mathds{1}
of
\mathbb{X}
for which each
n
-fold pullback
D_{n}
of the projection along itself is exponentiable in
\mathbb{X}
.

Remark 4.50
.

One could wonder if the tangentads on the
2
-category of display spans over the full subcategory
\mathsf{EXP}(\mathbb{X})
of exponentiable objects of
\mathbb{X}
could represent infinitesimal objects of
\mathbb{X}
. However, this is not the case, since
\mathsf{END}_{\mathsf{DSPAN}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathsf{EXP}(\mathbb{X}))}(\mathds{1})
is not equivalent to the whole category
\mathbb{X}
, but only to
\mathsf{EXP}(\mathbb{X})
. This implies that the universality of the vertical lift and the existence of the
n
-fold tangent pullbacks of the projection along itself only apply among those objects of
\mathbb{X}
which are exponentiable, while for an infinitesimal object, the universality is required to hold on the entire category
\mathbb{X}
.

One is left to wonder what the rest of the tangentads of
\mathsf{DSPAN}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbb{X})
might represent. Let us introduce the following definition.

Definition 4.51
.

A
relative microscopic object
on a category
\mathbb{X}
on an object
M
of
\mathbb{X}
consists of a microscopic object in the display slice category
\mathbb{X}/M
of
\mathbb{X}
on
M
. Concretely, this corresponds to an object
D
of
\mathbb{X}
equipped with a morphism
q\colon D\to M
, a coprojection
\zeta\colon M\to D
, for which the
n
-fold pushout
D_{n}
along
\zeta
to itself exists and is preserved by all functors
D^{n}\times_{M}(-)
, where
D^{n}=D\times_{M}\dots\times_{M}D
; a cosum
\delta\colon D\to D_{2}
making
\zeta
a cocommutative comonoid on the coslice category
\mathsf{id}_{M}/(\mathbb{X}/M)
; finally, a multiplication
\mu\colon D\times_{M}D\to D
, associative and commutative, which satisfies the zero rule, is compatible with the cosum, and making the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D\times D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D\times(D\star_{M}D)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(D\times D)\star_{D}(D\times D)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D\star D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\times\zeta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta\iota_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}_{D}\times\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu\star_{M}\pi_{1}}

a pushout diagram in
\mathbb{X}/M
.

A
relative infinitesimal object
on
M
is a relative microscopic object
D
on
M
for which each
D_{n}\to M
is exponentiable in
\mathbb{X}/M
.

It is immediate to see that relative microscopic/infinitesimal objects on the terminal object
\mathds{1}
of a Cartesian category
\mathbb{X}
are precisely microscopic/infinitesimal objects of
\mathbb{X}
.

Proposition 4.52
.

Relative microscopic objects of a category
\mathbb{X}
are equivalent to tangentads of the
2
-category
\mathsf{DSPAN}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbb{X})
.

Proof.

The proof is essentially the same as in Proposition
4.49
.
∎

We can now reinterpret the relationship between infinitesimal objects and representable Cartesian tangent categories as an equivalence between tangent structures. Indeed, an infinitesimal object
D
of a Cartesian category
\mathbb{X}
is a tangentad of
\mathsf{DSPAN}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbb{X})
on the terminal object
\mathds{1}
of
\mathbb{X}
for which each
D_{n}
is an exponentiable object of
\mathbb{X}
.

Exponentiable objects of
\mathbb{X}
are precisely those objects of
\mathsf{END}_{\mathsf{DSPAN}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbb{X})}(\mathds{1})\cong\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{op}}
which are coexponentiable.
Recall also that a tangentad on
\mathsf{DSPAN}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbb{X})
on
\mathds{1}
corresponds to a Leung functor
\mathfrak{L}[D]\colon\mathsf{WEIL}_{1}\to\mathsf{END}_{\mathsf{DSPAN}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbb{X})}(\mathds{1})
. By only considering those tangentads for which
D_{n}
are exponentiable in
\mathbb{X}
, one can extend the Leung functor
\mathfrak{L}[D]
to a new Leung functor
\mathfrak{L}[(-)^{D}]\colon\mathsf{WEIL}_{1}\to\mathsf{END}_{\mathsf{CAT}}(\mathbb{X})
, whose corresponding tangent structure is representable.

Conversely, if
\mathfrak{L}[\mathbb{T}]\colon\mathsf{WEIL}_{1}\to\mathsf{END}_{\mathsf{CAT}}(\mathbb{X})
represents a representable Cartesian tangent category, one can "de-exponentialize" the tangent bundle functor and define a Leung functor
\mathfrak{L}[D]\colon\mathsf{WEIL}_{1}\to\mathsf{END}_{\mathsf{DSPAN}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbb{X})}(\mathds{1})
, namely, a tangentad of
\mathsf{DSPAN}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbb{X})
on
\mathds{1}
, for which
D_{n}
are exponentiable in
\mathbb{X}
.
